HTTP Content Negotiation as a Deployment Model for Navigable-Document Standards

Research context: Exploring whether HTTP content negotiation is the right deployment model for navigable-document standards, or whether in-document manifests (à la JSON-LD) are more practical given how the web actually operates.

1. How Accept-Header Negotiation Works in Practice

Proactive (server-driven) content negotiation is the dominant pattern: the client sends an Accept header listing preferred MIME types with quality weights, the server selects the closest match, and the Vary response header tells caches which request headers were used for the decision. This mechanism is specified in RFC 7231 and has been in HTTP since 1.1.

What works reliably. Accept: text/markdown is a live example that is already working. Cloudflare's Docs for Agents (2025) accepts Accept: text/markdown, responds with a Markdown body, and adds an x-markdown-tokens estimate header. Claude Code and Cursor already send this header on documentation fetches. This narrow, binary negotiation — "give me Markdown if you have it, otherwise HTML" — works precisely because there are only two variants and the CDN layer is configured explicitly.

What breaks:

  • CDN caching with Vary: Accept is unreliable by default. Cloudflare does not natively honor Vary: Accept for cache key separation. Without Enterprise-tier custom cache key configuration, all requests to the same URL share a single cache entry regardless of the Accept value sent. The workaround — adding a query parameter (?fmt=json) — is effectively URL proliferation disguised as content negotiation. Fastly documents the same problem: Vary: Accept with many permutations "may noticably decline cache performance" and recommends normalizing the header in VCL before it reaches the cache key.
  • Browser caches store only one variant. Most browsers treat Vary as a validator, not a multi-entry key. They store one representation and revalidate if the current request's headers differ from the stored entry. Chrome's service worker Cache API had a known bug where it used the same validator approach instead of storing multiple variants. Only Firefox correctly stored multiple variations in the Cache API.
  • HTTP/2 push ignores Vary entirely. Pushed resources are matched to requests with no header comparison, meaning a pushed application/json variant could be served to a browser requesting text/html.
  • 406 Not Acceptable is rarely the real failure mode. Most servers are lenient and return a default representation when the Accept header doesn't match cleanly. The more insidious failure is silent: the server returns HTML when the agent expected JSON, producing a parse error or a silently corrupted context window downstream. WAF rules that inspect and block unusual Accept header values are the more common cause of 406 errors in production.
  • Proxy interference. Intermediary proxies frequently strip or normalize non-standard headers. A custom Accept: application/vnd.agent-manifest+json header may never reach the origin.

Operational cost. Accept-header negotiation requires server-side routing logic that is invisible to URL-based tooling, documentation systems, and link-sharing mechanisms. Every shared URL, screenshot, and curl command loses the negotiation context.

2. The application/vnd.* MIME Type Pattern

The vendor tree (vnd.) was defined in 1996 and is managed under RFC 6838 via IANA Expert Review — a lighter process than standards-track registration but still requiring a designated expert review and formal submission.

De facto adoption without IANA registration is common but risky. application/vnd.github+json and application/hal+json both circulated widely before or without formal registration. The practical barrier to adoption is not IANA bureaucracy but ecosystem support: the type must appear in client Accept headers, server response Content-Type headers, CDN bypass rules, WAF allow-lists, and documentation tooling simultaneously.

application/vnd.agent-manifest+json or application/llm-navigable+html specifically:

  • Neither exists in the IANA registry.
  • No LLM framework currently sends or recognizes them.
  • Deploying them requires every consumer to explicitly add them to their Accept header — an adoption bootstrapping problem with no forcing function. Compare to text/markdown, which already has wide client-side deployment as of 2025 (Cloudflare explicitly notes Claude Code and Cursor send it).
  • A custom type also does nothing to describe the schema of the manifest. It announces the container, not the content.

3. Precedents: Human-vs-Machine Negotiation of the Same URL

HAL (application/hal+json). The canonical example of Accept-header-based human/machine representation serving. In practice, HAL deployments overwhelmingly use a single content type and document it rather than doing runtime negotiation — the dual-representation serving model requires server complexity most API teams don't maintain.

JSON:API (application/vnd.api+json). Uses a registered vendor type. Content negotiation is defined in the spec, but in practice most JSON:API consumers hardcode the media type into the client.

Linked Data / RDF / RDFa. The most instructive precedent because it attempted exactly this architecture — a single URL serving HTML+RDFa to browsers and RDF/XML or JSON-LD to machines. The canonical deployment pattern required either 303 redirects (two round-trips per request, server configuration complexity) or hash URIs (simpler but constrained to static, co-located resource sets). Both approaches failed at web scale.

The definitive data point: schema.org abandoned content negotiation entirely. Their homepage now returns only HTML, the JSON-LD context moved to a dedicated URL (/docs/jsonldcontext.jsonld), and discovery uses Link headers per the JSON-LD 1.1 spec. The migration was accelerated by a DoS attack targeting the content-negotiated endpoint, but the underlying reason was operational fragility.

The W3C Content Negotiation by Profile spec (dx-prof-conneg). An attempt to extend content negotiation to profile/schema negotiation using Accept-Profile headers. The spec is a Working Draft (October 2023) with no broad implementation. It validates the problem but confirms there is no adopted solution.

4. The Case For and Against a Custom Negotiated Type

The case for HTTP content negotiation:

  • Semantically clean: the URL is the resource, the representation is negotiated. Consistent with REST's resource model.
  • Enables one canonical URL for both human and agent consumption.
  • The Vary header + proper CDN configuration can work — Cloudflare's image negotiation pipeline is proof that the infrastructure exists.

The case against:

  • Adoption bootstrapping is the primary failure mode. A new MIME type without existing client support means every agent must be updated to send the right Accept header. Compare to llms.txt: even with 10% domain adoption, it works because humans paste the URL and agents follow <link> tags.
  • CDN opacity. Most CDN setups silently collapse multi-variant responses unless explicitly configured.
  • The URL-is-the-contract assumption doesn't hold operationally. Shared URLs, browser bookmarks, curl, wget, and monitoring tools all operate on raw URLs with their own default Accept headers. The "same URL, different representations" model creates invisible behavior divergence.
  • Operational surface doubles. Every endpoint must now maintain two content paths, cache two variants, and document negotiation behavior.
  • The niche is being filled differently. The emerging 2025 pattern is: serve one canonical representation (Markdown or clean HTML), advertise alternatives via <link rel="alternate"> tags or Link headers, and surface a machine-readable index via llms.txt. This achieves the same goals without per-request negotiation logic.

5. In-Document Manifests vs. Content Negotiation

The JSON-LD approach. JSON-LD's <script type="application/ld+json"> in HTML <head> is the most widely deployed in-document machine-readable manifest pattern on the web. Schema.org markup now reaches billions of pages. However, controlled experiments in 2025 show that ChatGPT, Claude, Perplexity, and Gemini do not reliably extract or act on JSON-LD structured data — only Microsoft Copilot/Bing demonstrates consistent uptake. A perfectly deployed in-document manifest is not automatically consumed by agents unless agents are specifically coded to look for it.

Failure modes of in-document manifests:

  • Agents that parse HTML by stripping tags lose the manifest if it is buried in <head> metadata.
  • JSON-LD contexts loaded from external URLs introduce latency and availability dependencies.
  • Token cost: the manifest is always parsed even when the agent does not need it.
  • The manifest and the content can desync. Hash-based integrity checking mitigates this but requires the publisher to maintain fresh hashes.

6. What Works Better Than Both

The Evil Martians analysis and Cloudflare's docs-for-agents implementation point to a converging pattern:

  1. Serve Markdown alongside HTML via clean .md URL routes or Accept: text/markdown negotiation (the only content negotiation variant with actual client deployment in 2025).
  2. Advertise via <link rel="alternate" type="text/markdown"> in HTML head.
  3. Surface a machine-readable index via llms.txt or a per-section manifest at a well-known URL.

7. Is the Framing Right? Is the Niche Already Filled?

The specific design being explored — a manifest carrying id, human-authored summary, token estimate, content hash, and dependency edges — is not filled by any existing standard.

The framing question is slightly wrong. The real choice is not "content negotiation vs. in-document" but between:

A. Single-URL multi-representation via HTTP — the Linked Data failure model. Right architecture on paper for 20 years; has not achieved reliable deployment at the infrastructure level.

B. Parallel URL with Link header or <link> tag advertisement — the schema.org/JSON-LD 1.1 model after they abandoned negotiation. The canonical URL returns HTML; the manifest URL is a sibling resource advertised via a standard discovery header. Operationally simple, CDN-friendly, composable with existing tooling.

C. In-document <script type="..."> block — the JSON-LD/structured-data model. Fragile against token-stripping pipelines; works well for semantic-web tooling but not for raw LLM consumption in 2025.

Recommendation. For a section-level navigable document manifest, the most practical architecture is model B: keep the canonical HTML document unchanged, publish the manifest as a sibling JSON resource at a predictable URL (e.g., document.manifest.json or ?manifest), and advertise it in the HTML <head> via <link rel="manifest" type="application/json"> and in HTTP headers via Link: <url>; rel="manifest". This approach:

  • Works with CDNs without configuration changes.
  • Is visible to agents that parse HTML headers and to headless clients that read HTTP headers.
  • Does not require any new MIME type to have ecosystem adoption before it can be deployed.
  • Is trivially cache-separable because the manifest URL is distinct from the document URL.
  • Allows the manifest to evolve independently of the HTML document.

The application/vnd.agent-manifest+json type can be registered and used as the manifest's Content-Type when served from its own URL without any of the content negotiation failure modes.

Sources

  • Understanding The Vary Header — Smashing Magazine
  • Serving tailored content with Cloudflare
  • Content Negotiation by Profile - W3C
  • HAL - Hypertext Application Language
  • Schema.org moving from HTTP Content Negotiation to JSON-LD 1.1 Link header - GitHub issue
  • Media Types - IANA
  • Docs for agents - Cloudflare
  • Making your site visible to LLMs - Evil Martians
  • Cool URIs for the Semantic Web - W3C
  • JSON-LD 1.1 - W3C
  • Accept header - Fastly Documentation

llms.txt: Current State, Criticism, and the Gap to Section-Level Addressability

Research context: Exploring the gap between what llms.txt provides (file-level manifest pointing to separate markdown files) and what would be needed for in-document section-level addressability.

1. What llms.txt Actually Is

llms.txt was proposed by Jeremy Howard of Answer.AI in September 2024. It is a markdown file placed at /llms.txt on a domain root. Its structure is:

  • An H1 (required): the site or project name
  • An optional blockquote summary
  • Optional prose
  • Optional H2-delimited sections, each containing a markdown list of name: description entries
  • One section may be titled "Optional," signaling lower-priority URLs

A companion format, llms-full.txt, concatenates the full content of all linked pages into a single markdown file. A third variant, llms-ctx.txt, is a compiled XML-wrapped expansion of the link list with content inlined, generated by the llms_txt2ctx CLI tool.

The spec deliberately leaves processing undefined: it imposes no obligation on how a consumer interprets the file and prescribes no implementation behavior. It is a publishing convention, not a protocol.

2. Adoption Picture in 2026

The honest picture: adoption has plateaued at roughly 10% of sampled domains, growth has stalled, and real-world crawler consumption is nearly nonexistent.

A cross-domain study of 300,000 domains found approximately 10.13% had an llms.txt file. Adoption rate was nearly identical across low-, mid-, and high-traffic tiers — roughly 9–10% each — suggesting the plateau is structural, not a maturation curve still climbing. The file is present largely in developer-facing and technical documentation contexts: Stripe, Vercel, Cloudflare, Anthropic, Supabase, Cursor, Pinecone, LangGraph, and the documentation platforms (Mintlify, Fern) that auto-generate it. Conservative sectors — finance, healthcare, legal — show publication rates under 10% among top-100 domains.

Provider adoption is the critical failure:

  • Anthropic: Claude Desktop reportedly respects the file in manual retrieval workflows; ClaudeBot crawling behavior is unconfirmed
  • Perplexity: Described as using it to "prioritize page selection," with observable behavior but no formal commitment
  • OpenAI: GPTBot occasionally fetches the file but rarely; no confirmed use in ChatGPT retrieval
  • Google: Gary Illyes confirmed at Search Central Live in July 2025 that Google does not support llms.txt and is not planning to. John Mueller explicitly compared it to the defunct keywords meta tag
  • Mistral: Partial support with caveats

Server-log analyses show that GPTBot, ClaudeBot, PerplexityBot, OAI-SearchBot, and Google-Extended overwhelmingly skip the file and crawl HTML directly. One study from OtterlyAI found that only 0.1% of AI bot requests — 84 out of 62,100 — accessed llms.txt files.

The segment where llms.txt demonstrably works is IDE-based coding assistants: Cursor, Continue, and Cline do actively fetch documentation llms.txt files for context assembly. This is the original intended use case and the one where measurable benefit exists.

3. The Meta Keywords Criticism: Warranted or Not

The meta keywords comparison made by Google's John Mueller is partially warranted, partially overextended.

Where the comparison holds: Mueller's structural argument is sound. The keywords meta tag was self-declared, unverifiable, and ignored because bots could already extract signal from content directly. The same logic applies: an AI crawler that has already fetched the HTML has no clear incentive to make an additional request for a file whose claims about that same HTML it would need to verify anyway. The SE Ranking statistical finding sharpens this: an XGBoost model trained on AI citation data improved when the llms.txt variable was removed, meaning the file added noise rather than predictive signal.

Where the comparison breaks down: llms.txt links to real, verifiable URLs. Unlike meta keywords, the linked pages can be fetched and their content confirmed. The curation value — an author-chosen prioritization of which pages matter — is a meaningful signal that could theoretically be exploited by a system designed to trust it. The problem is that no major system has been designed to trust it.

Abuse and gaming risks: Documented concerns include cloaking (showing AI bots different content via llms.txt than human visitors see), affiliate link stuffing, and manipulative anchor text. These are real but not unique — schema.org markup faces identical gaming pressures and has not been abandoned.

4. Proposed Extensions: What Exists

The extension landscape is sparse. The GitHub repository has 59 open issues and 20 pull requests, but the specification has not evolved significantly since launch.

  • llms-full.txt: Concatenates all linked content into one file. Mintlify co-developed this with Anthropic; agents fetch it over twice as frequently as the curated index version. A brute-force solution to context assembly, not a structural improvement.
  • llms-ctx.txt: CLI-generated XML-wrapped expansion for Claude-compatible context files. A tooling artifact, not a spec change.
  • Path-scoped files: /docs/llms.txt — structural hierarchy through URL pathing rather than within-file addressing.
  • Query parameters: ?lang=python, ?excludeSpec=true — filtering mechanisms used by some implementations (Fern), but not standardized.
  • AgentReady: Incorporates llms.txt as one signal within a broader agentic readiness spec covering identity, OAuth, capabilities, and commerce. Does not extend the internal structure of llms.txt itself.

No published proposal exists for: binary format, content hashes per section, token cost annotations, dependency edges, section-level manifest, or in-document addressability.

5. The Specific Gap: File-Level vs. Section-Level Addressability

What llms.txt provides at its finest granularity: A URL plus a human-authored sentence of description. The unit of addressability is the document. An agent can select which documents to fetch; it cannot select which sections within a document to fetch without fetching the whole document.

What an in-document section manifest would need to do that llms.txt does not:

  • Sub-document addressing: A URL fragment (e.g., page.html#section-id) lets an agent identify a section, but the spec has no mechanism to list fragments as first-class addressable units with their own metadata. You cannot write a valid llms.txt entry that points to a specific anchor and declares what that anchor costs to load.
  • Token cost annotation: The spec carries no per-entry or per-section cost estimate. A consumer cannot make token-budget-aware decisions at selection time without fetching and counting. Turborepo's llms-full.txt consuming 116,000 tokens is the documented failure mode — the file gives no advance warning of its size. A section manifest would need a token_estimate (or character_count) per entry.
  • Content hash: The spec has no content_hash field. An agent cannot cheaply determine whether a section has changed since last ingestion without refetching the full document.
  • Dependency edges: llms.txt has no way to express that understanding Section B requires first reading Section A. There is no requires or depends_on field. A coding assistant working through a complex API reference cannot learn the prerequisite graph from the manifest.
  • Machine-authored summary vs. human prose: The current : description field is free text, optimized for human readability. A section manifest for programmatic consumption would benefit from structured fields: a summary string distinct from a title string, an optional entity_type or content_type annotation.

6. Closest Existence Proofs

Storybook Manifests (/manifests/components.json, /manifests/docs.json): JSON objects with id, name, path, description, and content per section. This is exactly the shape of a section-level HTML manifest — addressable by id, with description, with path. But Storybook's format is tool-specific (React/Storybook), generated from source code rather than authored, and has no token cost, content hash, or dependency fields. It is the clearest model of what filling the niche would look like.

HtmlRAG (WWW 2025): HTML structural information — headings, table structure, section hierarchy — is better than plain text for RAG retrieval, and block-tree-based pruning over HTML outperforms flat text chunking. This suggests the right retrieval unit is already finer than the document and that the research community is converging on section-aware retrieval — but the mechanism is automatic pruning, not author-declared manifests.

7. Trajectory Assessment

llms.txt's trajectory: useful but narrowly, stalled at the SEO-adjacent adoption layer where it was oversold, and genuinely functional only in the developer documentation / coding assistant niche where it was originally designed.

The overselling happened quickly. The SEO community adopted llms.txt as an "AI visibility" play and pushed adoption into domains where the file has no measurable effect. This drove the plateau and the noise finding. The format is not dead in its design envelope: Cursor and similar IDE agents do fetch and use these files. The coding assistant use case is working. The web-scale AI citation use case never worked, because no major AI search system committed to using it.

The criticisms from Google are structurally fair as descriptions of how AI crawlers currently work, but they are not evidence that the format cannot work — they are evidence that Google has chosen not to make it work.

The framing of "file-level manifest pointing to separate markdown files" is accurate and also the binding constraint. The format was designed for that granularity intentionally and has not evolved beyond it. The section-level extension is the unbuilt layer above it, and no one appears to be building it inside the llms.txt project. The Storybook manifest pattern and the HtmlRAG research both suggest the layer is worth building, but the llms.txt project is not the vehicle that will deliver it.

Sources

  • /llms.txt — a proposal to provide information to help LLMs use websites – Answer.AI
  • The /llms.txt file – llms-txt
  • Google Says LLMs.Txt Comparable To Keywords Meta Tag
  • No, llms.txt is not the 'new meta keywords'
  • The llms.txt is dead. More precisely: a dud.
  • GitHub – AnswerDotAI/llms-txt
  • AgentReady // The open standard for agent readiness
  • API Docs for AI Agents: llms.txt Guide May 2026 | Fern
  • What is llms.txt? Breaking down the skepticism | Mintlify
  • Manifests | Storybook docs
  • HtmlRAG: HTML is Better Than Plain Text for Modeling Retrieved Knowledge in RAG Systems
  • llms.txt Explained (May 2026) | Codersera

Web Components and Shadow DOM as Primitives for Agent-Navigation Manifests

Research context: Exploring whether Web Components compose cleanly with an agent-navigation manifest model, or whether the JavaScript-runtime requirement makes them the wrong primitive for a static-document-first standard.

1. How Web Components Encapsulate Structure

Web Components combine three distinct browser features: Custom Elements (the <my-section> tag registry), Shadow DOM (an isolated DOM subtree attached to a host element), and HTML Templates. The encapsulation model has three layers:

Light DOM is the standard document tree — everything an author places as children of a custom element in the source HTML. Light DOM is fully visible to page-level JavaScript (document.querySelectorAll), CSS, and any parser that reads the raw HTML.

Shadow DOM is an isolated subtree attached to the shadow host (the custom element itself). It has its own ID namespace, its own style scope, and is invisible to page-level selectors. JavaScript running outside the shadow root cannot reach span elements inside it via document.querySelector('span') — they simply do not appear. The only access path is through host.shadowRoot (if mode is "open") or through the accessibility tree after browser rendering.

Slots are the boundary crossing mechanism. A <slot> element inside shadow DOM projects light DOM children into a rendering position without moving them in the DOM. The projected content remains in the light DOM tree; it is only rendered as if it were inside the shadow. The content is technically addressable from outside — it stays in the main document — but its visual relationship to shadow-internal structure is established only at render time.

CSS Shadow Parts (::part() and exportparts) are the only official mechanism for external reach into shadow internals, and their scope is styling only.

2. Shadow DOM Visibility to a Parser

The answer depends sharply on which parser is walking the document.

A static text/HTML parser (curl, BeautifulSoup, readability, any tool that reads raw bytes) sees the source HTML. With imperative Shadow DOM — the dominant historical pattern — the shadow tree does not exist in the source. It is created at runtime by a connectedCallback() or customElements.define() call. The parser sees only <my-section id="intro"></my-section> and nothing inside. The content is fully invisible.

With Declarative Shadow DOM (DSD), the shadow tree is in the source HTML, encoded as a <template shadowrootmode="open"> child. A static parser will find the template element and can read its children as raw markup. However, the browser HTML parser replaces the template with the live shadow root during parsing — the template element does not survive into the DOM. So a static parser sees a <template> tag with children, while a live DOM inspection after parsing sees a shadow root. The content is present in bytes but the semantics are different depending on the reading context.

A live DOM walking agent (Playwright, browser automation, ARIA snapshot extraction) sees the fully resolved tree after JavaScript has run. Playwright's ARIA snapshot flattens the shadow boundary: <slot> elements are resolved to their projected content, and shadow-internal elements appear as if they are normal children. This transparency is supplied by the browser runtime, not by the document itself.

The accessibility tree flattens shadow DOM by design: browsers include shadow-internal elements in the AX tree as if no boundary exists. But ARIA relationship attributes (aria-labelledby, aria-controls, aria-owns) that cross shadow boundaries are broken — the ID scoping prevents them from resolving. This is an unresolved standards problem as of 2025, with several competing proposals (cross-root ARIA reflection, Reference Target) in origin trials but not yet shipped broadly.

The practical implication for agent navigation: an agent that receives a serialized accessibility tree or a Playwright ARIA snapshot gets a flattened view where shadow boundaries have been erased. An agent that receives raw HTML — including any static document distribution scenario — gets either nothing (imperative shadow DOM) or raw template bytes (Declarative Shadow DOM) that require explicit parsing awareness to interpret.

3. Has Anyone Proposed Web Components as Addressable Units for Agents?

The WICG webcomponents proposal repository contains no proposal connecting custom elements to LLM-agent addressability or document navigation manifests. The concept does not appear in any W3C or WHATWG standardization track.

The closest active work is the Custom Elements Manifest (CEM) — a community standard JSON format (open-wc.org) that documents the API surface of a component library: tag names, properties, attributes, CSS custom properties, slots, events, and shadow parts. CEM is a sidecar file (custom-elements.json, referenced from package.json) that describes the class API of components, not the content instances in a document. It does not describe what a particular <my-section> element contains in a given HTML document, and it has no fields for content hashes, token estimates, or dependency edges between rendered instances.

Dave Rupert (2025) has noted that the CEM's killer feature is its machine-readable API surface, and the most recent development is MCP (Model Context Protocol) servers that consume a project's CEM to answer questions like "what components are available in this project?" — a component catalogue for agents, not a document navigation manifest.

The agent web-navigation research literature (AutoWebGLM, WEPO, LCoW, WebLists) is focused on the opposite problem: simplifying HTML that is already too complex, removing non-content nodes, collapsing nested structures. None of this work proposes Web Components as a unit of addressability; they are obstacles to deal with, not primitives to build on.

4. The Downsides

Opacity to static parsers. Decisive problem. Imperative Shadow DOM is a black box to anything that reads raw HTML. Even with DSD, the <template shadowrootmode> element is a non-standard representation that most parsing libraries do not handle correctly.

JavaScript runtime requirement for imperative shadow DOM. Most Web Component use in the wild requires JavaScript to define the custom element and attach its shadow root. Before upgrade, the element is a generic HTMLElement with no shadow — the agent navigating a pre-upgrade document sees no internal structure.

ID namespace scoping breaks cross-document relationships. Because IDs are scoped inside shadow roots, the same id can legally exist in multiple shadow roots on the same page. An agent that receives id="intro" cannot resolve it unambiguously without knowing its shadow root ancestry.

Shadow DOM is a component encapsulation feature, not a document section feature. The design intent is to create reusable, self-contained UI primitives. Document sections are intentionally open: they are meant to be quoted, linked, indexed, and cross-referenced. Shadow DOM's encapsulation works against all of these use cases.

Slot resolution is conceptually confusing at scale. Content is in the light DOM but rendered through the shadow DOM's slot machinery. An agent would need to resolve slot assignments to understand what a section visually contains — browser-runtime work, not document-structure work.

5. Declarative Shadow DOM as a Workaround

DSD solves the JavaScript-runtime problem for rendering: a browser can paint a DSD document without executing any JavaScript. Available across all major browsers since February 2024.

However, DSD does not solve the problem for agent-navigation manifests:

  • Parser heterogeneity. The shadowrootmode attribute is a browser HTML parser feature. Non-browser parsers treat <template> as a regular element with children.
  • No declarative manifest. DSD adds shadow roots to the document but does not add any machine-readable metadata about sections — no IDs scoped to content identity, no summaries, no dependency declarations, no token estimates.
  • One shadow root per host. DSD has a hard constraint: one shadow root per host element. A document section model with overlapping or hierarchical groupings cannot map cleanly onto this constraint.
  • Content remains inside the shadow boundary. Even with DSD, content inside <template shadowrootmode> is shadow-encapsulated after parsing. An agent receiving the post-parse DOM still needs to pierce the shadow boundary explicitly.

The DSD design document states explicitly: "The primary motivating use case for declarative Shadow DOM is SSR and No-JS. Custom element definitions need JavaScript to function; therefore, this is a different use case/proposal." The feature is about rendering fidelity, not document semantics.

6. The Adjacent Standard: W3C Publication Manifest

The W3C Publication Manifest (W3C Publishing Working Group) is the closest existing standard to what the manifest model describes. It defines a JSON-LD sidecar that describes a digital publication's reading order, resources, creators, and — notably — resource integrity via Subresource Integrity hashes (integrity property on LinkedResource objects). It does not include token estimates or dependency edges between sections, and it is scoped to bounded digital publications (ebooks, audiobooks), not general web documents. But its LinkedResource + integrity pattern is direct prior art for the content-hash field in an agent-navigation manifest.

7. Verdict

Web Components are the wrong primitive for an agent-navigation manifest model aimed at static-document-first distribution.

The central incompatibility is architectural: Shadow DOM is an encapsulation mechanism designed to make component internals opaque, while a navigation manifest requires section content to be transparent and addressable. These goals are in direct opposition.

The more viable path:

  1. Semantic HTML elements (<section>, <article>) with explicit id attributes as the addressability primitive — these survive every parser without any special handling.
  2. A sidecar JSON manifest (in the spirit of W3C Publication Manifest or CEM) that maps id values to metadata entries (summary, hash, token estimate, dependency edges).
  3. Optionally, **inline <meta> or data- attributes* on section elements for manifests that must be self-contained — these are visible to every parser and carry arbitrary key-value metadata without any boundary semantics.

The Custom Elements Manifest's MCP-server pattern is the most relevant emerging prior art for the agent-facing side: a machine-readable JSON file that gives an AI agent structured discovery of named units in a project. Adapting that pattern to document sections (rather than component class APIs) is a cleaner design target than trying to make Shadow DOM carry the semantics.

  • Declarative Shadow DOM - web.dev

  • The killer feature of Web Components - Dave Rupert

The Hypermedia Revival and the Case for HTML-Native Agent-Navigable Documents

Research context: Exploring whether the hypermedia revival tradition has already made the intellectual case for HTML-native agent-navigable documents, and what specific claim about "agents as hypermedia clients" remains to be written.

I. The Core Hypermedia Argument

The htmx movement's philosophical center is a claim about betrayal. Roy Fielding's 2000 dissertation defined REST not as a description of JSON-API development but as an architectural description of the World Wide Web itself. The defining constraint, HATEOAS — Hypermedia as the Engine of Application State — requires that responses be entirely self-describing: the server encodes all available transitions in the response itself, through links and forms, so that a client needs "no prior knowledge beyond the initial URI" to navigate the system.

Carson Gross's essay "How Did REST Come to Mean the Opposite of REST?" reconstructs the historical mechanism of the inversion: XML then JSON APIs were declared RESTful by syllogism (REST is not SOAP; JSON APIs are not SOAP; therefore JSON APIs are REST), despite the fact that JSON provides no hypermedia controls and therefore structurally cannot satisfy the uniform interface constraint.

The practical import: a JSON API client requires out-of-band documentation — Swagger, OpenAPI, GraphQL schemas — to know what transitions are available. An HTML response containing forms and links encodes all of this in-band, within the response itself. The browser needs no documentation to know that a <form method="post" action="/checkout"> submits an HTTP POST to /checkout with the fields listed in the form. The document is its own specification.

The "Hypermedia APIs vs. Data APIs" essay draws the corollary explicitly: hypermedia APIs are "trivially REST-ful" and accommodate significant change without versioning, because the server can add, remove, and modify available transitions by modifying the HTML it returns. The "Two Approaches to Decoupling" essay adds an important concession: hypermedia APIs "make for a terrible general purpose API" for machine clients, because machine consumers cannot easily parse HTML to extract data. The recommended pragmatic response is to maintain separate hypermedia (HTML) and data (JSON) APIs for their respective audiences. This concession was written before LLMs and may have expired.

The Hypermedia Systems book (Gross, Stepinski, Akşimşek, 2023; free at hypermedia.systems) defines a hypermedia client as software that can "interpret a particular hypermedia and the hypermedia controls within it" without domain-specific knowledge. The canonical client is the browser, which "doesn't know what a contact is" but can render a contacts page and expose its forms regardless.

II. The Ecosystem and Its Shared Design Commitments

Hotwire (Basecamp/37signals): Turbo Drive handles page-level navigation, Turbo Frames enable partial page replacement, Turbo Streams push server-sent updates. The philosophical commitment is "HTML Over the Wire" — the server sends HTML fragments, never JSON. Used in production by HEY and Basecamp.

Unpoly: A library with a similar server-rendered fragment model that predates htmx and adds more opinionated tooling including migration scripts and layer management.

Datastar: A TypeScript rewrite that combines htmx-style fragment replacement with Alpine.js-style client reactivity, unified via Server-Sent Events (SSE). Its specific contribution is treating SSE as a first-class transport — this turns out to matter for AI agent interfaces, because agents stream intermediate outputs and SSE natively maps onto that pattern.

Triptych (Alexander Petros, htmx-adjacent): Three proposals to incorporate htmx's core capabilities — PUT/PATCH/DELETE in HTML forms, button-level HTTP actions, partial page replacement — into the HTML standard itself. Carson Gross identifies this as htmx's long-term institutional ambition: make the browser itself a richer hypermedia client by extending the HTML spec.

The shared design commitments across these projects are: (1) the server owns state and renders HTML; (2) the client requests HTML fragments and replaces DOM regions; (3) JavaScript is used declaratively via HTML attributes rather than imperatively; (4) the system stays within REST's architectural constraints.

III. What Hypermedia-Revival Thinkers Have Actually Written About LLM Agents

This is where the research produces a clear demarcation: direct, sustained treatment of LLMs as hypermedia clients is scarce and concentrated in one place.

Carson Gross (htmx.org): Has written one essay directly on MCP and hypermedia — "Hypermedia Friendly Model Context Protocol App Architecture" (March 2026). The essay argues that when building UIs for LLM-hosted MCP applications, hypermedia-based server rendering is architecturally superior to client-side frameworks, specifically because (a) the iframe constraints of MCP app environments favor minimal client state, and (b) hypermedia fragments reduce unnecessary LLM token consumption by allowing client-side interactions without re-rendering the entire UI through the AI. This treats the LLM as the host environment, not as the hypermedia client. The "Hypermedia Clients" essay contains no discussion of AI agents at all.

Mike Amundsen: The most active thinker at the intersection. His Substack "Signals from Our Futures Past" (mamund.substack.com) contains: "Hypermedia is the Missing Control Surface for AI" (February 2026), "Reimagining Agentic Systems," "Why I Keep Talking About Affordances," "Don't Design Workflows, Discover Them." His GRAIL framework (Goal-Resolution through Affordance-Informed Logic) is a working demonstration: an agent is given a goal and a hypermedia environment; it discovers available affordances at runtime, attempts actions, receives failure responses that expose unmet preconditions as further affordances, and navigates from start to finish without a pre-defined workflow. The Nordic APIs article "HATEOAS: The API Design Style That Was Waiting for AI" (September 2025) quotes Darrel Miller (Microsoft): "hypermedia does solve the AI agent problem of tool selection and efficiently maintaining context." Kevin Duffey: "HATEOAS was just waiting for the right technology" — LLMs and MCP.

Amundsen's core claim: hypermedia provides an environment of affordances — things that can be done here and now, in context, visible without prediction or prior agreement, actionable rather than inferrable. For AI agents, this solves three problems simultaneously: tool selection (the response tells the agent what is available given the current state), context maintenance (the response carries the current state), and provider control (hypermedia can guide agents through permitted operations through what it exposes or omits).

HyperAgents workshop (ECAI 2025): An academic community bridging semantic web and autonomous agents, now explicitly incorporating LLM considerations. The 2023 AAMAS paper "Signifiers as a First-class Abstraction in Hypermedia Multi-Agent Systems" (Vachtsevanou et al.) formalizes the notion that hypermedia environments should expose machine-readable signifiers — structured signals about what affordances exist and for whom — to enable heterogeneous agents with different capabilities to discover and exploit affordances at runtime.

IV. What the Tradition Implies for Agent-Navigable Document Design

Assembling the logical chain from the tradition:

Step 1 (Fielding/Gross): A properly REST-ful client needs no out-of-band knowledge. The document is its own specification. All transitions are encoded in the response.

Step 2 (Gross, "Hypermedia Clients"): A hypermedia client must be able to render the hypermedia without domain-specific knowledge. The browser achieves this for human-facing HTML. The question of what an LLM achieves for machine-facing HTML is precisely the question the tradition has not yet asked.

Step 3 (Gross, "Two Approaches to Decoupling"): HTML currently "makes for a terrible general purpose API for machine clients" — the concession is that extracting structured data from HTML is hard for programs. But this complaint was written before LLMs, which parse and navigate HTML with reasonable competence. The concession may have expired.

Step 4 (Amundsen, GRAIL): An LLM agent navigating a hypermedia environment that exposes contextual affordances can discover workflows at runtime without pre-programmed knowledge. The inference: if the document encodes what sections exist, what each contains, and what transitions are available, an LLM agent can navigate it without a separate documentation layer.

Step 5 (VOIX paper, arXiv 2511.11287): The state of practice is that AI agents "must infer available actions by scraping HTML," leading to brittle, inefficient, insecure interactions. The VOIX framework (2025) proposes new <tool> and <context> HTML tags to expose machine-readable affordances inline. This is hypermedia thinking without citing hypermedia tradition — it independently converges on the htmx/HATEOAS insight that the document should carry its own specifications.

Step 6 (IETF draft-han-ai-manifest): The AI Manifest proposal embeds a JSON manifest (reachable via /.well-known/ai-manifest.json or as a linked resource) that declares UI workflow instructions, known traps, and shortcuts for AI agents. The demonstrated result: 81.9% reduction in input tokens consumed by the AI agent, task success rate rising from 20% to 100% on a representative multi-step transaction.

The tradition implies but has not stated: an HTML document whose sections carry in-document machine-readable summaries of what they contain, estimated reading cost, content hashes for cache validation, and dependency edges to other sections would be a genuine hypermedia artifact in Fielding's sense — one where the document is its own index and the client needs no external documentation to decide whether and how to read it.

V. The Existing Landscape for AI-Navigable Documents

llms.txt (Jeremy Howard, September 2024): A Markdown file at the site root providing a curated map of documentation content. Solves the problem of AI systems wasting context parsing navigation elements and scripts. But it is external to the HTML document, and carries no section-level granularity, no token cost estimates, no content hashes, no dependency structure.

Vercel Agent Readability Spec: Best practices covering llms.txt discovery, Schema.org JSON-LD structured data, content negotiation to serve Markdown mirrors, heading density requirements, code block labeling. No hypermedia framing. No in-document manifest concept.

"The Unreasonable Effectiveness of HTML" (Thariq Shihipar, Anthropic, May 2026): An Anthropic engineer's argument that HTML is superior to Markdown for Claude Code output — richer information density, native browser rendering, shareability, two-way interaction. Mentions in-document structure implicitly but frames it entirely as human-readability and richness, not machine navigability. Does not cite hypermedia tradition.

VOIX (arXiv 2511.11287, November 2025): Proposes <tool> and <context> HTML elements that expose machine-readable affordances inline. Does not cite hypermedia tradition. Does not address section-level navigation, token estimation, or dependency edges.

IETF AI Manifest draft (draft-han-ai-manifest): External JSON with shortcut declarations and DOM selectors. Not in-document. No hypermedia framing.

VI. What Has and Has Not Been Argued

What has been argued

  • HTML is a natural hypermedia and the browser is a natural hypermedia client (Gross, Fielding, Hypermedia Systems book — well established).
  • HATEOAS principles apply to AI agents navigating API workflows (Amundsen, Nordic APIs, GRAIL framework — argued 2025-2026, with working demonstration).
  • AI agents suffer when forced to infer affordances from human-oriented interfaces; they need explicit in-document machine-readable affordances (VOIX paper, IETF manifest draft — argued 2025, with empirical evidence for token reduction).
  • HTML-native hypermedia controls should be extended via Triptych proposals (argued and in progress as W3C/WHATWG discussion).
  • Hypermedia environments expose contextual affordances that let agents discover workflows at runtime without pre-programmed knowledge (Amundsen GRAIL, HyperAgents workshop — argued 2023-2025).

What has not been argued — the specific claim that remains to be written

The tradition has not made the argument that a single HTML document artifact should carry an in-document manifest of its own addressable sections, where each manifest entry exposes an id, human-authored summary, estimated token cost, content hash, and dependency edges — and that this constitutes fulfilling Fielding's uniform interface constraint for LLM clients specifically.

The gap is precise: the htmx/hypermedia tradition has applied HATEOAS to API workflow navigation by AI agents (Amundsen), and the agent-web community has proposed external JSON manifests and new HTML elements for action affordances (VOIX, AI Manifest). But no one has made the document-level claim: that the in-document manifest of addressable reading sections — not action affordances but navigation affordances for a reading agent — is the correct unit of analysis, and that embedding token cost, content hash, and dependency edges directly in the document makes the document REST-ful in precisely Fielding's sense for LLM clients.

The VOIX paper's <context> element is the nearest precedent — it exposes application state as plain text for agent consumption. The section-manifest idea is the <context> pattern applied not to application state but to the document's own structure.

What might suggest the framing is wrong

The htmx tradition's own concession — that HTML is "a terrible general purpose API for machine clients" because data extraction is hard — suggests the tradition sees a categorical difference between human hypermedia clients (browsers) and machine clients (programs). The argument that LLMs dissolve this distinction would need to be made explicitly, and it has not been. If that argument fails — if LLMs are actually better served by clean Markdown or JSON than by structured HTML — then the hypermedia framing adds philosophical weight but not practical advantage.

The llms.txt ecosystem's rapid adoption suggests practitioners are solving the problem with external manifests rather than in-document ones. If the question is "what do LLM agents actually use to navigate sites?", the answer currently is external files, not in-document structure. The in-document approach has stronger philosophical grounding (Fielding's in-band principle) but no empirical track record yet.

Protocol-Level Context Architecture: Designing the Agent-Addressable Knowledge Object

The Context Layering Landscape and Analogies

As artificial intelligence agents transition from guided chat assistants to autonomous web-scale actors, the mechanisms of knowledge retrieval and execution demand structural reform. Historically, developer integrations with foundation models have relied on stateless, eager injection of tool schemas and document indices. Under standard implementations of the Model Context Protocol (MCP), an agent client retrieves a full directory of available capabilities and metadata upon connection, stuffing thousands of token-heavy schemas directly into the prompt window before a single processing turn occurs. This operational model introduces what is recognized as the "Tools Tax" or "MCP Tax"—a hidden, per-turn computational overhead ranging from 10,000 to 60,000 tokens.

This token inflation has compounding negative externalities: it accelerates the compaction of key-value (KV) caches, increases transactional latency, escalates operational costs, and induces severe degradation in computational planning as context utilization approaches published fracture points around 70% of maximum capacity. While sparse attention kernels, flash attention optimizations, and KV cache quantization reduce hardware-level costs, they fail to address the underlying protocol inefficiency.

To eliminate this structural tax, emerging patterns are shifting focus from expanding context windows to enforcing architectural precision. The objective is the formulation of a standard artifact primitive: a human-readable, agent-navigable, selectively hydratable knowledge object.

To contextualize this primitive within the modern documentation stack, it is necessary to contrast it with existing standards. At the repository level, AGENTS.md functions as the onboarding manual for coding agents, specifying tech stacks, directory trees, compilation commands, and forbidden coding patterns. At the execution level, Agent Skills package reusable procedures using YAML discovery metadata, Markdown instructions, and dynamic on-demand resources.

While MCP standardizes the protocol-level hosting of tools, prompts, and resources, its stateless design creates severe context burdens that newer patterns, such as Tool Search and Programmatic Tool Calling, attempt to mitigate via progressive disclosure.

The proposed Agent-Addressable Knowledge Object (AAKO)—hereafter referred to as the Context Surface—occupies a distinct functional space. It packages semantic knowledge rather than active operational capabilities, remaining richly readable to humans as standard HTML while exposing a compact machine-readable map or manifest to agents.

By defining clear structural nodes, the Context Surface allows an agent to understand what exists inside the artifact without loading the underlying bulk data, selectively hydrating only the precise sections or objects needed for the current reasoning act.

Artifact Layer Primary Focus Human Readability Machine Readability Execution Trigger State Model
AGENTS.md Repository-level onboarding and style rules High (Markdown) Moderate (Structured Markdown) Initial agent entry / session setup Static
Agent Skills Procedural workflows and action-oriented steps Moderate (Markdown rules) High (YAML discovery metadata) Explicit task delegation Transitional
Model Context Protocol Dynamic tool calling and capability execution Low (Unstructured outputs) High (JSON Schemas) Agentic execution loops Dynamic
Context Surface (Proposed) Highly structured semantic knowledge packaging High (HTML rendering) High (JSON-LD manifest and Markdown) Dynamic intent gating / Selective hydration State-gated / Selective

Prior Art and Adjacent Architectural Foundations

The development of the Context Surface does not require building from a blank slate; rather, it synthesizes technical traditions from adjacent web standards, digital publishing formats, and structured technical authoring.

Adjacent Web-Native Specifications

Within the web ecosystem, Vercel’s Agent Readability Specification establishes a concrete model for serving dual-surface representations. It outlines sitemap requirements (such as sitemap.xml with \<lastmod\> tags and a user-friendly sitemap.md for hierarchy navigation), site-level index files (llms.txt and llms-full.txt served with a text/plain content-type), and page-level markdown mirrors. Vercel's protocol relies on standard HTTP content negotiation: when a client requests a page with an Accept: text/markdown header, the server returns a clean markdown representation containing metadata frontmatter and standard anchor paths, bypassing layout styling and script overhead.

A direct comparison reveals how these specifications target different phases of the agentic lifecycle:

Dimension llms.txt agents.md Context Surface Protocol (Proposed)
Primary Focus Factual Site Overview (Discovery) Action-Oriented Instructions (How-to) Segmented, Hydratable Knowledge Nodes
Typical Token Budget Minimal (30-80 lines) Moderate (200-2,000 lines) Scalable (Indexed dynamically)
Hydration Pattern Eager (Full load of index) Eager (Full load of prompt rules) Lazy Gated (Sectional Phase-1/Phase-2)
Primary Client General Search/Research Crawlers Active Interaction/Transaction Agents Reasoning Models needing explicit Context
Payload Format Plain text / Concise Markdown Rich Markdown with code blocks/schemas Negotiated Markdown \+ JSON-LD Manifest

Concurrently, Google's experimental WebMCP standard (introduced behind experimental flags in Chrome 146 Canary) explores a Declarative API that turns standard HTML forms into agent-callable tools. By adding attributes such as toolname, tooldescription, and toolparamdescription directly to client-side input tags, WebMCP wraps existing client-side JavaScript logic into a standardized tool surface.

While WebMCP optimizes capability execution inside the browser, it highlights the companion need for a structured knowledge substrate: agents require a robust way to digest the technical data that feeds these declarative tools.

Prior Art in Digital Publishing and Technical Authoring

Outside the field of artificial intelligence, mature specifications have long managed the challenges of content distribution and structural indexing. The W3C Publication Manifest and Readium Web Publication Manifest establish models for representing publications over HTTPS. A Readium manifest uses a JSON-LD serialization containing descriptive metadata, a default reading order (readingOrder), and a resources list (resources) comprised of hypermedia Link Objects. These Link Objects specify URI templates, media types, sizes, and relationships, allowing user agents to parse document boundaries and access individual chapters progressively without pulling down the entire publication archive.

In the domain of enterprise technical writing, IBM's XML-based Darwin Information Typing Architecture (DITA) organizes technical documentation into modular, semantic, context-free topics (such as concepts, tasks, and reference sheets). These topics are authored against strict schemas, separated from visual layout rules, and assembled into hierarchical maps. Modern implementations, such as Oxygen's XML Positron 8.1, compile these structured topics into optimized markdown files, allowing multi-step agent planners to coordinate project-wide documentation updates without hitting context window boundaries.

Furthermore, visual and spatial navigation systems have introduced Set-of-Mark (SoM) prompting. By overlaying spatial coordinates, speakable numerical markers, and bounding boxes onto layout screenshots, SoM enables multimodal foundation models to navigate interfaces and complex documents as visual canvases, bypassing underlying HTML parsing entirely. Microsoft's Magma foundation model integrates these SoM annotations to acquire spatial intelligence across both digital screenshots and physical environments, demonstrating that visual structuring can complement purely textual semantics.

Structural Analysis of the Seven Architectural Candidates

Developing a community-level primitive requires analyzing the technical mechanics of the competing candidate families.

Candidate 1: Manifested HTML Artifact (.agent.html semantic dialect)

This candidate models the Context Surface as a specialized HTML5 dialect. The document utilizes semantic elements such as \<nav\>, \<main\>, \<section\>, \<aside\>, and \<h1-h6\> heading levels, augmented with ARIA landmark regions and semantic state tags to expose a clean structural taxonomy.

By default, standard web browsers render the visual styling to human readers, while headless drivers extract the document's underlying Accessibility Tree—which filters out thousands of styling div tags, layout scripts, and interactive noise—directly as the agent interface. WebMCP-style declarative attributes are embedded directly inside container elements to flag structural blocks as hydratable nodes.

While highly compatible with existing web deployment patterns, raw DOM parsing is highly token-inefficient if the browser rendering layer fails to pre-process the accessibility snapshots.

Candidate 2: EPUB-like Agent Publication Package

Derived from the Readium Web Publication standard, this architecture bundles knowledge into a compressed .zip or .epub package containing a top-level JSON-LD publication manifest and a folder of modular Markdown content nodes. The manifest defines structural metadata, an explicit chronological reading order, and dynamic resource lists.

The primary advantage of this candidate is its exceptional offline portability: the package has rigid file boundaries, can be distributed via simple peer-to-peer sharing, and maintains perfect internal link integrity.

However, the requirement to programmatically compile, write, and unzip physical archive packages creates substantial friction for web developers and introduces massive performance overhead during real-time generation and selective node hydration over standard network protocols.

Candidate 3: HTML \+ Sidecar JSON Semantic Index

This dual-surface candidate divides responsibilities: a standard, highly styled HTML document serves human readers, while a companion JSON sidecar file acts as the machine-readable map of the document's internal nodes.

The HTML page remains standard, but includes a \<link rel="alternate" type="application/json" href="/doc.json"\> tag in its header. The sidecar JSON file maps specific CSS selectors, DOM anchors, or URI fragments to their corresponding semantic hashes, node summaries, and token weights.

When an agent accesses the resource, it first fetches the lightweight JSON index, processes the node summaries, and then requests only the specific HTML nodes required, parsing them via the CSS selector map.

While elegant, this candidate introduces a high risk of "index drift," where modifications to the human-facing HTML document are not mirrored in the selector references of the JSON sidecar, leading to parsing failures.

Candidate 4: XML/DITA-like Authoring Model Compiled to HTML \+ Agent Map

Under this paradigm, developers author knowledge as semantic, context-free XML topics using precise schemas. A compilation step (e.g., via the DITA Open Toolkit or Oxygen AI Positron engines) compiles these files into two output targets: a human-oriented web layout and a companion, highly optimized agent map built on a clean markdown schema.

Because DITA forces technical authors to explicitly separate semantic content from visual display, the resulting agent map contains highly precise technical instructions, surfacing implicit assumptions and documenting the "why" behind the data.

Despite these structural benefits, the learning curve for DITA/XML structured authoring is incredibly steep, and compiling XML schemas is fundamentally incompatible with the rapid, LLM-driven generation pipelines that dominate modern application design.

Candidate 5: HTTP-First Semantic Representation Protocol

This candidate relies entirely on standard HTTP protocols and content-type negotiation to handle dual-surface delivery. A single canonical URL represents the knowledge resource.

When a browser requests the URL with Accept: text/html, the server delivers a fully styled visual interface. When an AI agent requests the same URL with Accept: text/markdown or Accept: text/markdown+agent, the server uses content negotiation to deliver a clean markdown mirror.

The markdown mirror contains frontmatter metadata and a Link HTTP response header pointing back to the canonical HTML page. For selective hydration, the agent uses HTTP Range-like headers (e.g., Range: bytes= or custom X-Agent-Section: selectors) to request only the specific sections of the markdown file.

This model requires no packaging overhead and integrates seamlessly with modern web CDNs, but it lacks offline viability without a server layer to handle active negotiation.

Candidate 6: SOM-like Agent Object Model for Authored Documents

Adapting visual grounding principles to document layouts, this approach completely bypasses textual DOM parsing. The authoring pipeline compiles documents into standard multi-page PDFs or high-resolution layout images.

An automated segmentation layer overlays numerical bounding boxes and speakable marks onto the visual canvas. Multimodal models (VLMs) process the spatial image directly, referencing these numeric markers to determine which sections to analyze.

While highly generalizable for complex visual layouts (such as financial charts and engineering diagrams), this candidate is extremely token-inefficient for text-centric technical documentation, as visual models require large image-token allocations and are prone to transcription errors during copy-paste operations.

Candidate 7: Hybrid Skill \+ Context Surface Package

This architecture integrates static knowledge surfaces with active execution paths, creating a single package that contains both declarative data and the code patterns needed to interact with it. Housed in a structured Markdown or JSON-LD configuration file, it merges the sitemap pattern of an llms.txt file with the deep operational execution paths of an agents.md or MCP server description.

For example, a database catalog would not only describe the schema and column metadata but also include the BAML prompt schemas and HTTP authentication credentials required to query the live endpoints.

While highly powerful for transactional integrations, this unified package violates the separation of concerns, dramatically increasing the "Tools Tax" if the capability schemas are loaded alongside the static knowledge context.

Comprehensive Architectural Evaluation Matrix

Determining the community-level standard requires mapping all seven candidates across fourteen distinct engineering dimensions, derived from practitioner telemetry and deployment constraints.

Evaluation Dimension Candidate 1: Manifested HTML Candidate 2: EPUB Package Candidate 3: Sidecar JSON Candidate 4: XML/DITA Compiled Candidate 5: HTTP-First Candidate 6: SOM Spatial Candidate 7: Hybrid Package
Human Readability High (Visual rendering) Moderate (Requires zip extractor) High (Direct HTML render) High (Post-compile render) High (Native HTML render) High (Visual PDF layout) Moderate (Textual Markdown)
LLM Authorability Moderate (Complex tag layouts) Low (Must build zip directories) High (Standard JSON schemas) Low (Requires valid XML validation) High (Outputs clean Markdown) Low (Must render coordinates) Moderate (Complex schema files)
Developer Learning Curve Low (Standard HTML5/ARIA) High (EPUB packaging standards) Medium (Maintaining JSON indices) High (XML/DITA schemas) Low (Basic routing & Markdown) Medium (Computer vision coordinate systems) Medium (BAML/MCP tool routing)
Token Efficiency Low (Heavy DOM wrapper noise) High (Manifest blocks data) High (Index abstracts nodes) High (Highly compressed text maps) High (Serves raw Markdown) Low (Large image token allocations) Moderate (Payload includes schemas)
Selective Hydration Precision Moderate (Parses DOM subtrees) High (Loads isolated file nodes) High (Resolves targeted selectors) High (Maps exact DITA topics) High (Uses HTTP ranges/headers) Low (Requires visual cropping) High (Resolves exact BAML objects)
Offline/Local Use High (Single local file) High (Self-contained archive) Medium (Requires two files) High (Local file directory) Low (Requires active web server) High (Local PDF/images) High (Self-contained local rules)
Web Deployment High (Standard hosting) Low (Requires MIME type support) High (Dual-file static hosting) High (Standard HTML output) High (Content negotiation CDN) Medium (Heavy media servers) Medium (Dynamic web endpoints)
