Provenance: Claude (Sonnet) research document, 2025

DITA as Prior Art for an HTML-Based Progressive-Disclosure Standard for LLM Agents

TL;DR

  • The three or four DITA primitives most worth borrowing are: (1) the topic map / ditamap as a manifest separate from content, (2) shortdesc (a human-authored, addressable one-paragraph summary intended for link previews), (3) conref-style transclusion by stable element @id, and (4) the chunk attribute's logic for controlling rendering granularity at the manifest layer. Topic typing, reltables, and the full conditional-processing apparatus are not worth porting — they encode workflows the LLM use case does not need.
  • Deliberately leave behind: the XML toolchain, DITA-OT preprocessing, DTD/schema validation, specialization, props/audience/platform/product filtering, DITAVAL files, and the strict concept/task/reference taxonomy. These are exactly the parts that drove developers, OSS communities, and small teams to reject DITA in favor of Markdown.
  • **The actual unbuilt gap is not the manifest itself — it is the single‑artifact, in-band manifest with per-section token costs and content hashes.** llms.txt, sitemap.xml, MCP resources, OpenAPI, and Anthropic's SKILL.md already cover site-wide and capability-level progressive disclosure across multiple files. None of them yet describe sub-sections of one HTML document with hashable, hydratable fragments addressed by stable ID. That is the niche; the rest is filled.

Key Findings

  1. DITA already provides every conceptual primitive the proposal needs — a manifest (ditamap), per-topic summaries (shortdesc), addressable IDs (@id/@href), dependency edges (reltable/conref), and output-granularity control (chunk). The proposal essentially reinvents the topic map.
  2. DITA failed in exactly the population the proposal targets — developers, OSS, small teams — primarily for tooling and Git-diff reasons, not because the underlying ideas were wrong. The lesson is to keep the primitives but discard the XML/DTD/DITA-OT substrate.
  3. DITA's own attempt to lighten itself, Lightweight DITA (LwDITA/MDITA/HDITA), has effectively stalled. OASIS members of the DITA TC approved the Committee Note "by a Full Majority vote on 30 December 2018" (the document bears an internal draft date of 30 October 2018), it is explicitly non-normative ("This is a Non-Standards Track Work Product"), and no major OSS docs project uses it. This is a cautionary signal: a lighter DITA was not enough; the LLM-agent use case calls for a different artifact entirely.
  4. Several adjacent standards already do parts of this — llms.txt (site-level Markdown manifest), sitemap.xml/site-llms.xml (URL discovery), Anthropic's Agent Skills (SKILL.md frontmatter as a progressive-disclosure manifest for skills), MCP resources (capability discovery), and OpenAPI (machine-readable API surface). What none of them do is describe the internal sections of a single HTML document with hashable, token-priced, dependency-aware metadata.

Details

1. DITA topic structure and addressability

A DITA topic is a self-contained XML file with a title, an optional shortdesc/abstract, and a typed body (conbody, taskbody, refbody). The shortdesc element is explicitly designed as a reusable summary — "the short description, which represents the purpose or theme of the topic, is also intended to be used as a link preview and for search results," and is recommended to be "a single, concise paragraph containing one or two sentences of no more than 50 words" (OASIS DITA 1.3 Language Reference). This is, almost word-for-word, the "human-authored summary" the researcher wants in their manifest.

Addressability is two-layered. Each topic has an @id; each element inside can have its own @id. Cross-references use the syntax filename.dita#topicid/elementid. Topics reference each other through <xref>, but the canonical cross-topic dependency graph lives in the ditamap via <topicref href="…"> elements with @keys for indirect addressing. The @keys/@keyref system (preserved in LwDITA) allows references to be resolved at build time, decoupling logical name from physical location — directly analogous to a hash-addressed manifest entry.

Conref (content reference) is DITA's transclusion mechanism: any element with an ID becomes a reusable fragment, addressable as file.dita#topicid/elementid. The OASIS spec describes it as "similar to XInclude and to HyTime value references," but with validity constraints checked at build time. Conref's defining feature for our purpose: the referencing element is a placeholder — the agent equivalent is "don't load this section yet; if you need it, here is the resolvable address."

The chunk attribute is DITA's lever for deciding at manifest time what becomes one output document vs. many. Values include by-topic/by-document (split policy), to-content/to-navigation (rendering policy), and select-topic/select-document (selection policy). The OASIS Architectural Spec gives a worked example: a chunk="to-content" on a parent <topicref> collapses children into a single output HTML file. The relevance for an LLM artifact: chunking decisions belong in the manifest, not in the content, and they should be independent of the content hash.

2. Toolchain and build-time composition

DITA-OT (the Open Toolkit) and editors like Oxygen XML process a ditamap by walking the topicref tree, resolving conrefs and keyrefs, applying conditional filters (DITAVAL files via --args.filter=), and emitting deliverables (HTML, PDF, EPUB). The ditamap functions as exactly the kind of separate manifest the proposal envisions: it carries navtitles, shortdesc overrides per context, reltables, chunking decisions, and metadata cascades — and the topics themselves stay reusable across maps. This is the architectural pattern worth lifting wholesale: content is content; the manifest decides what it becomes.

Conditional processing uses six classifying attributes (@audience, @platform, @product, @otherprops, @props, @rev), with a DITAVAL profile saying which values to include, exclude, or flag. This is essentially per-fragment feature flags. For an LLM-agent artifact, the equivalent would be agent capability/role tags (e.g., "load only sections audience=developer") — a useful idea, but, as discussed below, probably not worth borrowing directly because it duplicates what the agent can already decide from the shortdesc.

3. Adoption history — what succeeded, what failed

Succeeded in: IBM (the originator; DITA was built to manage 60M+ documents in 40 languages), Adobe, AMD/ATI, BlackBerry, Nokia, Siemens Healthcare, Kone, and enterprises in semiconductors, telecom, medical devices, and aerospace/defense. The common factor is large-scale, multi-language, multi-deliverable, regulated documentation where translation cost dominates and reuse pays for the tooling overhead (Amber Swope's cited case: $225,000 in translation savings in three months on a single doc set, per Heretto's "10 Cases for Enterprise DITA Adoption").

Failed/rejected in: developer documentation communities, API docs, OSS, small teams. The most-cited reasons:

  • XML verbosity — comparisons of a "print a page" task in DITA vs. Markdown show 5–10× more characters.
  • XML editor dependency — practical authoring requires Oxygen or FrameMaker; "writing in DITA needs an XML editor" (Writing Technically docs).
  • Poor Git diffs — XML's smallest meaningful unit (the element) often spans multiple lines, and line-based diff tools "can be fiddly to work with and you can easily end up with invalid files or missing content" (Earfinders). Even DITA shops resort to Oxygen's custom XML-aware diff (ditanauts.org).
  • Steep learning curve — specialization, constraints, key spaces, and DITAVAL together create a "300 kg tool" (passo.uno) where "developers… are used to 300 gr notepads."
  • Tooling mismatch — Tom Johnson's much-cited critique: "Nearly every developer tool for API documentation, such as Slate, API Blueprint, Doxygen, Nanoc, Github, and Docco all use Markdown instead of HTML or XML." DITA never crossed that boundary.

LwDITA — the failed lighter alternative. OASIS's own attempt to make DITA lightweight (MDITA in Markdown, HDITA in HTML5) is frozen at Committee Note 02; per the OASIS announcement, "Members of the DITA TC approved this Committee Note by a Full Majority vote on 30 December 2018" (the document itself is internally dated 30 October 2018), and it is explicitly labeled non-normative — "This is a Non-Standards Track Work Product. The patent provisions of the OASIS IPR Policy do not apply." DITA-OT documentation still warns: "Since Lightweight DITA has not yet been released as a formal specification, the implementation… is subject to change." TC working schemas remain at v0.3.0.2. No well-known OSS docs project (Kubernetes, Python, Django, React, Docusaurus, MkDocs, Sphinx, etc.) has adopted it. The DITA TC's energy has moved to DITA 2.0 instead. **This matters for the proposal: it is direct evidence that "lighter DITA" is not what the developer ecosystem wanted; they wanted not DITA.**

4. Portability of DITA primitives to an HTML/Markdown LLM-agent standard

DITA primitive Portable? Verdict
Topic typing (concept/task/reference) No Drop. Anthropic's SKILL.md uses a free-form description field with no enforced taxonomy and works fine. The LLM can infer purpose from the shortdesc. LwDITA itself dropped the typed-topic taxonomy in its core profile.
Conref / transclusion Yes Keep, but as a stable #fragment ID convention plus content hash, not as XML-validated element substitution. The agent needs to know "this section can be hydrated independently and has identity X."
Reltables No (mostly) Drop the table syntax; keep the concept of typed cross-section edges (requires, extends, see-also). Reltables solved a 2005 problem (auto-generating "Related Topics" lists in rendered HTML); the LLM use case wants the edge graph itself, not generated link sections.
Conditional processing / DITAVAL Probably no Drop. The agent can filter by reading the shortdesc. A DITAVAL-style profile adds a separate build step and a separate file, exactly what the single-artifact proposal is trying to avoid.
ditamap (the manifest) Yes — this is the core borrow. Keep. The map-vs-topic separation is DITA's strongest idea. Anthropic Skills' YAML frontmatter, llms.txt's H1+bulleted-link layout, and MCP's resources/list are all rediscoveries of it.
chunk attribute Yes (as logic, not syntax) Keep the principle — the manifest decides hydration granularity. The proposal's "agent reads the manifest and hydrates only sections it needs" is chunk="by-topic" plus processing-role="resource-only".
Metadata (navtitle, shortdesc, audience, product) shortdesc yes, rest no shortdesc is the single most directly portable element: a 50-word, link-preview-grade summary written by humans. audience/product are profiling baggage. navtitle collapses into the manifest entry's own title.

5. Adjacent prior art that partially solves this

  • llms.txt (proposed by Jeremy Howard of Answer.AI on 3 September 2024). A root-level Markdown file with H1 site title, blockquote summary, and bulleted Page title: one-line description entries. Solves: site-level discovery, human-authored summaries, link-to-Markdown-version convention. Doesn't solve: sub-document granularity, dependency edges, content hashes, token costs. Empirical adoption is weak: an August 2025 CDN-log audit by Flavio Longato (GEO Strategist at Adobe) across 1,000 Adobe Experience Manager domains over 30 days found "LLM-specific bots stayed away. No GPTBot, ClaudeBot, PerplexityBot, or similar were seen at all"; the same audit reports that "Google still probes everything. Its desktop crawler accounted for 95% of all hits." Major AI labs have not formally endorsed the format.
  • sitemap.xml / site-llms.xml. Pure URL discovery + <lastmod>. No summaries, no edges.
  • Anthropic Agent Skills (SKILL.md). A directory with YAML frontmatter (name, description, optional allowed-tools) and a Markdown body. Officially implements three-level progressive disclosure. Per Aurimas Griciūnas's measurements in the SwirlAI Newsletter (11 March 2026) across Anthropic's 17 official skills: "the median discovery cost is ~80 tokens per skill, ranging from ~55 (webapp-testing) to ~235 (xlsx). All 17 skills together cost ~1,700 tokens"; body size "ranges from ~275 tokens (internal-comms) to ~8,000 tokens (skill-creator), with a median around 2,000." This is the closest existing precedent to the researcher's proposal, but it operates over a folder tree, not a single HTML artifact, and is scoped to agent skills/tools, not arbitrary document sections.
  • Model Context Protocol (MCP). Defines resources (read-only data), tools, and prompts with list/get JSON-RPC methods. Progressive disclosure over capabilities, not over document interiors. Production benchmarks vary widely: Matthew Kruczek's January 2026 whitepaper Progressive Disclosure MCP: 85x Token Savings Benchmark reports "85-100x reductions in token usage while maintaining or improving tool selection accuracy"; Speakeasy's Gram benchmarks claim "up to 160x token reduction compared to static toolsets while maintaining 100% success rates across diverse tasks."
  • OpenAPI 3.x + API plugin manifests. Microsoft's OpenApi.ApiManifest slices large OpenAPI descriptions (Microsoft Graph has "1000s of operations") into per-task subsets with auth metadata. AI agents now treat OpenAPI as their primary API-discovery surface. Again — capability surface, not document surface.
  • JSON-LD / schema.org TechArticle / HowTo. Provides typed metadata about an article as a whole, embeddable in HTML via <script type="application/ld+json">. Does not natively address fragments inside the article, and is optimized for search-engine rich results, not LLM hydration.
  • HTML Imports. Worth naming and dismissing: deprecated by all major browsers by ~2018–2020 in favor of ES Modules. Not a viable substrate.
  • Web Components / <template> / Shadow DOM. Solves UI encapsulation, not semantic addressability for agents.

Recommendations

Stage 1 — Borrow exactly four primitives, in this order:

  1. A manifest separate from content, embedded in-band as a single <script type="application/ld+json"> (or application/json) block at the top of the HTML. This is the ditamap, repackaged. Every entry maps to a <section id="…"> in the same document. Carry: id, title, shortdesc (≤50 words, human-authored), tokens (integer estimate), hash (sha256 of the rendered section), deps[] (array of other ids this section requires loaded first).
  2. A shortdesc-style summary requirement. Make it mandatory and enforce the 50-word ceiling. This is the load-decision payload for the agent; everything else in the manifest is for verification and ordering. Do not allow it to default to the first paragraph — that fails for the same reason DITA shops insisted on hand-written shortdesc.
  3. Conref-by-fragment-ID. Stable id attributes on each <section>, plus a convention that #fragment is a contractual addressing surface, not a layout artifact. Borrow the discipline (every reusable unit has a permanent ID) without the XML validity machinery.
  4. A chunk-equivalent on each manifest entry. Two booleans suffice: standalone: true/false (can this section be hydrated without its parent?) and expand: lazy/eager (should agents preload it?). Skip DITA's full token vocabulary.

Stage 2 — Deliberately leave behind:

  • The XML substrate, schema validation, DTDs, specialization, DITAVAL conditional profiling, the concept/task/reference taxonomy, reltables, the entire audience/platform/product/otherprops attribute family, and DITA-OT-style build steps. These are the bits that drove the developer rejection.
  • Do not require an external file (like ditamap, llms.txt, sitemap.xml). The single-artifact constraint is what differentiates this from existing prior art.
  • Do not invent a topic-type taxonomy. Let the shortdesc do the routing work, the way Anthropic Skills' freeform description does.

Stage 3 — Thresholds that would change the recommendation:

  • If LwDITA suddenly moves to OASIS Standard status with strong tooling, re-evaluate MDITA .mditamap (Markdown H1 + bulleted links) as a serialization. As of May 2026 there is no evidence this will happen — the spec has not advanced past its 30 December 2018 Committee Note approval.
  • If Anthropic Skills' SKILL.md or MCP resources/list is extended to address sub-document fragments by ID with hash + token cost, the proposed standard becomes redundant — adopt the upstream extension instead.
  • If browser engines ship a native primitive for declarative HTML fragment manifests (no current proposal exists), reconsider whether a JSON manifest is still the right shape.

Caveats

  • The framing in the original question is mostly right but slightly mis-scoped. "Prior art" for the manifest concept is overwhelming — DITA, llms.txt, Skills, MCP, OpenAPI all do it. The novel claim is the single-artifact, in-band, sub-document, hash-and-token-priced version. Lead with that delta; do not lead with "manifest of addressable sections" because that part is solved.
  • Several DITA evangelist sources are vendors (Heretto, Oxygen, MadCap, IXIASOFT) with commercial interest in DITA succeeding. Translation savings figures (the $225K case) are vendor-cited and not independently audited.
  • llms.txt adoption claims from documentation platforms (Mintlify, Fern, GitBook, Yoast) are self-interested. Longato's 30-day CDN audit across 1,000 Adobe Experience Manager domains is the strongest independent evidence and it is negative: bots don't actually fetch llms.txt yet.
  • Token-cost estimation in the proposed manifest is harder than it looks — tokenizer differences across model families mean a single integer is approximate at best. The Anthropic Skills approach of measuring against a fixed tokenizer (Claude's) is the realistic precedent.
  • The DITA conref validity model (build-time element-type checking) has no equivalent in a hash-based fragment system. Loss of that integrity guarantee is a real cost, just one the LLM use case can probably tolerate.
  • LwDITA stagnation (one Committee Note in 7+ years, no major OSS adoption) is the single most important data point in this entire research file. It suggests that anything resembling DITA — even radically simplified — faces structural rejection in the OSS/developer ecosystem the researcher is targeting. The proposed standard should be careful to look and feel nothing like DITA, even though it borrows DITA's best ideas.

Provenance: Gemini research document, 2025

Evolutionary Archetypes of Agentic Web Infrastructure: The Emergence of the Selective-Hydration Semantic Agent Artifact

The rapid maturity of agentic artificial intelligence from simple conversational interfaces to autonomous software systems has initiated a profound shift in web architecture.1 For over three decades, the engineering patterns of the World Wide Web were optimized exclusively for human visual consumption, emphasizing layout structures, hierarchical visual weights, and behavioral click-through optimization.2 However, autonomous agents have introduced a completely distinct set of requirements to the browser.2 Operating over raw Document Object Model (DOM) trees, executing scraping routines, or capturing visual screenshots through headless browser engines incurs a massive "visual tax".2 This visual tax represents thousands of tokens of stylistic layout definitions, analytics trackers, and nested structural elements that do not contribute to core machine comprehension.2 A standard modern page can consume anywhere from 150,000 to 800,000 tokens of raw HTML, severely bloating the context window of large language models (LLMs), inducing latency, and driving up operational expenses.4 In response to these inefficiencies, a new agent-facing artifact primitive is emerging. This proposed paradigm defines a human-readable, agent-navigable, and selectively hydratable knowledge object. The motivating objective is to establish a standardized, highly compressed, and semantically dense medium that allows agents to discover, navigate, and dynamically execute capabilities within a single, secure transport package. This transition is heavily shaped by the fundamental technical divergence between static workflows and autonomous agents.1 In standard workflow systems, control is explicitly coded, meaning that identical inputs always traverse pre-defined logical branches.1 Agents, by contrast, make dynamic decisions at runtime, selecting tools on demand and executing self-repair loops over conversational context.1 Consequently, agents require rich, real-time semantic discovery protocols rather than rigid, hardcoded data structures.1

Comparative Analysis of Artifact and Protocol Representations

To determine the technical viability of this emerging primitive, it is necessary to evaluate how existing serialization formats, structured data protocols, and content packaging mechanisms stack up against the requirements of an agentic web.

Representation / Approach Structural Format Semantic Density & Token Efficiency Interactive & Behavioral Capabilities Client-Side Hydration Mechanics Contextual Applications & Tools
HTML (Standard DOM) 2 Nested hierarchical tree structure Extremely poor; presentational and tracking scripts cause extreme bloat. Implicit; interactions are bound to elements and must be inferred. Native browser parsing; framework-dependent hydration. Broadest consumer web footprint; high rendering latency.
Semantic Object Model (SOM) 4 Flat, non-nested region-to-element mapping Extremely high; averages a 17x to 133x token compression ratio. Explicit; interactive elements carry deterministic action annotations. Progressive visual layout sorting and selective elements. Plasmate agent engine; WebTaskBench benchmark runs.4
JSON Sidecars & A2A Cards 7 Key-value schemas or JSON-RPC outputs High; represents target metadata in a streamlined programmatic frame. Defines available API endpoints and programmatic skills. Discovered on-demand; dynamically read via JSON-RPC request/response. Platform-level agent discovery interfaces (e.g., agents.ml).
JSON-LD / RDFa 3 Semantic graph notation Variable; depends on ontology nesting and graph size. Passive; represents conceptual relationships, not executions. Single-pass extraction; lacks dynamic rendering states. Search engine optimizations; entity relational mapping.9
XML (DITA / DocBook) 10 Strongly typed markup templates Moderate; tag verbosity increases data footprint. Sequence mapping via content references and map files. Assembled statically prior to publishing or delivery. Modular technical authoring, content libraries, and CMS platforms.13
EPUB / W3C Publication Manifest 15 Grouped file container (ZIP / JSON metadata) Moderate; designed for offline distribution of static packages. Explicit sequencing through defined reading orders. Native client-side progressive loading of resource URLs. Readium architecture; OPDS 2.0 digital publishing networks.15
CSS-Based Selection 17 Structural style sheets and classes High; filters out layout elements by styling rules. None; only defines visual rendering states. Hooked to browser-level events (e.g., viewports, idle, triggers). PurgeCSS cleanups; web content extraction pipelines.18
BAML (Boundary AI Markup Language) 19 Specialized function contract DSL High; reduces schema token footprints by roughly 60%. High; compiles structured output constraints directly. Real-time stream parsing with immediate type validation. VS Code playgrounds, multi-language prompt engineering.19
HTTP Profile Negotiation 22 Protocol-level headers and parameters High; negotiates payload constraints at the request layer. Contextual selection of resource representations. Active negotiation during the initial request handshake. Dataset Exchange Working Group (DXWG) standards.23

While traditional markup languages like HTML provide high visual expressiveness for humans, their extreme token bloat makes them economically unviable for direct ingestion by reasoning models.4 On the other end of the spectrum, semantic formats like JSON-LD successfully map entity relationships but fail to capture human-readable visual flows or express interactive user-interface controls.8 This gap is filled by hybrid, flat JSON representations such as the Semantic Object Model (SOM), which map visually ordered webpage elements into actionable nodes while achieving substantial token savings.4

Prior Art and Motivating Analogies

The evolution of agentic artifacts spans multiple architectural layers, ranging from repository governance files to complex, multi-agent enterprise specs. An examination of these patterns highlights how developers have progressively adapted information structures for machine consumption.

Codebase and Repository-Level Artifacts

At the localized software layer, the developer community has established patterns like AGENTS.md, MEMORY.md, and CLAUDE.md.1 In highly complex repositories, files such as AGENTS.md act as the primary governance interface and relay channel for coding agents.25 In the Sayhi-bzb/Agent-HTML codebase, AGENTS.md dictates the working rules, contribution boundaries, architecture checkpoints, and verification expectations.25 This ensures that an incoming agent can immediately comprehend its constraints without processing hundreds of lines of developer chat logs or auxiliary documentation files.1 Crucially, this codebase structures its documentation hierarchically: high-level architecture rules live in the blueprint/ directory, daily constraints live in AGENTS.md, and specific procedural guides are stored under .agents/skills/.25 This separation of concerns directly supports the concept of selective hydration, ensuring that the agent's active memory is never overwhelmed with irrelevant details.1

Enterprise metadata and Tool-Calling Bundles

At the enterprise service scale, Salesforce's Agentforce DX demonstrates how metadata architectures can be structured natively for AI agents.27 Developers author an agent's logic using its "Agent Script" file—a language that blends natural language prompt directives with programmatic business expressions to enforce deterministic behaviors.27 The resulting script is packaged within an XML-based metadata component called the AiAuthoringBundle.27 To optimize this process, developers generate an "agent spec" YAML file using a dedicated CLI:

Bash agent generate agent-spec \--name "customer-agent"

This spec captures the core capability boundaries of the agent before compiling them into a final authoring bundle, demonstrating how compact, human-readable templates can serve as direct blueprints for agent behavior.27

Operational Role Specifications and Security Scaffolding

In security environments, Cisco's Foundry Security Spec establishes a standardized framework for security evaluation agents using GitHub's spec-kit.28 Rather than treating a frontier LLM as a loose chat interface, the Foundry specification wraps the model in a structured harness defined by eight core agent roles (such as orchestrator, indexer, cartographer, and detector) and roughly 130 functional requirements.28 To enforce this architecture, the spec operates alongside Project CodeGuard—a community-driven ruleset that builds security-by-default rules directly into coding workflows.28 These rules can be injected during the planning, code generation, or review phases of coding agents like Claude Code, Cursor, or Windsurf.28 This prior art proves that complex agentic actions can be successfully governed and executed using structured, rules-based specifications.28

Function Contracts and Schema-Aligned Parsing

To guarantee that model outputs strictly match application interfaces, Boundary AI Markup Language (BAML) provides a domain-specific language (DSL) that re-imagines prompts as typed function contracts.19 Traditional structured prompt libraries rely on verbose Python Pydantic models or JSON schemas that must be entirely parsed at runtime, leading to bloated token footprints and frequent structural hallucinations.19 BAML bypasses this by allowing developers to define schemas in a highly compact, custom syntax.19 A direct prompt comparison underscores the token efficiency achieved by this schema structure:

Code snippet // BAML Schema representation class Experience { company: string years: int }

When evaluated against a standard JSON Schema prompt, which requires extensive bracket overhead, type declarations, and nullability properties, the BAML equivalent achieves a 60% reduction in token count with zero loss of semantic fidelity.21 Furthermore, BAML leverages unconstrained decoding, allowing models to think via Chain of Draft (CoD) techniques in natural language, while its underlying Rust-based parser automatically handles dirty strings, converting written text (such as "five") into verified data types (such as 5).20

Technical Architecture of the Semantic Object Model and Agent Web Protocol

The most prominent implementation of an agent-facing semantic artifact is the Semantic Object Model (SOM), co-developed alongside the Agent Web Protocol (AWP) by the W3C Web Content Browser for AI Agents Community Group.3 Formulated as a flat, JSON-based format, SOM compiles complex interactive HTML structures into an optimized, machine-readable snapshot.4 Unlike standard HTML, which features deeply nested DOM trees that are computationally expensive to parse, a SOM document is strictly flat: documents contain regions, and regions contain elements.4

JSON { "som\_version": "1.0", "url": "https://example.com/docs/getting-started", "title": "Getting Started", "lang": "en", "regions": } \] } \], "meta": { "html\_bytes": 145000, "som\_bytes": 1200, "element\_count": 24, "interactive\_count": 2, "compression\_ratio": 120.8 } }

To ensure that elements remain stable across dynamic page refreshes—which frequently disrupt multi-step agent workflows—SOM generates deterministic, 12-character hex IDs.4 The SHA-256 hashing algorithm utilizes a concatenation of the origin domain, the element's semantic role, its W3C computed accessible name, and its simplified CSS path from the root.4 Mathematically, the cryptographic derivation of the element identifier is formulated as: This ensures that the exact same interactive element yields an identical ID over multiple sessions, providing stable targets for agentic interactions.4

Execution Protocol and Performance Benchmarks

To traverse and manipulate these documents, the Agent Web Protocol (AWP) establishes a native connection protocol.6 AWP replaces the heavy resource footprint of standard browser automation frameworks.6 Instead of spinning up a heavyweight headless Chrome instance via the Chrome DevTools Protocol (CDP), which typically consumes upwards of 20 gigabytes of memory across 100 pages, the Plasmate AWP engine executes client-side script via V8 in a compiled Rust binary, requiring a mere 30 megabytes of memory and resolving page snapshots in 4 to 5 milliseconds.6 The complete protocol is restricted to seven core methods: navigate, snapshot, click, type, scroll, select, and extract.6 The concrete token-saving benefits of this architecture are measured in real-world benchmarks run under WebTaskBench (utilizing Plasmate version 0.5.1 and evaluated via a tiktoken cl100k\_base GPT-4 tokenizer).4

Benchmark URL Document Category Original HTML Tokens Compiled SOM Tokens Compression Factor
cloud.google.com 4 SaaS & Cloud 861,000 6,000 133.8x
arstechnica.com 4 News & Media 138,000 1,000 106.7x
kubernetes.io/docs 4 Dev Tools & Docs 125,000 1,000 103.5x
stripe.com/docs 4 SaaS & Cloud 359,000 7,000 53.8x
typescriptlang.org 4 Dev Tools & Docs 103,000 4,000 23.4x
en.wikipedia.org 4 General Reference 188,000 28,000 6.8x
pypi.org 4 Minimalist Static 6,000 6,000 1.0x
crates.io 4 Minimalist Static 72 348 0.2x

The empirical results demonstrate a clear architectural trend: on visually dense, script-heavy SaaS portals, the removal of layout styling and analytics wrappers yields up to a 133.8x reduction in token overhead, dramatically reducing agent processing latency.4 On highly optimized, minimalist static sites, however, the structural overhead of the SOM JSON wrapper can occasionally exceed the footprint of the raw source HTML, indicating that the value of this primitive scales directly with the interactive and structural complexity of the target interface.4

Mechanics of Selective Hydration and WebMCP Integration

A central requirement of the proposed knowledge artifact is its ability to support selective hydration—allowing agents to ingest a lightweight, static visual representation of a page, and subsequently load and hydrate complex interactive operations only when a specific task demands execution.2 This concept is modeled after client-side web rendering optimizations.17 In modern development frameworks like React 19 and Next.js, static server-rendered HTML can be progressively hydrated on the client using asynchronous defer blocks.17 Developers gain granular control over this process by attaching execution parameters like hydrate on (such as loading on interaction or when the main browser thread becomes idle) or hydrate never to isolate static, non-interactive components.31 In an agentic environment, this selective hydration pattern is realized through the WebMCP API.2 WebMCP co-developed by engineers at Google and Microsoft and available in early previews in Chromium 146.0.7672.0 or higher, extends the Model Context Protocol directly into client-side browser runtimes.2 By exposing JavaScript-based tools directly through browser navigator interfaces, pages can act as local MCP servers, bypassing standard visual screen scraping.2 Web applications register these tools dynamically using the navigator.modelContext.registerTool(tool, options) method.33 To protect client execution environments, the browser executes a rigorous, ten-step validation sequence during registration:

│ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 1\. Activity Verification │ ──► Throws InvalidStateError if Document is inactive └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 2\. Permissions Policy Gate │ ──► Throws NotAllowedError if 'tools' policy is disabled └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 3\. Tool Map Retrieval │ ──► Resolves internal context tool register └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 4\. Name & Description Check │ ──► Enforces alphanumeric constraints (length 1–128) └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 5\. Schema Serialization │ ──► Runs JSON.stringify() validation on inputSchema └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 6\. Annotation Hint Mapping │ ──► Attaches readOnlyHint and untrustedContentHint └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 7\. Abort Signal Attachment │ ──► Registers automatic cleanup algorithms └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 8\. Trusted Origin Processing │ ──► Validates and parses URLs in the exposedTo array └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 9\. Definition Generation │ ──► Packages callback, schema, and security metadata └──────────────────────────────┘ │ ▼ ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │ 10\. Store & Notify │ ──► Commits tool to context and triggers change hooks └──────────────────────────────┘

By deploying this API, web applications can implement State-to-Tool Sync.2 For example, in a booking application like "AirBird," developers expose a series of core functional tools, such as search\_listings, get\_available\_listings, select\_listing, and reserve\_listing.2 Instead of requiring the agent to visually inspect search inputs, type into form fields, and click reserve buttons, the agent interacts directly with these client-side hooks.2 This imperative execution pattern reduces token consumption by 5x to 10x compared to traditional DOM scraping, while establishing a highly deterministic, cost-efficient transaction boundary.2

Multi-View Content Negotiation and Structured Discovery

For these semantic knowledge artifacts to scale globally, they must be discoverable and queryable using standardized web interfaces.7 A promising design pattern is the unified-URL profile, which serves multiple representations of the same resource depending on the requester.7 This strategy coordinates the agent's entry point, allowing both humans and machines to reference a single canonical URL while receiving optimized payloads.7

┌────────────────────────┐ │ Incoming GET /url │ └────────────────────────┘ │ ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────┐ ▼ ▼

│ │ Accept: text/html Accept: text/markdown │ │ ▼ ▼ ┌────────────────────────┐ ┌────────────────────────┐ │ Human Profile Page │ │ Clean Markdown Ingest │ │ (Visual layout, UI) │ │ (Hierarchical content) │ └────────────────────────┘ └────────────────────────┘ ▲ ▲ └─────────────────┬─────────────────┘ │ Query Parameters / Paths │ ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────┐ ▼ ▼ ?format=json /agent-card.json │ │ ▼ ▼ ┌────────────────────────┐ ┌────────────────────────┐ │ Structured JSON Data │ │ A2A Agent Card │ │ (Basic API endpoints) │ │ (Endpoints & Auth) │ └────────────────────────┘ └────────────────────────┘

This multi-view approach resolves the "discovery problem" by utilizing standard HTTP Content Negotiation.7 By requesting Accept: text/markdown, an LLM agent receives a highly clean, token-efficient Markdown version of the target content with standard front-matter.7 Alternatively, by appending /agent-card.json or invoking a "agent/getCard" JSON-RPC method, other systems can ingest an Agent-to-Agent (A2A) agent card.7 This machine-readable manifest explicitly outlines the agent's active endpoints, supported interfaces, skill tags, version profiles, and required authorization methods.7 This architectural pattern is supported by the W3C Content Negotiation by Profile specification.23 Operating as a functional profile of this standard, clients negotiate for representations that adhere to specific structural or semantic constraints—independent of the underlying media type—by transmitting custom Accept-Profile and Link rel="profile" headers.22 When servers implement both HTTP headers and URL Query String Arguments (QSAs) like \_profile and \_mediatype, the order of precedence is strictly defined: the QSA parameters override HTTP headers.24 This priority rule assumes that a query string represents a more explicit, targeted request by the user agent, ensuring predictable behavior across varying client applications.24

Foundations of Static Indexing and Verification

This dynamic content-negotiated layer is grounded in static indexing systems.26 Placing a standardized llms.txt and llms-full.txt file at a website's root has quickly become a standard pattern for AI-readiness, supported natively by platforms like Mintlify, Stripe, and Optimizely.26 While llms.txt acts as a curated overview of high-value pages, llms-full.txt provides the complete, concatenated markdown of the entire documentation site, allowing models to grasp the full structural layout in a single request.35 To automate the production of these indexes, enterprises deploy production-grade crawling tools.37 These tools execute intelligent breadth-first search (BFS) traversals over target sites, parse pages using BeautifulSoup and Playwright, optimize descriptions via models like Grok 4.1-Fast, and host the final, compliant files on global content delivery networks (CDNs) with scheduled auto-updates.37 To verify that these developer surfaces are truly agent-ready, teams run automated auditing tools.38 The Machine Agent Readiness Kit (MARK) operates as a deterministic, CI-friendly CLI that evaluates sites across four key dimensions: discovery (verifying robots.txt, llms.txt, and AGENTS.md), content structure (assessing markdown clean signals and JSON-LD), capabilities (auditing OpenAPI specs and MCP tools), and trust protocols (checking HTTPS, OAuth profiles, and crawler permission boundaries).39 A typical audit execution:

Bash npx mark-agent-readiness-kit https://docs.example.com \--profile docs \--fail-under 80

ensures that documentation boundaries remain optimized for agents, preventing regressions during continuous integration deployments.38

Cyber Security Vectors and Structural Vulnerabilities

Exposing structured semantic profiles and allowing agents to execute dynamic client-side tools introduces critical security surfaces.40 As agent systems scale, they rely heavily on application-layer API routers (such as LiteLLM or OpenRouter) to load balance requests, optimize API costs, and translate tool-calling structures across varying upstream model providers.40 Because these routing layers terminate and re-originate TLS connections, they operate with full plaintext access to API keys, system prompts, tool definitions, and tool-call responses.40

┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ │ AI Agent │ ──(Plaintext)─► │ Compromised │ ──(Plaintext)─► │ Upstream │ │ Client │ │ API Router │ │ Model (GPU) │ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ │ │ │ │ │ │ │ ◄───────────────────────────────│ │ │ (AC-1: Arbitrary Code Exec) │ │ │ │ │ │ │ ──(Exfiltrates Credentials)───► │ │ │ (AC-2: Passive Theft) │

This architectural vulnerability exposes agents to two primary classes of adversarial behavior:

  • Payload Injection (AC-1): A compromised router alters tool-call arguments or dependency names within dynamic install scripts.40 Since the client never sees the original upstream payload, and the modified arguments remain schema-valid, this attack can achieve silent, arbitrary code execution on client machines.40
  • Secret Exfiltration (AC-2): The router passively harvests plaintext credentials—such as AWS tokens, GitHub personal access tokens (PATs), or Slack bot keys—using basic regex matching.41 This passive collection is completely invisible to both the client and the upstream provider, and because the secrets are captured on the request path, traditional response-signing mechanisms are powerless to prevent it.41

To mitigate these supply-chain vulnerabilities, emerging semantic artifacts must enforce cryptographic end-to-end integrity.40 By cryptographically signing compiled SOM packages and WebMCP manifests at the origin server—relying on technologies like Signed Exchanges or Web Bundles—agent clients can verify that the visual maps and functional tools they discover have not been modified or injected by intermediate API routing layers.40

Credible Next Steps for the Industry

The analysis of the 2024 to May 2026 technical landscape indicates that the transition to an agentic web is limited more by fragmented, non-interoperable standards than by LLM reasoning capabilities.2 To establish a robust, universally supported, and selectively hydratable semantic agent artifact, the industry should focus on four key integration pathways:

Standardizing the W3C WebPageSemanticRepresentation Type

Websites must be able to advertise the availability of their agent-ready representations directly in standard HTML.3 The industry should align on the W3C Web Content Browser for AI Agents Community Group's Schema.org proposal, implementing the WebPageSemanticRepresentation type.3 Content management systems (CMS) and documentation engines (such as Mintlify and Optimizely) should automatically inject this JSON-LD metadata, pointing agents to compiled, deterministic SOM endpoints.3

Hardening WebMCP and Chromium Integration

Browser engines must establish secure boundaries for client-side tool execution.2 This requires graduating Chromium’s early WebMCP implementation from testing flags to a hardened, standard browser interface.2 Security controls must strictly enforce origin-based permissions via the exposedTo options, isolate the execution context of registered callbacks, and prevent intermediate proxy layers from capturing plaintext system prompts or credentials.33

Standardizing the Content-Negotiated Single-URL Identity

Web developers should adopt the single-URL pattern, serving human-readable HTML, clean markdown, and structured A2A agent cards from the same endpoint via HTTP headers and Query String Arguments.7 By standardizing on Accept: text/markdown and /agent-card.json paths, the web maintains a unified identity space, allowing human users and AI agents to discover resources at the same canonical address without custom discovery APIs.7

Automating CI-Driven Readiness Gates

To maintain quality across rapid software releases, development teams should integrate Machine Agent Readiness Kit (MARK) audits directly into their CI/CD pipelines.39 By treating agent discoverability, markdown clean flags, and OpenAPI/MCP metadata validation as hard build targets, organizations can prevent documentation regressions, ensuring that their public web assets remain navigable and executable by autonomous models.38
