Provenance: Claude (Sonnet) research document, 2025

HATEOAS, Hypermedia, and LLM Agents: Is Your Document-Manifest Concept Really HATEOAS?

TL;DR

  • HATEOAS is a partially correct frame for your concept, but it is not the cleanest one. Your design is closer to progressive disclosure (the architectural pattern Anthropic codified in Claude Agent Skills in October 2025 and that the MCP community is layering on top of the protocol) than to Fielding-style HATEOAS. The genuinely HATEOAS-shaped piece is the manifest entry's dependency edges and "what to fetch next" affordances; everything else (token cost, content hash, summary) is metadata about a static document, not state transitions in an application.
  • There is real, recent precedent for treating LLMs as hypermedia clients — most concretely in IETF draft-jimenez-t2trg-iot-agent-00 (Ericsson, April 14, 2026), Mike Amundsen's GRAIL project, and a Spring/Tanzu webinar arguing "MCP exposes capabilities, hypermedia guides behavior." But almost no mainstream LLM agent today (browser-use, WebAgent, MindAct, ReAct) reads IANA link relations, HAL, Siren, or Hydra semantically; they treat HTML as a flat DOM accessibility tree.
  • The niche of "single HTML artifact with a machine-readable section manifest for selective hydration" is mostly unbuilt for documents, but the protocol-level version is already crowded (llms.txt, Anthropic Skills' SKILL.md frontmatter, MCP progressive-disclosure proposals, mcpdoc). Your strongest novelty is the self-contained HTML carrier (manifest + sections in one file, addressable by fragment) plus per-section token cost and dependency edges — none of the existing standards do that exactly.

Key Findings

1. Fielding's HATEOAS argument and why it failed in mainstream APIs. In his October 20, 2008 blog post "REST APIs must be hypertext-driven," Roy Fielding listed six numbered rules. The most consequential is rule 6: "A REST API should be entered with no prior knowledge beyond the initial URI (bookmark) and set of standardized media types… From that point on, all application state transitions must be driven by client selection of server-provided choices that are present in the received representations." In a follow-up comment he defined hypertext as "the simultaneous presentation of information and controls such that the information becomes the affordance through which the user (or automaton) obtains choices and selects actions" — explicitly admitting machine clients ("automaton"). HATEOAS lost to JSON-RPC-style APIs for four reasons consistently surfaced across Carson Gross's htmx essay "How Did REST Come To Mean The Opposite of REST?", Ben Morris's "Pragmatic REST" critique, and Reda Hmeid's "The Trouble with HATEOAS": (a) no canonical hypermedia format won — HAL, Siren, JSON:API, Hydra, Collection+JSON, Mason all fragmented the market; (b) clients were almost never written generically — front-ends are hand-built against specific back-ends, so the runtime discovery story buys nothing; (c) tooling (Swagger/OpenAPI, Postman, code-generators) crystallized around static schemas; (d) most "RESTful" APIs are RPC dressed in HTTP verbs and never reach Richardson Maturity Model Level 3. Postman's 2025 State of the API Report — a survey of more than 5,700 developers, architects, and executives — states "REST still dominates at 93%" adoption, and contains no measurement of HATEOAS adoption as a separate category.

2. The htmx/Hotwire revival is philosophical, not just technical. Carson Gross's argument in Hypermedia Systems (2023, with Adam Stepinski, Deniz Akşimşek, foreword by Mike Amundsen) and in essays like "How Did REST Come To Mean The Opposite of REST?" and "Hypermedia-Driven Applications" is that HTML is the only widely-deployed natural hypermedia format and that JSON APIs are RPC in disguise. The Hypermedia-Driven Application (HDA) constraint set is explicit: HTML-embedded declarative interactivity (not imperative JS), and server-to-client communication in HTML (not JSON). DHH's Hotwire ("HTML Over The Wire," released by Basecamp Dec 2020) is the same philosophy with a different lineage — Turbo Drive, Turbo Frames, and Turbo Streams ship HTML fragments and let the document, not a client-side state store, carry application state. The wire-level difference between REST-over-HTTPS and true hypermedia: in REST-over-HTTPS the response is {"status":"OVERDRAWN"} and the client has out-of-band knowledge that "OVERDRAWN" means show-the-deposit-button; in hypermedia the response is the rendered HTML with only the deposit form present and the withdraw button absent. The state and the affordances arrive together.

3. LLM agents as HATEOAS clients: explicitly argued, but in a niche. The clearest primary source is IETF Internet-Draft draft-jimenez-t2trg-iot-agent-00, "Agentic AI Operation of Constrained RESTful Environments," authored by Jaime Jimenez (Ericsson) and submitted April 14, 2026, to the Thing-to-Thing Research Group. It states verbatim: "This approach applies the Hypermedia as the Engine of Application State (HATEOAS) principle to IoT: the agent needs no a priori knowledge of specific devices." The mechanism: CoAP devices expose RESTful interfaces with CoRE Link Format metadata (RFC 6690) and Semantic Definition Format (SDF) models; an LLM parses these, reasons about device capabilities, and constructs valid protocol interactions "much like a web browser navigates HTML pages using hyperlinks." Independently, Mike Amundsen's GRAIL (Goal-Resolution through Affordance-Informed Logic) — pitched at apidays Paris 2025 in the talk "Hypermed-AI: Where Intelligence Emerges from Connection" — argues that smart agents should be given a goal and an environment of in-situ affordances, not a workflow; the agent discovers procedure at runtime. Amundsen calls the OpenAI tool-calling description "effectively a hypermedia affordance." A Tanzu/VMware webinar (March 25, 2026) by Adib Saikali makes the explicit case: "the strongest agent architectures combine both MCP to expose capabilities, and hypermedia to guide and constrain agent behavior." But the contrast with mainstream agent practice is stark: browser-use (open-source MIT, 79k+ GitHub stars per its official docs), WebAgent (Gur et al. 2023), MindAct (Deng et al. 2023), WEPO (Liu et al. 2024), and ReAct-style agents do not read rel="next", rel="section", or HAL/Siren/Hydra. They read the DOM accessibility tree (or screenshots), extract clickable elements, and let the LLM pick one. The 2025 BrowserGym IPI-attack paper (arXiv:2507.14799) describes the standard pipeline: "scripts to compile prompts from user instructions and website HTML… parsing LLM responses… for specific language corresponding to computer-use actions, like 'click' or 'scroll.'" Link relations are essentially ignored.

4. Manifest schemas and link vocabularies that already exist. IANA's link-relations registry (per RFC 8288) contains, relevant to your concept: section, subsection, chapter, contents (table of contents), index, up, next, prev, start, related, describedby/describes (RFC 6892), canonical (RFC 6596), type, service-desc and service-meta for machine consumption (RFC 8631), cite-as, collection. HTML5 link types overlap. Schema.org has WebPageElement and its subtypes SiteNavigationElement, WPHeader, WPFooter, WPSideBar, Table; it has a WebContent umbrella type ("It is sometimes the case that detailed distinctions between Web pages, sites and their parts are not always important"). Schema.org does not have a first-class TableOfContents type — only SiteNavigationElement and ad-hoc mainContentOfPage/mainEntity properties; a real per-section manifest is not standardized. Hypermedia formats: HAL (Mike Kelly, 2011) only does links; Siren (Kevin Swiber, 2012) adds first-class actions and entity subentities; Hydra (W3C Hydra Community Group, JSON-LD-based) adds operations and machine-readable API documentation; JSON:API and Collection+JSON are also candidates. Antoine Cheron's survey concludes "Mason offers the most comprehensive expressiveness… HAL is the most commonly used." For per-section metadata, the W3C Web Annotation Data Model (Recommendation, 2017) is the most under-used candidate: it can carry a body (your summary, token cost, content hash, dependencies) attached to a target identified by FragmentSelector/CssSelector/XPathSelector — exactly your "addressable section." Annotations are RDF/JSON-LD-serializable. Sitemap XML is a useful analogy — flat list of URLs with <lastmod> and <priority> — but it has no summaries, no dependencies, no token cost, no section granularity, and no schema for affordances. llms.txt (Jeremy Howard, 2024) is the closest existing thing to your concept at the site level: a Markdown file with H1 title, blurb, sectioned Name: description lists, and an ## Optional section for skippable entries. It is informational, not a standard, and not yet honored by major LLM crawlers (Google's John Mueller confirmed on Bluesky). Anthropic's Agent Skills (released Oct 2025, formalized Dec 18, 2025) are the strongest existing implementation of progressive disclosure: SKILL.md with YAML frontmatter (name + description, median ~80 tokens per skill, ranging from ~55 [webapp-testing] to ~235 [xlsx] per newsletter.swirlai.com's measurement across Anthropic's 17 official skills), full body loaded only when relevant, and references to additional files loaded only on demand. Per paperclipped.de, by March 2026, 32 tools support the Agent Skills specification, including products from Anthropic (Claude Code), OpenAI (Codex CLI, ChatGPT), Microsoft (VS Code, GitHub Copilot), JetBrains (Junie), AWS (Kiro), and Block (Goose). The MCP ecosystem has parallel proposals: MCP-1st-Birthday/mcp-extension-progressive-disclosure (Michael Martin's hackathon submission), agentgateway's get_tool/invoke_tool pattern, and GitHub Issue #275 on dynamic tool selection — all motivated by the same problem (developer Scott Spence directly measured his MCP setup and found 66,000 tokens consumed at conversation start, with the GitHub MCP server alone using 55,000 tokens across its 93 tool definitions, per fastn.ai).

5. The honest assessment of the framing. HATEOAS as Fielding defined it is about application state transitions — what verbs/forms are valid given the resource's current state ("the deposit form is present because the account is overdrawn"). Your concept is about static document navigation with cost-aware lazy loading. The shared DNA is "the document tells the client what's available next, the client doesn't hardcode the URL space." But your manifest entries with token_cost, content_hash, and dependency_edges are not affordances in Fielding's sense — they're metadata for a retrieval planner. The closer analogies, in order of fit: (a) code-splitting manifests (Webpack/Vite/Turbopack chunk manifests with entrypoints, dependencies, hash-named chunks) — almost a perfect structural match; (b) HTTP/2 server push hints and <link rel="preload">/rel="modulepreload"> — same "tell the client what it will need" pattern; (c) service worker precache manifests (Workbox); (d) content negotiation + Vary headers; (e) Anthropic Skills' progressive disclosure (lightweight index + on-demand bodies); (f) then HATEOAS. Carson Gross's own caveat in "When Should You Use Hypermedia?" is relevant: hypermedia shines for "media-oriented" applications and CRUD, and gets wobbly when there are tight inter-region UI dependencies. A document with a section manifest is closer to a media-oriented application than to a stateful workflow, so calling it HATEOAS is defensible — but the framing buys you Fielding's authority more than it buys you architectural guidance.

Details

What Fielding actually said in 2008 (verbatim)

The six rules Fielding listed:

  1. Protocol-independence: "A REST API should not be dependent on any single communication protocol… Failure here implies that identification is not separated from interaction."
  2. Don't redefine the protocol: "A REST API should not contain any changes to the communication protocols… Failure here implies that the resource interfaces are object-specific, not generic."
  3. Spend descriptive effort on media types: "A REST API should spend almost all of its descriptive effort in defining the media type(s)… Failure here implies that out-of-band information is driving interaction instead of hypertext."
  4. No fixed URI structure: "A REST API must not define fixed resource names or hierarchies (an obvious coupling of client and server)… Failure here implies that clients are assuming a resource structure due to out-of-band information."
  5. No client-significant resource types: "A REST API should never have 'typed' resources that are significant to the client… The only types that are significant to a client are the current representation's media type and standardized relation names."
  6. Entry from URI + media types only: "A REST API should be entered with no prior knowledge beyond the initial URI (bookmark) and set of standardized media types… all application state transitions must be driven by client selection of server-provided choices that are present in the received representations."

Fielding's gloss on hypertext, in a comment reply: "When I say hypertext, I mean the simultaneous presentation of information and controls such that the information becomes the affordance through which the user (or automaton) obtains choices and selects actions… Hypertext does not need to be HTML on a browser. Machines can follow links when they understand the data format and relationship types." That last sentence is the entire intellectual foundation for the "LLM-as-HATEOAS-client" argument.

Why HATEOAS failed in mainstream REST

Across the surveyed sources the agreed causes are: (i) No standard hypermedia format won. HAL covers links but not actions; Siren adds actions but is less adopted; Hydra/JSON-LD is intellectually clean but heavyweight; JSON:API is closer to a serialization convention than a hypermedia format. Antoine Cheron's 2019 survey identifies 9 message formats and 7 frameworks — fragmentation is the diagnosis. (ii) No generic client emerged. Browsers are generic HTML clients; nothing equivalent exists for JSON+links. Ben Morris: "Given this diversity of implementation, there's no prospect of being able to create a generic REST client, like a web browser for data." (iii) OpenAPI/Swagger filled the documentation niche that HATEOAS was supposed to fill. (iv) Clients aren't generic. Front-ends are coupled to specific back-ends; mobile apps ship with hardcoded endpoint knowledge; clients won't dynamically discover. Andreas Reiser: "if you have a public facing API, you can be 100% certain that your clients won't use [HATEOAS]. This renders the whole concept of HATEOAS useless." (v) Versioning concerns. Header-based versioning and URL-versioning solved the practical decoupling problem that HATEOAS promised, without the overhead. Public counter-examples (PayPal, GitHub partial, AWS AppStream, Stormpath) exist but are exceptions.

The Richardson Maturity Model and Level 3

Leonard Richardson's model (popularized by Martin Fowler, 2010): Level 0 = HTTP-as-tunnel, Level 1 = resources, Level 2 = HTTP verbs, Level 3 = hypermedia controls. Fielding rejects the model as a definition of REST ("REST is not a maturity model"). Devopedia's survey notes Adobe, Netflix, parts of GitHub, PayPal, and "news aggregators and media companies like The Guardian and Wall Street Journal" claim Level 3 conformance, but most public APIs sit at Level 2.

The htmx / Hotwire / Hypermedia Systems philosophical core

Carson Gross's central claim ("How Did REST Come To Mean The Opposite of REST?"): the term REST was appropriated from its original web-architecture context into JSON-RPC API design, and the resulting "RESTful" APIs are functionally opposite to what Fielding defined because they fail the uniform-interface constraint. Gross is sympathetic to GraphQL — "GraphQL isn't REST, it doesn't claim to be REST, it doesn't want to be REST" — but insists JSON-with-links isn't either. The Hypermedia-Driven Application architecture constraint pair: declarative HTML-embedded interactivity, and HTML (not JSON) as the wire format. Hotwire's pitch (DHH, Dec 2020): "sending HTML instead of JSON over the wire." Both communities cite Fielding's section 5.1.5 (the trade-off between uniform interface and per-application efficiency) as licensing the choice.

Modern hypermedia + AI: the explicit arguments

The strongest explicit argument that LLM agents are the natural HATEOAS client comes from three sources:

  • IETF draft-jimenez-t2trg-iot-agent-00 (Ericsson, J. Jimenez, April 14, 2026, Thing-to-Thing Research Group). Verbatim: "This approach applies the Hypermedia as the Engine of Application State (HATEOAS) principle to IoT: the agent needs no a priori knowledge of specific devices." Mechanism: CoAP + CoRE Link Format (RFC 6690) + SDF + LLM-as-discovery-engine, with tools list_resources, get_resource, set_resource, observe_resource. Importantly, the draft explicitly raises prompt-injection via device metadata as a security concern — relevant to your concept if section summaries are author-provided.
  • Mike Amundsen's GRAIL (Goal-Resolution through Affordance-Informed Logic). Amundsen's substack: "GRAIL treats the world as the source of procedural knowledge. The agent's job is not to predict what should happen, but to ask the right question first and keep asking it as conditions change. 'Don't design workflows, discover them.'" Apidays Paris 2025 talk: "Hypermed-AI: Where intelligence Emerges from Connection." Frame: agents traverse runtime affordances rather than pre-baked workflows.
  • Nordic APIs and Tanzu/VMware advocacy. Adib Saikali's Tanzu webinar (March 25, 2026): "the strongest agent architectures combine both MCP to expose capabilities, and hypermedia to guide and constrain agent behavior — unlocking existing APIs for AI at enterprise scale." Towards Data Science article "Talk to my Agent": "When being consumed by LLMs, beyond serving functional purposes, [APIs] are, in essence, a reverse prompt… An ended interaction is a dead end, however, any data we return back to the agent gives it a chance to pull on another thread in its investigative process."

The disconnect with mainstream practice: WebAgent (Gur et al. 2023), MindAct (Deng et al. 2023), WEPO (Liu et al. 2024), browser-use (open-source MIT, 79k+ stars), and OpenAI's Operator all consume HTML as a flat tree of clickable elements. The DOM accessibility tree dominates; semantic link relations are ignored. No major LLM agent framework (LangChain, LlamaIndex, AutoGen, CrewAI, smolagents) ships with a Siren or HAL parser. Hypermedia-aware agents are an academic/conference idea, not a shipped pattern.

Manifest formats, evaluated for your use case

Format Section identity Summary Token cost Hash Dependency edges Affordances
IANA link relations (section, subsection, next, prev, contents) in HTML <link> ✓ ✗ ✗ ✗ partial (via next/prev/up) ✗
HAL ✓ ✗ ✗ ✗ links only links, no actions
Siren ✓ partial ✗ ✗ sub-entities ✓ first-class actions
Hydra / JSON-LD ✓ ✓ via schema.org ✗ ✗ ✓ via @type ✓ Operations
JSON:API ✓ ✗ ✗ ✗ relationships partial
Schema.org WebPageElement ✓ via cssSelector/xpath ✓ ✗ ✗ ✗ ✗
Sitemap XML flat URL list ✗ ✗ ✗ ✗ ✗
W3C Web Annotation Model ✓ via Selectors ✓ as body extensible extensible extensible extensible
llms.txt flat link list ✓ ✗ ✗ "## Optional" section ✗
Anthropic SKILL.md ✓ as files ✓ in frontmatter implicit filesystem "see FORMS.md" links scripts as tools
Code-splitting manifest (Webpack) ✓ chunk id ✗ ✓ (size) ✓ ✓ ✗

The closest cluster to your full requirement is Web Annotation Model + a custom JSON-LD vocabulary, or Hydra/JSON-LD with extension terms. Hydra explicitly was designed to let machines discover available actions on a resource — closer to Fielding's vision than any other JSON format. If you want HTML-native, the cleanest path is <link rel="contents"> to an inline <script type="application/ld+json"> block of Web Annotations whose targets are CSS selectors into the same document.

Where your concept genuinely diverges from HATEOAS

  1. Static content, not application state. Fielding's HATEOAS is about transitions ("now you can deposit, you cannot withdraw"). A reference-document manifest doesn't change with user state.
  2. Cost-aware retrieval. Token cost has no analogue in classical HATEOAS. The closest is HTTP Content-Length and Vary; the right intellectual lineage is resource hints (<link rel="preload">) and code-splitting manifests, not REST.
  3. Hashes for cache validation. This is ETag/Cache-Control: immutable territory, not HATEOAS.
  4. Dependency edges between sections. This is a directed acyclic dependency graph and maps to module graphs (ES modules, Webpack), not link relations. IANA's up and requires (the latter not registered for documents) get partway there.

Where it genuinely is HATEOAS

  1. Single entry point + discoverable internal structure. The agent gets a URL, reads the manifest, navigates. This is Fielding's rule 6.
  2. The document tells the client what's available, not vice versa. The agent doesn't pre-know #section-3.2 exists.
  3. Late binding. The agent commits no out-of-band knowledge of URL/fragment structure.

Adjacent concepts that are arguably closer than HATEOAS

  • Progressive disclosure (Anthropic Skills, MCP extensions, agentgateway). This is the term Anthropic chose and the term the agent community has adopted — it's the operational pattern your concept implements.
  • Code-splitting / chunk manifests (Webpack/Vite/Turbopack). The structural match — entry/chunk graph with hashes, size, and dependencies — is striking.
  • <link rel="preload"> and resource hints. Browser-side analogue of "agent, you will need this, plan accordingly."
  • Service worker precache manifests (Workbox precacheManifest).
  • JSON-LD Frames and SHACL for content-shape negotiation.
  • The Range header / byte-serving. HTTP already supports partial retrieval; what's missing is semantic partial retrieval indexed by manifest.

Recommendations

Stage 1 — Reframe (do this first, low cost). Replace "HATEOAS for LLM agents" with "progressive-disclosure document manifest" as the primary frame in the paper/spec. Cite HATEOAS as one of three lineages (the others: code-splitting manifests, Anthropic Skills' progressive disclosure). This reframe (a) avoids fighting twenty years of HATEOAS-failure literature; (b) lets you cite the IETF draft-jimenez and Amundsen GRAIL as supporting precedents without depending on them; (c) connects you to the active Skills/MCP discussion where the practitioner audience is.

Stage 2 — Pick a carrier format (decide within 2–4 weeks). Three viable options, in order of how much standards capital they buy:

  • Web Annotation Model + JSON-LD context with a custom vocabulary for tokenCost, contentHash, dependsOn. W3C Recommendation, supports CSS/XPath selectors into the same HTML document, RDF/JSON-LD-native. Highest standards weight; mildly heavy.
  • Hydra/JSON-LD with extension terms. Existing precedent for machine-readable affordances; less common in LLM tooling.
  • Custom JSON in <script type="application/manifest+llm"> with IANA <link rel="contents" href="#manifest"> pointing to it. Simplest; no standards backing.

Recommend: Web Annotation Model. Trigger to revisit: if a competing standard (likely an extension of llms.txt or an MCP "document resource" type) reaches ≥3 major vendors.

Stage 3 — Engage the right communities (within 1–2 months).

  • Submit to the IETF HTTPAPI WG or T2TRG (where draft-jimenez landed) — there is a documented appetite there.
  • Comment on the MCP progressive-disclosure discussion (modelcontextprotocol #631, #275). The MCP community is already inventing this and would benefit from a document-shaped formalization.
  • Open an issue on llms.txt; the proposal is intentionally open.

Stage 4 — Build the missing piece. No existing format combines per-section token cost + content hash + dependency edges. The unbuilt artifact is: a spec for what should go in each manifest entry, with reference encodings in (a) Web Annotation JSON-LD and (b) plain JSON-in-script for low-friction adoption. Ship a prototype hydration client (Python or TypeScript) that an LLM agent can call as a single tool: hydrate(url, sections=[...]) returning the requested fragments with citations. This is the niche genuinely empty: a document-level analogue of Anthropic Skills.

Benchmarks that would change the recommendation.

  • If Anthropic Skills ships an official document-shaped extension (likely 6–12 months), abandon the manifest format question and adopt theirs.
  • If llms.txt gets per-section token cost and dependency edges (unlikely in current spec), the niche closes.
  • If draft-jimenez-t2trg-iot-agent advances and adds a generic-document analogue, align with it directly.
  • If any of LangChain/LlamaIndex/AutoGen/CrewAI/smolagents ships a HAL/Siren/Hydra-aware retrieval plugin, the HATEOAS frame becomes defensible mainstream rather than niche, and you can lean harder into it.

Caveats

  • Provenance and dates. Several sources cited are forward-dated relative to the original Fielding/Richardson/Amundsen lineage (e.g., the Tanzu webinar is dated March 25, 2026; Postman 2025 State of the API; IETF draft April 14, 2026). These reflect the current date (May 20, 2026) and the active state of the field; cross-check against the linked primary sources before quoting in publication.
  • The htmx and Hotwire arguments are partisan. Carson Gross and DHH are advocates with skin in the game; their framing of "REST means the opposite of REST" is correct on Fielding's letter but oversimplifies the practical reasons JSON APIs won.
  • The "LLM as ideal HATEOAS client" argument is largely prospective. Outside the IETF draft and Amundsen's GRAIL, this is a conference-talk and blog-post idea, not deployed practice. Treat it as a research thesis, not a fait accompli.
  • Anthropic Skills' "progressive disclosure" terminology is contested — Phil Whittaker argues "progressive discovery" is more accurate. Either way, the pattern (lightweight index + lazy body + lazy references) is well-established. Use the Anthropic term for community recognition.
  • MCP progressive disclosure is not in the official spec (modelcontextprotocol.io as of 2025-11-25 spec). It is a design pattern layered on top by GitHub MCP Server, agentgateway, and the MCP-1st-Birthday extension. Treat as de-facto, not de-jure.
  • Browser-based agent literature is moving fast. The WebAgent/MindAct/WEPO snapshot is current as of late 2024 and early 2025; OpenAI Operator, Anthropic Computer Use, Manus, and Gemini Deep Research have all shipped since. None of them, as of the most recent public documentation, semantically parse IANA link relations or HAL — but this is the most likely place for the assumption to be wrong.
  • The Web Annotation Model is mature but lightly adopted. Hypothesis is the major deployment; outside scholarly annotation, it is rare. Choosing it for standards weight has a real adoption-risk cost.

Provenance: Gemini research document, 2025 — largest document in the corpus (~161 KB)

Context Economics and the Hyper-Knowledge Object (HKO)

Technical Report: Designing an Agent-Navigable, Selectively Hydratable Knowledge Primitive

Abstract

Modern autonomous agents operating over vast corporate repositories, technical documentations, and codebase graphs face a critical performance bottleneck: the physical and economic constraints of the Transformer attention window. Brute-force ingestion of documentation leads to high API costs, increased latency, and severe cognitive decay (e.g., the "lost in the middle" phenomenon).

This report evaluates the emerging paradigm of the Hyper-Knowledge Object (HKO)—a hybrid knowledge packaging format designed specifically to allow agents to discover, traverse, and selectively hydrate context without preloading entire documents. We formalize the taxonomy of the context window economy, review the limitations of current state-of-the-art mitigation strategies (including sitemaps, progressive tool calls, MCP, and AXTree parsing), perform a comparative evaluation of underlying technology substrates (XML, JSON-LD, BAML, CSS selectors, and HTTP Profile Negotiation), and lay out a concrete specification and roadmap for the HKO standard.

1\. Introduction: The Economics of the Context Window

While modern LLMs boast context limits spanning hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens, the operational economics of deploying autonomous agents do not scale linearly with context size. The attention mechanism within standard transformer-based architectures introduces mathematical and physical limits that make "context-stuffing" a highly inefficient default behavior.

1.1 The Mathematical and Computational Costs of Context

Let represent the input prompt sequence length (measured in tokens), and let represent the model's hidden dimension. The primary computational complexity of the standard self-attention mechanism (without sparse or linear approximations) scales quadratically:

For autoregressive generation, this quadratic scaling behaves differently in two distinct phases of inference:

  1. Prefill Phase: The entire input prompt of length is processed in parallel to compute the initial Key-Value (KV) cache. The computational cost is strictly .
  2. Decoding Phase: Each newly generated token must attend to all previous tokens stored in the KV cache. While the computation per decoding step is linear with respect to the current step, the overall memory footprint of the KV cache scales linearly with sequence length:

Where is the byte size per parameter (typically 2 bytes for FP16/BF16, or less under quantization). For a 70-billion-parameter model processing a token context, the KV cache alone can exceed of VRAM per user session. This massive memory overhead severely restricts batch sizes, increases Time-to-First-Token (TTFT), and degrades generation throughput.

1.2 Financial Economics of LLM APIs

For commercial API endpoints (e.g., OpenAI, Anthropic, Google), pricing structures are divided into input and output tokens. Let and represent the cost per million input and output tokens, respectively. The cost of a multi-turn agentic task over turns, where the agent appends history and context at each turn, is given by:

If an agent loads a large, monolithic \-token document into its context to perform a multi-step execution loop across 15 turns, the input tokens accumulate cumulatively:

Even if each response is only tokens, the total cost scales as input tokens. This brute-force pattern leads to unsustainable API bills, motivating the need for an infrastructure-level mechanism that exposes only the relevant subsections of documents when they are explicitly required.

1.3 Cognitive Attention Decay

Beyond financial and compute metrics, long context windows suffer from cognitive attention decay. The attention weight distribution from query token to key token is calculated as:

As , the denominator grows, diluting individual attention scores. Empirical studies on "lost in the middle" attention curves show that information placed near the center of a long context window experiences significantly lower recall rates than information placed near the beginning or the end.

To capture this mathematically, we define an attention decay function , where represents the normalized position of a target token within the window. The probability of an agent successfully retrieving a key fact is proportional to:

To optimize , we must keep the active execution context small, packing it only with highly dense, relevant facts (minimizing and shifting closer to or ).

2\. A Taxonomy of the Context Window Economy

To design an optimal system, we must establish a clear taxonomy of information layers. Current agents suffer because they do not distinguish between the metadata required to find a capability or resource, and the raw payload of the resource itself.

\+------------------------------------------------------------+ | PERSISTENT SUBSTRATE (Cold Storage: Complete Docs/DBs) | | Size: Infinite | \+------------------------------------------------------------+ │ (On-demand traversal) ▼ \+------------------------------------------------------------+ | DISCOVERY CONTEXT (Lightweight Map / Manifest) | | Size: \~10^2 tokens | \+------------------------------------------------------------+ │ (Match found) ▼ \+------------------------------------------------------------+ | ACTIVATION CONTEXT (Operational Guidelines / Rules) | | Size: \~10^2 \- 10^3 tokens | \+------------------------------------------------------------+ │ (Contextual hydration) ▼ \+------------------------------------------------------------+ | HYDRATED CONTEXT (Targeted Factual Snippets) | | Size: \~10^2 \- 10^4 tokens | \+------------------------------------------------------------+ │ (Active execution loop) ▼ \+------------------------------------------------------------+ | EXECUTION CONTEXT (Working Memory / Prompts / Chat) | | Size: \~10^3 \- 10^5 tokens | \+------------------------------------------------------------+

2.1 The Five Layers of the Context Window

  • Discovery Context: The metadata layer exposed to the agent at initialization to signal the existence, relevance, and semantic boundaries of capabilities or information. It is designed to consume minimal tokens (e.g., sitemap indexes, YAML frontmatter, tool headers).
  • Activation Context: The operational policy, constraints, and rule-based boundaries loaded when a specific tool, directory, or workflow is triggered (e.g., AGENTS.md rules, localized system prompts, style guides).
  • Execution Context: The active, stateful working memory of the conversation, including the immediate prompt, conversation history, and latest tool outputs.
  • Hydrated Context: On-demand, high-density knowledge fragments programmatically pulled into the active attention window from the persistent substrate only when the execution path requires them.
  • Persistent Substrate: The cold storage layer containing the complete, un-hydrated body of documentation, files, scripts, and databases. This layer exists on-disk, in a DB, or on a remote network, incurring zero token overhead until accessed.
Taxonomy Layer Average Token Footprint Primary System Function Representative Real-World Artifact
Discovery Context **** tokens per registry entry Low-cost signal matching to establish capability/domain relevance. llms.txt, YAML frontmatter of SKILL.md, MCP tool list.
Activation Context **** tokens Loads core heuristics, runtimes, security limits, and execution rules. AGENTS.md root, main SKILL.md instruction blocks.
Execution Context **** tokens Stateful session tracking and immediate prompt-response sequence. Active chat history, system prompt, user queries.
Hydrated Context **** tokens Highly focused, on-demand factual payload injected to resolve a localized task. Pruned DOM subtrees, target markdown pages, target paragraphs.
Persistent Substrate Infinite (Disk/Cloud Storage) Structural vault of raw documents, databases, and executable assets. Local Markdown repositories, Git monorepos, document DBs.

3\. Empirical Evaluation of SOTA Mitigation Strategies

To contextually position the proposed Hyper-Knowledge Object, we must analyze how contemporary systems attempt to manage the token-cost-performance trade-offs of the context window.

3.1 Repository and Sitemap-Style Discovery

  • AGENTS.md: This emerging standard, governed by the Linux Foundation's Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF), serves as an operational policy and instruction set for coding runtimes. It lists exact development commands, styling rules, and security boundaries. While it supports hierarchical directory scoping to override rules locally, AGENTS.md is fundamentally a static command directory and style guide. It is not designed to organize, query, or selectively hydrate structured, domain-specific factual knowledge.
  • llms.txt: This specification mirrors the concept of a search engine sitemap, serving a plain Markdown file at predictable endpoints (e.g., /llms.txt or .well-known/llms.txt). It lists high-level documentation structures, linking each page title to a clean Markdown alternative (.md) to bypass heavy HTML processing. However, llms.txt provides only a flat summary index. To consume the linked documentation, the agent must download the entire targeted markdown file, which often leads to the brute-force, context-stuffed approach represented by consolidated llms-full.txt files.

3.2 Capability-Oriented Progressive Disclosure

  • Agent Skills (SKILL.md): This framework implements a directory-based progressive disclosure model designed to keep agent context focused. At startup, the host pre-loads only the metadata name and description from the YAML frontmatter of each SKILL.md file, costing approximately 100 tokens per skill. When a task matches this metadata, the agent uses bash commands to read the core markdown instructions of SKILL.md into active context. Detailed references, schemas, and executable scripts are left on the filesystem, accessed on-demand only when referenced during execution. Crucially, when executing internal utility scripts, the script code itself never enters the context window; only the standard output is captured, preserving valuable token space. While highly efficient, this progressive disclosure model is architecturally bound to executable capabilities and procedural workflows rather than declarative knowledge bases.

3.3 Karpathy's LLM Wiki Paradigm

  • LLM Wiki: This workflow compiles unstructured, fragmented knowledge sources into a tightly structured, interlinked directory of plain Markdown files. This approach enforces a consistent internal template across files, typically requiring a title, summary line, topic tags, and cross-reference links using Obsidian-style syntax. AI coding agents can then navigate the directory locally to answer queries. In small-scale setups containing fewer than 200 curated sources, this paradigm achieves a token footprint reduction of up to 95% compared to naive document loading, as the high semantic density of the summaries provides strong relevance signals.
  • Vulnerability: However, the LLM Wiki exhibits a fatal architectural vulnerability: the compounding hallucination problem. If an agent introduces a minor semantic distortion or factual error during the ingestion and summarization of a new source, that error is permanently compiled into the active markdown wiki. As new pages are added, the model cross-references and links to the corrupted node, propagating the hallucination structurally through the network. Unlike traditional vector-based RAG—where a corrupted chunk is isolated and can be corrected by re-indexing the source document—correcting a structural error in an LLM Wiki requires continuous, expensive linting and manual human-in-the-loop verification of the entire compiled directory.

3.4 MCP Context Inflation and Tool Description Smells

  • The Model Context Protocol (MCP): MCP standardizes how agents interact with external tools and data sources. However, naive MCP implementations introduce massive context inflation by loading complete tool names, descriptions, and complex JSON schemas directly into the system prompt. This challenge is compounded by the poor quality of tool descriptions in the wild. Empirical reviews of 856 tools across 103 MCP servers reveal that 97.1% of active tool descriptions exhibit "description smells."
  • Description Smells: The most prevalent smells include unstated limitations (affecting 89.8% of tools), missing purpose definitions (56%), and duplicated display names (73%) across different tools within a single server. These defects lead directly to model misselection. While programmatically augmenting descriptions to resolve these smells improves overall task success by a median of 5.85 percentage points, it inflates the average number of execution steps by 67.46%, illustrating that simply expanding descriptions drives up token consumption and operational costs.
  • Mitigation Patterns: Two primary architectural patterns have emerged to mitigate this cost:
  1. Progressive Tool Discovery: Rather than loading all tool definitions upfront, the host exposes a lightweight meta-tool (such as search\_tools). The model queries this tool to receive a list of matching tool names and brief summaries (Layer 1: Catalog), invokes get\_tool\_details to inspect the full JSON schema of only the target candidates (Layer 2: Inspect), and finally executes the selected tool (Layer 3: Execute).
  2. Programmatic Tool Calling (Code Mode): Instead of making sequential round trips through the model's context for multi-tool chains, the model writes an executive script (e.g., using a sandboxed runtime like Deno, Monty, or Wasmtime). The script executes multiple tool calls locally within the sandbox, intercepting intermediate data streams. The broker routes data directly between servers, returning only a final summary line to the model's primary context window.

3.5 HTML RAG and Accessibility Tree (AXTree) Representations

  • Web Browsing Noise: In web-browsing agent frameworks, raw HTML documents introduce significant semantic noise. Converting HTML to markdown at the edge yields an average token reduction of 80% to 95%, dropping a typical page from over 16,000 tokens to approximately 3,100 tokens. Alternatively, leveraging the browser’s Accessibility Tree (AXTree) via Playwright or similar engines exposes only interactive elements with labeled roles, states, and relationships, stripping away layout divs and styles. This transition reduces downstream scraping costs to approximately $0.0002 per page.
  • Accessibility Overloading Contention: However, a fierce architectural debate exists regarding the overloading of accessibility features for bot navigation. Utilizing the AXTree as an agent interface risks creating an incentive for developers to optimize accessibility markers specifically for machine efficiency rather than human usability. Additionally, if web administrators deploy defensive ARIA patterns to trap or block bots, they risk blocking human screen-reader users in the process, creating a fragile alignment between accessibility technology and automated scraping.

3.6 Document Indexing Without Vector Search

  • PageIndex/Vectorless RAG: These architectures replace semantic vector similarity search, which frequently suffers from chunk fragmentation and loss of structural context. Instead of embedding flat chunks of text, these systems parse documents into hierarchical, structured trees representing chapters, sections, and paragraphs, preserving their natural semantic and logical relationships.
  • Logical Tree Traversal: At query time, the model executes a logical tree traversal. The system feeds the model the root-level summaries and immediate child node definitions. The model reasons over this high-level table of contents, prunes irrelevant branches, and incrementally descends to the target leaf node. This model provides a highly precise, auditable retrieval trace. However, vectorless RAG is currently implemented as custom, system-side database-and-orchestrator pipelines rather than a standardized, portable packaging format.

3.7 Comparative Benchmarking of Agent Interfaces

The ACM Web Conference 2026 technical report comparing Model Context Protocol (MCP), Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), Natural Language Web (NLWeb), and traditional HTML browsing highlights the performance trade-offs inherent in context design.

Interface Paradigm Target Language Model Task Completion Rate (CR) F1-Score Average Prompt Tokens Average Cost per Task Average Runtime
AX+MEM (HTML) GPT-4.1
AX+MEM (HTML) GPT-5
AX+MEM (HTML) GPT-5-mini
AX+MEM (HTML) Claude Sonnet 4
RAG Agent GPT-4.1
RAG Agent GPT-5
RAG Agent GPT-5-mini
RAG Agent Claude Sonnet 4
API (MCP) GPT-4.1
API (MCP) GPT-5
API (MCP) GPT-5-mini
API (MCP) Claude Sonnet 4
NLWeb GPT-4.1
NLWeb GPT-5
NLWeb GPT-5-mini
NLWeb Claude Sonnet 4

These performance dynamics reveal that RAG-based extraction and standardized natural-language interfaces (like NLWeb, which outputs Schema.org-compliant JSON) achieve higher task completion rates while reducing token consumption by a factor of two to five compared to traditional browser-based HTML execution.

4\. Structural Analysis of Potential Substrate Technologies

To select the most robust substrate for the proposed Hyper-Knowledge Object, we must analyze the strengths and weaknesses of adjacent technical standardizations.

4.1 XML and Structured Authoring (DITA)

The Darwin Information Typing Architecture (DITA) XML standard provides a mature foundation for modular, topic-based structured authoring. In DITA, content is developed as discrete, self-contained "topics" that address a single question or concept. These topics are organized into output deliverables using a "DITA map," which defines the structural relationship and hierarchy of the content.

DITA natively supports conditional processing (profiling). Authors attach metadata attributes (such as audience, platform, or custom properties) directly to structural elements within a topic. At processing time, a separate filtering profile—defined in a .ditaval file—tells the engine which elements to include, exclude, or flag. This permits a single master file to render highly specialized versions of documentation without duplicating the core content.

  • Trade-off: XML structures are highly verbose, causing substantial token bloat. Additionally, parsing XML is computationally expensive and less natively aligned with modern agent tools compared to JSON/HTML.

4.2 EPUB and Publication Manifest Standards

The W3C Publication Manifest standardizes how a collection of discrete digital resources (HTML, audio, images, scripts) is organized into a single cohesive, packaged work (such as an ebook or audiobook). Serialized using JSON-LD and Schema.org vocabularies, the manifest explicitly defines:

  • The bounds of the publication: An exhaustive resource list detailing every asset required to construct the work, ensuring nothing from the external web is implicitly assumed.
  • The default reading order: A linear sequence (the readingOrder array, analogous to the EPUB "spine") indicating how a user agent should step through primary text assets.
  • Structural landmarks: Links pointing to key meta-resources such as the table of contents, cover image, or index.

The manifest is designed to be parsed into a canonical internal data structure, enabling user agents to navigate, cache, and render modular packages with strict integrity and zero ambiguity.

  • Trade-off: The ZIP-based packaging requirements of EPUB/LPF add significant serialization, compression, and disk-read latency, making it less than ideal for real-time web agents.

4.3 JSON-LD, RDFa, and Microdata

Linked data formats serialize semantic meaning directly alongside structural content. JSON-LD excels at establishing explicit child-parent relationships, global identifiers, and structured metadata schemas (using vocabularies like Schema.org). It can be hosted as a lightweight sidecar file to maintain a clean separation of concerns, or embedded directly within HTML as structured blocks. RDFa and Microdata embed these semantic properties directly into HTML markup as attributes.

  • Trade-off: While Microdata is highly human-readable because it resides within the presentation layer, it creates a high parsing burden on models, as the model must traverse the entire DOM to extract the semantic graph. JSON-LD hosted as a sidecar resolves this by delivering a dense, machine-readable map of the document that can be parsed independently of the human-readable HTML representation.

4.4 CSS and Selector-Based Target Extraction

Using CSS selectors as an extraction layer provides a highly precise, standardized mechanism for navigating document hierarchies. Instead of relying on fragile keyword searches or expensive semantic embeddings, an agent can execute a simple selector query (e.g., article\#mfa-requirements \> section\#crypto) to isolate a target node within a DOM tree. CSS rules cascade naturally, allowing styles and structural constraints to propagate from parent to child elements.

  • Trade-off: CSS selectors are highly dependent on DOM stability. If a developer changes a class name or alters the nesting hierarchy of the HTML substrate, the selector breaks. To mitigate this, selector targets must be bound to immutable semantic IDs or explicit data attributes (e.g., data-node-id) rather than fragile layout classes.

4.5 BAML and Schema-Aligned Parsing Contracts

The Boundary AI Markup Language (BAML) introduces a type-safe, domain-specific programming language for structuring LLM inputs and outputs. BAML replaces verbose standard JSON schemas with compressed, TypeScript-like type definitions, achieving a token count reduction of up to .

BAML's primary innovation is Schema-Aligned Parsing (SAP), a high-performance, Rust-based engine that automatically parses, coerces, and repairs malformed model responses in under 10 milliseconds. SAP resolves missing quotes, escaped characters, mismatched keys, and unquoted strings. Furthermore, during active streaming operations, SAP can dynamically parse incomplete chunks as partial arrays or objects, allowing applications to consume structured data in real time.

  • Trade-off: While excellent for client-to-model parsing contracts, BAML itself is not designed to be a standardized, distributed document storage format.

4.6 HTTP and Protocol-Level Profile Negotiation

Under RFC 6906, a web link can declare its conformance to a "profile"—a client-centric interaction definition that establishes semantic and structural constraints on a resource representation, extending beyond the media type.

The W3C Content Negotiation by Profile specification standardizes how clients programmatically negotiate for representations conforming to these profiles. A client sets headers such as Accept-Profile pointing to the desired profile's URI, and the server returns a matching representation of the resource. Alternatively, in query-based environments where headers are restricted, a standardized query parameter (such as \_profile) is utilized. This protocol-level approach allows a single URI endpoint to deliver different structural shapes of a document (e.g., a lightweight semantic tree index versus a fully detailed textual layout) depending on the caller’s capabilities and requirements.

5\. The Hyper-Knowledge Object (HKO) Specification

To integrate the best elements of these adjacent technologies, we propose the Hyper-Knowledge Object (HKO). The HKO is a standardized, self-describing, and portable knowledge packaging format that decouples machine-navigable discovery from human-friendly presentation while enabling protocol-level selective hydration.

5.1 Architecture Overview

An HKO is distributed as a single logical resource containing two symmetrical, tightly coupled representations:

  1. document.html (Presentation Substrate): A valid HTML5 document containing the human-readable text, structured using native semantic elements and immutable selector IDs.
  2. manifest.jsonld (Discovery Manifest): A lightweight JSON-LD sidecar detailing the document's hierarchy, nodes, target selectors, and high-quality, smell-free summaries.

\+-------------------------------------------------------------+

Hyper-Knowledge Object
\+--------------------------+ \+------------------------+
manifest.jsonld document.html
\[Discovery Context\] \[Presentation Layer\]
\- Node Hierarchies \- HTML5 Markup
\- Target CSS Selectors ◄─┼─ \- Semantic IDs
\- Smell-free Summaries \- Full Factual Body
\+--------------------------+ \+------------------------+

\+-------------------------------------------------------------+

5.2 Symmetrical Manifest & Document Implementations

The manifest.jsonld (Machine-Readable Sidecar):

The manifest represents the HKO’s Discovery Context. The agent reads this file first. It costs negligible tokens and exposes a complete map of the document's interior.

{ "@context": \ "\[https://schema.org\", "\https://openagents.org/contexts/hko\" \], "@type": "HyperKnowledgeObject", "identifier": "urn:uuid:e9c2b1a3-4d5e-6f7a-8b9c-0d1e2f3a4b5c", "name": "Enterprise PostgreSQL Replication Runbook", "description": "Factual operational steps, boundaries, and validation criteria for setting up high-availability Postgres clusters.", "hasPart": \[ { "@id": "\#sec-1", "@type": "KnowledgeNode", "headline": "1. Multi-Factor Authentication Requirements", "summary": "Mandatory crypto standards and key configurations for configuring multi-factor DBA access.", "selector": "article\#mfa-requirements" }, { "@id": "\#sec-1.1", "@type": "KnowledgeNode", "headline": "1.1 Cryptographic Algorithms", "summary": "Approved symmetric and asymmetric algorithms (AES-256-GCM, RSA-4096) for database encryption keys.", "selector": "section\#mfa-crypto" }, { "@id": "\#sec-2", "@type": "KnowledgeNode", "headline": "2. High-Availability Replication Topologies", "summary": "Primary-replica configurations, streaming replication variables, and automated failover scripts.", "selector": "article\#replication-topologies" } \] }

The document.html (Human-Readable Presentation Substrate):

The document substrate contains the full factual data. It is constructed so that every section has a direct, matching ID corresponding to the CSS selector declared in the machine-readable manifest.

\<\!DOCTYPE html\> \<html lang="en"\> \<head\> \<meta charset="UTF-8"\> \<title\>Enterprise PostgreSQL Replication Runbook\</title\> \<link rel="alternate" type="application/ld+json" href="manifest.jsonld" title="HKO Manifest"\> \<style\> body { font-family: sans-serif; max-width: 800px; margin: 40px auto; padding: 0 20px; color: \#333; } article, section { border-bottom: 1px solid \#eee; padding: 20px 0; } h1 { color: \#111; } h2 { color: \#444; } \</style\> \</head\> \<body\> \<nav id="toc"\> \<h2\>Table of Contents\</h2\> \<ul\> \<li\>\<a href="\#mfa-requirements"\>1. Multi-Factor Authentication Requirements\</a\>\</li\> \<li\>\<a href="\#replication-topologies"\>2. High-Availability Replication Topologies\</a\>\</li\> \</ul\> \</nav\>

\<article id="mfa-requirements" data-node-id="\#sec-1"\> \<h1\>1. Multi-Factor Authentication Requirements\</h1\> \<p\>All administrative access to the database migration system must enforce multi-factor authentication. Direct password logins are disabled in pg\_hba.conf.\</p\>

\<section id="mfa-crypto" data-node-id="\#sec-1.1"\> \<h2\>1.1 Cryptographic Algorithms\</h2\> \<p\>Approved algorithms are restricted to AES-256-GCM for data at rest, and RSA-4096 or ECDSA (SHA-384) for TLS connection handshakes. Do not use legacy blowfish or md5 hashes.\</p\> \</section\> \</article\>

\<article id="replication-topologies" data-node-id="\#sec-2"\> \<h1\>2. High-Availability Replication Topologies\</h1\> \<p\>To establish primary-replica node replication, configure physical replication slots and execute the pg\_basebackup utility on the standby host:\</p\> \<pre\>\<code\>pg\_basebackup \-h primary\_ip \-D /var/lib/postgresql/data \-U replicator \-P \-R\</code\>\</pre\> \</article\> \</body\> \</html\>

6\. Access and Selective Hydration Mechanics

An agent can interact with and selectively hydrate the HKO in both remote web environments and local filesystems.

6.1 Protocol-Level Web Hydration (HTTP)

When hosted on an enterprise knowledge server, the HKO endpoint supports Content Negotiation by Profile (RFC 6906 / W3C specifications).

AGENT CLIENT ENTERPRISE SERVER │ │ ├─── (1) GET /docs/runbook.kno ──────────────────►│ │ Accept: application/ld+json │ │ │ │◄── (2) 200 OK (Returns manifest.jsonld) ────────┤ │ \[Sitemap & Node summaries mapped\] │ │ │ │ \[Agent evaluates target node: \#sec-1.1\] │ │ │ ├─── (3) GET /docs/runbook.kno ──────────────────►│ │ Accept: text/html │ │ Accept-Profile: \<.../hko-node\>; id="\#sec-1.1"│ │ │ │◄── (4) 200 OK (Returns Pruned HTML Subtree) ────┤ │ \[Only section\#mfa-crypto loaded to context\] │ ▼ ▼

Step 1: Discover Document Structure

The agent initiates a request specifically negotiating for the machine-readable linked sitemap (the discovery manifest):

GET /documents/runbook.kno HTTP/1.1 Host: api.enterprise.org Accept: application/ld+json

The server returns the manifest.jsonld payload, costing the agent only tokens of discovery context.

Step 2: Request Selective Hydration of Target Node

Once the agent analyzes the summaries and identifies that the cryptographic specification is contained in node \#sec-1.1, it requests only that specific section. It negotiates for the HKO Node profile:

GET /documents/runbook.kno HTTP/1.1 Host: api.enterprise.org Accept: text/html Accept-Profile: \https://openagents.org/profiles/hko-node\; id="\#sec-1.1"

The server parses the request, matches the ID \#sec-1.1 to the manifest's selector section\#mfa-crypto, extracts that pruned HTML element from the raw presentation file, and returns only that subtree:

HTTP/1.1 200 OK Content-Type: text/html; profile="\https://openagents.org/profiles/hko-node\"

\<section id="mfa-crypto" data-node-id="\#sec-1.1"\> \<h2\>1.1 Cryptographic Algorithms\</h2\> \<p\>Approved algorithms are restricted to AES-256-GCM for data at rest, and RSA-4096 or ECDSA (SHA-384) for TLS connection handshakes. Do not use legacy blowfish or md5 hashes.\</p\> \</section\>

Result: The agent processes fewer than 100 tokens to get the precise, context-isolated fact it needed, bypassing the entire rest of the runbook.

6.2 Local SDK Execution

For filesystems where an agent is executing locally (e.g., in a Workspace IDE like Cursor or VS Code), a lightweight runtime SDK manages hydration transparently:

import { loadHKO } from 'hko-runtime';

// 1\. Instantly load the lightweight manifest (Discovery Context) const runbook \= await loadHKO('./runbook.kno');

// 2\. Locate the node ID associated with 'Cryptographic' using semantic or matching keys const targetNode \= runbook.manifest.hasPart.find( node \=\> node.headline.includes('Cryptographic') || node.summary.includes('AES') );

if (targetNode) { // 3\. Selective Hydration: Under the hood, the SDK parses document.html // and extracts the target element using targetNode.selector const hydratedHTML \= await targetNode.hydrate();

// Inject exactly this HTML node into the active agent context agent.injectContext(hydratedHTML); }

7\. Design Heuristics and Key Anti-Patterns

To prevent the HKO standard from degrading into yet another verbose, fragile web interface, implementation must adhere to strict architectural constraints.

7.1 Design Heuristics

  • Enforce Symmetrical Synchronization: High-performance linter chains must be run as pre-commit hooks. If a developer edits document.html and changes an element's ID, the compiler must automatically update the corresponding selector in manifest.jsonld.
  • Use Immutable Semantic Target Elements: Never bind manifest selectors to fragile, presentation-dependent class strings (e.g., div.flex.item-center.p-4.text-sm). Target selectors must always bind to immutable, semantic IDs or structural data attributes (e.g., section\#mfa-crypto or \[data-node-id="\#sec-1.1"\]).
  • Decouple Capability from Fact: An HKO must contain only declarative facts and knowledge. It should not package executable utility scripts, API tool descriptions, or active workflows—which are natively governed by MCP servers and SKILL.md frameworks.
  • Optimize for Prefix Caching: Modern LLM providers support Prompt Caching (where input sequences that match previously processed prompts are cached at a fraction of the cost). To preserve this, selectively hydrated HKO content must be appended linearly to the end of the conversation prompt, rather than dynamically re-ordered, which would invalidate the prefix cache.

7.2 Core Architectural Anti-Patterns to Avoid

❌ ANTI-PATTERN: The Flat Context Dump Agent ──► Loads complete 100k-token manual ──► Exponential self-attention cost ($O(N^2)$) \+ "Lost in the Middle" decay

✔️ HKO PATTERN: Selective Node Hydration Agent ──► Parses 200-token manifest sitemap ──► Selectively hydrates 100-token target node ──► Ultra-high recall \+ minimal cost

  • The Flat Context Dump: Ingesting raw, non-indexed PDFs or Markdown bodies directly into the execution prompt. This wastes computational resources, inflates response times, and decreases task success rates.
  • Dynamic Selector Generation: Forcing agents to parse raw HTML pages and construct CSS/XPath selectors at runtime to fetch data dynamically. This results in high selector breakage rates and unpredictable navigation behaviors.
  • Ingest-Time Hallucination Propagation: Allowing agents to summarize and write arbitrary text updates into a central Markdown LLM Wiki without programmatic verification or schema boundaries. Over time, slight summarization errors propagate, structurally corrupting the knowledge node graph.
  • Bypassing the Human Accessibility Layer: Creating parallel, machine-only "shadow APIs" that discard HTML completely. This practice removes human auditability and detaches the agentic path from the design constraints of modern visual layouts and accessibility trees.

8\. Strategic Standardisation Roadmap

For the Hyper-Knowledge Object to achieve wide adoption across the developer ecosystem, its rollout must be executed systematically.

PHASE 1 (Q3 2026\) PHASE 2 (Q4 2026\) PHASE 3 (Q1 2027\) PHASE 4 (Q2 2027\) AAIF Schema SAP Runtime Framework Middleware Empirical Benchmarks Standardization ───► Parsers (Rust) ───► (FastAPI/Spring) ───► vs. Traditional RAG

  1. Phase 1: AAIF Schema Standardization (Q3 2026\)

Submit the JSON-LD schema definitions for HyperKnowledgeObject and KnowledgeNode to the Linux Foundation’s Agentic AI Foundation (AAIF) for open-source standardization. Coordinate with authors of AGENTS.md and the Model Context Protocol (MCP) to ensure smooth layer interoperability.

  1. Phase 2: High-Performance Runtime Parser (Q4 2026\)

Build lightweight, open-source SDK runtimes in Python, Rust, and TypeScript. Implement BAML-style Schema-Aligned Parsing (SAP) to ensure any data extracted or parsed by agents is instantly coerced, repaired, and validated under 10 milliseconds.

  1. Phase 3: Backend Framework Middleware (Q1 2027\)

Develop off-the-shelf middleware packages for popular web frameworks (e.g., FastAPI, Express, Spring AI, Next.js). This middleware must intercept incoming HTTP requests, parse RFC 6906 Accept-Profile headers, and automatically slice raw HTML templates before returning the payload.

  1. Phase 4: Comparative Benchmarking (Q2 2027\)

Deploy the HKO standard across open-source agent evaluation systems (such as SWE-bench and WebArena). Quantify the exact impact on Task Completion Rates (CR), F1-scores, Prompt Token Footprints, and monetary execution costs across diverse LLMs (including Claude Sonnet, GPT-5, and open-source models).

By standardizing a human-readable, machine-navigable, and selectively hydratable knowledge primitive, we can build an agentic web that scales information volume without collapsing under the computational weight of the attention window.
