Provenance: Claude (Sonnet) research document, 2025

Is <script type="application/ld+json"> the Right Carrier for an In-Document Agent Manifest?

TL;DR

  • Technically yes, architecturally probably no. The HTML spec, the JSON-LD 1.1 spec (§7), and the IANA media-type registry all permit exactly the pattern you describe — a custom-@context JSON-LD block carrying section IDs, summaries, token costs, hashes, and dependency edges — and there is direct structural prior art (RO-Crate, YAML-LD). But the agent-facing "niche" is being filled in parallel by out-of-document mechanisms (llms.txt, WebMCP/navigator.modelContext, MCP Resources, Claude Skills, NLWeb, AIPREF), and no agent runtime in production today routinely parses arbitrary ld+json blocks for navigation hints.
  • A new MIME is aesthetic, not necessary. application/ld+json;profile="https://example.com/agent-manifest" (RFC 6906) is the spec-clean answer. A bespoke application/agent-manifest+ld+json is also legal under the JSON-LD WG's +ld+json resolution (Resolution #4, 2019-11-01 minutes) but buys nothing semantically. Inventing application/agent-manifest+json without the +ld+json suffix would actively lose* you the JSON-LD processor compatibility that justifies using the slot in the first place.
  • The strongest contrarian read: you're picking a slot that's structurally fine but ecosystically empty. SEO-grade JSON-LD is consumed by Google/Bing/Yandex with vocabulary expectations; LLM agents are coalescing around browser APIs (WebMCP) and out-of-band manifests (llms.txt, MCP). Shipping an in-head agent manifest only pays off if you also ship the agent (or an adapter). If you do ship it, do it as JSON-LD with a custom @context and a profile parameter — and assume you are inventing a private protocol, not joining a public one.

Key Findings

1. JSON-LD is the dominant structured-data carrier on the web, but its consumers are search engines, not agents

  • Scale: The WebDataCommons October 2024 release found structured data on 51.25% of crawled webpages, up from 5.7% in 2010. Per their published report: "JSON-LD is the most widely adopted markup format for structured data annotation, used by 70% of websites that annotate structured data" (Microdata 46%, RDFa 3%, Microformats 23%). The October 2024 corpus contains 74 billion RDF quads from 16.5 million websites.
  • Triples per page: JSON-LD averages 57 triples per webpage in 2024 (up from 10 in 2015) — i.e., JSON-LD blocks have grown not just in count but in density.
  • Who actually consumes it: Google, Bing, Yandex, Yahoo (the original 2011 schema.org founders), plus knowledge-graph pipelines. The dominant @type distribution skews to Product, Organization, WebSite, BreadcrumbList, Article, LocalBusiness, FAQPage. No LLM agent runtime is known to consume in-page JSON-LD reliably: in early 2026, Mark Williams-Cook / Richard Barrett's "DUCK YEA T-SHIRTS" test (a fabricated address hidden only in JSON-LD) showed ChatGPT and Perplexity did sometimes surface the address — but behavior varies, and the SEO-research consensus is that LLMs read prose, not JSON-LD blocks, except where a vendor has wired in explicit structured-data extraction.
  • Distinction from Open Graph: Open Graph (<meta property="og:">) is consumed by Facebook/LinkedIn/Slack/Discord crawlers for preview cards; JSON-LD is consumed by search engines and AI-search citations*. The two are non-overlapping in consumers and complementary in production sites. Google's John Mueller has repeatedly confirmed OG tags are not a Google ranking signal, and Facebook's facebookexternalhit does not read JSON-LD.

2. The mechanics of <script type="application/ld+json"> allow exactly what you propose

WHATWG HTML spec ("The script element"): Any type attribute value that is a valid MIME-type string and is not a JavaScript MIME-type essence match causes the element to be treated as a data block: "not processed by the user agent, but instead by author script or other tools." The spec explicitly requires a MIME type so that "your data block will not ever be reinterpreted as a different script type, even in future user agents." application/ld+json is one such MIME; application/agent-manifest+json would be another. module, importmap, and speculationrules are reserved non-MIME exceptions.

JSON-LD 1.1 §7, "Embedding JSON-LD in HTML Documents" (W3C Recommendation 16 July 2020), specifies:

  • §7.2 (Restrictions for contents of JSON-LD script elements): the body MUST be valid JSON; occurrences of </script, <!--, --> inside strings MUST be escaped (e.g., \u003c/script). No CDATA wrapping. This is also Next.js's documented mitigation pattern.
  • Multiple script blocks: the design intent — per Gregg Kellogg's 2013 public-linked-json proposal that became §7 — is that "if the HTML file contains multiple JSON-LD script tags … the result is the RDF merge of the datasets." Multiple blocks union into one graph by default. §7.3 ("Locating a Specific JSON-LD Script Element") additionally allows fragment-identifier addressing (page.html#manifest selects <script id="manifest" type="application/ld+json">).
  • profile parameter (RFC 6906): IANA's registration of application/ld+json defines exactly one optional parameter — profile, "a non-empty list of space-separated URIs identifying specific constraints or conventions." Live deployments use it: RO-Crate's metadata file is served as application/ld+json; profile="http://www.w3.org/ns/json-ld#flattened http://www.w3.org/ns/json-ld#compacted https://w3id.org/ro/crate". A custom profile="https://example.com/agent-manifest" is fully legitimate.
  • **+ld+json suffix:* the JSON-LD WG resolved (2019-11-01, Resolution #4) that "Conforming json-ld processors SHOULD treat *+ld+json in the same way as the application/ld+json media type." So application/agent-manifest+ld+json is spec-legal and gives you a distinct dispatch name while inheriting JSON-LD parser semantics.

Google's parser: Google's structured-data parser extracts JSON-LD from <script type="application/ld+json"> in both server-rendered and JavaScript-generated DOM. Crucially, it parses by @context + @type lookup against schema.org (and a small set of allowed vocabularies). A block whose @context resolves to a non-schema.org IRI is parsed as data but is ignored for rich-result eligibility — i.e., it's syntactically valid and harmless, but invisible to SEO features.

3. Agent-facing metadata: the niche is half-filled, and nobody picked the <script> slot

Concrete prior art for "agent reads document, decides what to hydrate":

Mechanism Where it lives Status (May 2026) Relevance
llms.txt /llms.txt (root file, Markdown) Proposed by Jeremy Howard / Answer.AI, Sept 2024. Adopted by Anthropic, Cursor, Cloudflare, Vercel via Mintlify. Not a Google initiative. John Mueller on Google Search Central (≈June 2025): "AFAIK none of the AI services have said they're using LLMs.txt (and you can tell when you look at your server logs that they don't even check for it). To me, it's comparable to the keywords meta tag, this is what a site owner claims their site is about. At that point, why not just check the site directly?" OtterlyAI's 90-day measurement: 84 of 62,100 AI bot requests hit /llms.txt (0.1%). Gary Illyes confirmed at Search Central Live July 2025 that Google does not support it. Directly competing solution to the same problem — but at a different layer (per-site, not per-document).
WebMCP (navigator.modelContext) Browser API + HTML form attributes W3C Web ML CG incubation; Chrome 146 Canary (Feb 2026); Edge co-developing; stable ~Chrome 148 (mid-2026). Polyfill @mcp-b/global. Lets the page register tools (callable functions), not content sections. Fills the "actions" half of the agent-facing niche; does not fill the "reference content navigation" half.
MCP Resources Server-side (JSON-RPC over HTTP) Anthropic, Nov 2024; Linux Foundation–hosted. resources/list + resources/read + URI templates + audience/priority annotations. The closest semantic analog: an index of addressable resources with descriptions and metadata. But MCP resources are served by a separate MCP server, not embedded in HTML.
Claude Agent Skills Filesystem directory + SKILL.md + YAML frontmatter Anthropic, Oct 2025. Three-tier progressive disclosure (name+description → SKILL.md → sub-references). Implements exactly the progressive-disclosure pattern you describe, but at the agent-skill level, not the document level. Not in HTML.
NLWeb Site-wide ask endpoint backed by Schema.org + vector store + MCP Microsoft, May 2025; R. V. Guha leading; reuses Schema.org. "AI queries the site" rather than "AI reads in-page metadata."
AIPREF (IETF) robots.txt extension + HTTP headers + in-content metadata IETF WG chartered Jan 2025; draft-ietf-aipref-vocab and draft-ietf-aipref-attach (Jan 2026). Vocabulary for training-permission preferences, not for navigation. But the WG's charter explicitly mentions liaising with WHATWG/W3C on HTML-attached preferences — the closest existing IETF venue for agent-facing in-document metadata.
ai-plugin.json /.well-known/ai-plugin.json OpenAI ChatGPT plugins, March 2023. Effectively superseded by GPT Actions and now MCP. First widely-deployed "agent manifest" pattern. Lived at well-known URI, not in HTML.
Web Application Manifest Linked via <link rel="manifest"> W3C Rec; application/manifest+json registered with IANA. Only registered "manifest+json" today — but for PWAs, not agents, and a separate file.
RO-Crate ro-crate-metadata.json (custom-@context JSON-LD) W3ID since ~2019. Uses application/ld+json; profile="https://w3id.org/ro/crate" to describe a packaged collection of addressable resources. Closest structural precedent: custom non-schema.org @context, addressable-resource manifest, lives in JSON-LD. But also a separate file, not in-document.

What I could not find: any WHATWG/W3C issue or working-group proposal for an in-<head> agent-navigation <script> block. The pattern you describe — <script type="application/ld+json"> with custom @context carrying a per-document TOC of addressable sections with token costs and dep edges — appears to be genuinely novel, with RO-Crate as the only direct structural analog and nobody else proposing the in-document variant.

4. JSON-LD criticisms that do apply to your use case

  • Parser cost. Dr. Charles Severance ("Dr. Chuck") benchmarked Manu Sporny's own digitalbazaar/php-json-ld library on PHP 5.5 ("Unconstrained JSON-LD Performance Is Bad for API Specs", April 2016): 1,000 iterations of parsing product.json took 0.09 s for JSON-only vs. 167.58 s when jsonld_compact was called and fetched the remote schema.org context — a ~1,862× slowdown. Sporny's own follow-up ("JSON-LD Best Practice: Context Caching") acknowledged the issue and recommended context caching. For an agent-manifest use case where the agent (an LLM tool harness) parses the block, a full JSON-LD processor is overkill — you want shape-validated JSON. Mitigation: require the manifest to be JSON-LD-compatible (so it round-trips to RDF if anyone cares) but specify that conforming agents MAY parse it as plain JSON against a fixed schema. This is exactly what Schema.org does in practice — almost nobody runs @context expansion on schema.org blocks; they treat them as keyed JSON.
  • Drift between markup and prose. This is Google's actual policy lever, not just a critique: Google Search's General Structured Data Guidelines explicitly forbid "marking up content that is not visible to readers of the page." For schema.org entity data the rule is enforced. For your case it is the opposite problem: the manifest describes the prose (summaries, hashes), so the prose is the source of truth and drift means the manifest is stale. The content-hash field in your design (good!) catches this on the agent side, not the search-engine side. Google will not penalize you for an @context it doesn't recognize, but a sloppy hybrid (schema.org Article alongside agent-manifest data in the same block) will trip the visible-content rule.
  • Memory-unbounded parsing → DoS. The IndieWeb wiki notes that "the overly-flexible nature of JSON-LD … proves impossible to bound the memory consumption of your JSON-LD parser, making DoS attacks certain" — Mastodon/Pleroma both punted on full JSON-LD for this reason. AT Protocol's FAQ explicitly rejected JSON-LD: "JSON-LD simplifies the task of consuming RDF vocabularies, but it does so by hiding the underlying concepts, not by making RDF more legible." Mitigation: define your manifest with a fixed @context that conforming agents may pin/cache and treat as JSON Schema, not as a generic RDF processor input.
  • Duplicate maintenance burden. Schema App's 2017 "State of Schema Markup" survey (Martha van Berkel, 75 respondents) found 40% of marketers maintain schema markup manually; Search Engine Watch's write-up of the same survey noted "close to a fifth of respondents (19%) said that they update their Schema markup every day." More recently, the Digital Applied 5,000-site audit (2026) found 49% of deployed JSON-LD is silently broken on at least one required field. A per-section manifest with summaries and token counts and hashes — if hand-maintained — will rot at least as fast. The hashes are doing real work here: they let the agent detect drift and refuse to trust the summary. The token counts can be machine-recomputed in CI. The summaries are the actual long-lived authoring burden.

5. Hidden-content concerns: narrower than they sound

Google's policy is: "Don't mark up content that is not visible to readers of the page." This is enforced for types Google awards rich results to (Recipe, Product, Article, Event, FAQ, etc., per schema.org). A block with @context: "https://example.com/agent-manifest" and @type: "AgentManifest" does not claim a Google-recognized type, so Google's structured-data parser sees it as out-of-vocabulary and ignores it. You are not violating Google's policy by shipping an agent manifest in this slot; you are using a slot Google's parser will not interpret. The risk is co-mingling: if you ship the manifest and schema.org Article markup in the same block, or if your manifest's summaries contradict the visible prose, you risk being flagged as a cloaking signal under the spam-policy umbrella.

6. Will the LLMs that the manifest is for actually read it?

This is the most honest contrarian point. In production today:

  • ChatGPT browsing / Perplexity / Claude web search: generally extract main-content text (often via Readability-style heuristics) and feed it to the model. Some pipelines extract structured data; most do not specifically extract JSON-LD with non-schema.org contexts.
  • Anthropic Claude Skills / OpenAI Apps SDK / MCP Resources: consume their own protocols (filesystem markdown, MCP servers). They do not look at HTML <script> blocks for skill metadata.
  • WebMCP-enabled browsers (Chrome 146+ Canary): consume navigator.modelContext tool registrations, not <script type="application/ld+json">.

So the realistic 2026 consumer of your manifest is your own agent, or an agent you ship an adapter for. That changes the framing: this isn't a public-web standard play, it's a carrier choice for your own protocol that happens to be parseable by anyone else who wants to.

Details: Recommended Type / Naming Conventions

If you proceed:

MIME / type attribute:

  • Use <script type="application/ld+json">. This gives you immediate compatibility with JSON-LD playgrounds, validators, RDF tooling, and any future agent that already knows how to parse JSON-LD. WHATWG-safe (it's a real MIME, not a reserved keyword), and the JSON-LD WG's *+ld+json resolution lets you migrate to application/agent-manifest+ld+json later without changing the wire format.
  • If you want HTTP-level dispatch when the manifest is served standalone, use application/ld+json; profile="https://yourdomain.example/ns/agent-manifest/v1". This is RFC 6906 and matches the RO-Crate precedent.
  • Do not invent application/agent-manifest+json without the +ld+json suffix. You lose JSON-LD processor compatibility for zero gain. The whole reason to use this slot is to inherit the existing JSON-LD parsing ecosystem.

@context:

  • Host a context document at a stable IRI you control (e.g., https://yourdomain.example/ns/agent-manifest/v1). Define your terms (sections, sectionId, summary, tokenCost, contentHash, dependsOn) and pin them to that IRI.
  • Do not put https://schema.org as the default @vocab. Schema.org's context claims thousands of terms (the WG explicitly debated this in schemaorg/schemaorg#990) and will shadow yours.
  • If you want loose schema.org compatibility (e.g., for a WebPage parent), use an array context: "@context": ["https://yourdomain.example/ns/agent-manifest/v1", "https://schema.org"] — but be aware ordering matters for term overrides.

@type:

  • A single root with @type: "AgentManifest" (resolved via your custom context to https://yourdomain.example/ns/agent-manifest/v1#AgentManifest). Each section entry can be @type: "ManifestSection".
  • Multiple blocks per page are fine — JSON-LD §7 merges them. But for an agent-manifest use case, prefer one block with id="agent-manifest" so consumers can address it via fragment per JSON-LD §7.3.

Inside the block:

  • Strictly valid JSON (no comments, no trailing commas).
  • Escape </script as \u003c/script in any string value.
  • Use @id (or your own sectionId mapped to @id) with URI fragments — #section-onboarding, #section-api-reference — so each section is a real RDF node and the agent can dereference it back to a DOM element with id="section-onboarding".

Verification fields:

  • contentHash: a SHA-256 of the section's canonicalized DOM text. Mandatory. Lets the agent detect manifest/prose drift without re-parsing the whole document.
  • tokenCost: integer; specify which tokenizer (e.g., "tokenizer": "cl100k_base") at the manifest root. Otherwise the number is meaningless across model families.
  • dependsOn: array of @id references. Use JSON-LD @type: "@id" coercion so the values expand to IRIs in RDF form.

Recommendations

  1. Ship it as JSON-LD with a custom @context and a profile parameter — not as a new MIME. application/ld+json; profile="https://yourdomain.example/ns/agent-manifest/v1" is the spec-clean choice. It gets you (a) WHATWG safety, (b) JSON-LD-tooling compatibility, (c) RDF round-trip if anyone needs it, (d) RFC 6906 HTTP-level dispatch, (e) zero conflict with Google's structured-data parser.
  2. Treat the carrier as a private protocol that happens to be parseable by anyone. Do not invest in lobbying WHATWG or W3C. The agentic-web standards landscape is in active flux: AIPREF, WebMCP, MCP, NLWeb, llms.txt are all competing. The "winning" slot may be the browser API surface (WebMCP) or the well-known URI surface (/.well-known/awp.json, /llms.txt), not the in-document slot. Pick the in-document slot because your agent benefits, not because the ecosystem is converging there.
  3. Pair it with an HTTP Link header. Serve Link: </your-doc.html#agent-manifest>; rel="describedby"; type="application/ld+json"; profile="..." so a crawler that fetches HEAD can discover the manifest without parsing HTML. Cheap, standards-compliant, and lets you publish a sidecar JSON file with the same content.
  4. Don't co-mingle with schema.org blocks. Keep entity data (Article, WebPage, Organization) in separate <script type="application/ld+json"> blocks with @context: "https://schema.org". Keep the agent manifest in its own block. JSON-LD §7's merge semantics make this safe, and it prevents the visible-content / hidden-content collision under Google's spam policy.
  5. Plan for the manifest to be authored by tooling, not by hand. The drift-and-rot critique that kills schema.org adoption (49% of deployed JSON-LD is broken per the Digital Applied 2026 audit) will kill this too if humans write it. Generate tokenCost and contentHash in CI from the rendered DOM. Treat the human-authored part (summaries, dependency edges) as the only field that can be wrong.
  6. Benchmarks that would change my advice:
  • If WebMCP ships stable in Chrome 148 with a resources/read-style API (not just tool-calls), reconsider — the browser API may become the right slot.
  • If AIPREF or a successor IETF WG charters work on in-HTML agent-discovery metadata, join that conversation rather than ship a private convention.
  • If a major agent runtime (Anthropic, OpenAI, Google) ships a public extractor for <script type="application/ld+json"> blocks with custom contexts, the calculus shifts from "private protocol" to "candidate de-facto standard."

Caveats

  • The framing might be wrong. Your design assumes the agent loads the HTML, parses the manifest, then hydrates sections. In practice, the cheapest version is: the agent fetches a .md or JSON sidecar (llms-full.txt pattern, or MCP resources/list) — no HTML parsing, no DOM-section addressing, no escape concerns. The in-document carrier is only the right choice if (a) the document is the canonical artifact (e.g., the user has it open in a browser and the agent is co-located), or (b) you need the manifest and the prose to ship as one atomic, signable, cacheable resource.
  • LLM-as-parser is unreliable today. The DUCK YEA T-SHIRTS test (Mark Williams-Cook, early 2026) shows that current LLMs sometimes pick up JSON-LD content the page does not visibly contain, and sometimes don't — behavior varies by vendor and pipeline. Don't rely on opportunistic LLM extraction of your manifest; build the extractor.
  • No standards body owns this slot. Unlike schema.org (Bing+Google+Yandex+Yahoo coalition), llms.txt (one proposer, partial vendor adoption), or WebMCP (Chrome+Edge co-development), there is no convening body for in-document agent manifests. Whatever you ship is a private convention until it isn't.
  • Source-quality note: several adoption statistics in this brief come from SEO marketing blogs that cite HTTP Archive / WebDataCommons but inflate or recontextualize for marketing purposes. The hard numbers I trust are from WebDataCommons (51.25% structured-data adoption, JSON-LD on 70% of structured-data sites, 74B quads from 16.5M websites, Oct 2024 release), the W3C/WHATWG specs themselves, Severance's 2016 benchmark of Sporny's own library, and Schema App's 2017 survey (Martha van Berkel, 75 respondents); treat the rest as directional. The "39% update monthly" figure I had originally drawn from Schema App is not confirmed in the primary source — the verified number from that survey is that 19% update daily and 40% maintain markup manually.

Provenance: Claude (Sonnet) research document, 2025

MCP Resources as a Carrier for Progressive-Disclosure Reference Content: What's Solved, What's Adjacent, and Where the Gap Is

TL;DR

  • MCP Resources solves the transport, addressing, listing, subscription, and cache-invalidation plumbing for progressive-disclosure reference content (URIs, resources/list with cursor pagination, resources/read, resources/subscribe, notifications/resources/updated, lastModified annotation), but it does not solve the document-level concerns you're after: per-section token cost estimates, content hashes, dependency edges, and human-authored section summaries distinct from content. Those live above the protocol.
  • No production MCP server today exposes sub-sections of a single document as separately addressable Resources with a dedicated URI scheme. The closest precedent — SEP-2640's skill:// scheme from the Skills Over MCP Working Group — addresses whole files inside a skill directory, and it explicitly omits content digests, defines no dependency edges, and has no per-resource token-cost annotation. The HTML-manifest idea is in genuine green space.
  • The gap is mostly at the document-format layer, partly at the server-implementation layer, and not at the protocol layer. MCP Resources can carry an HTML artifact with a machine-readable manifest of addressable sections (via URI templates, _meta, and annotations); building the manifest schema and the per-section affordances is the work that is not yet done.

Key Findings

  1. The MCP Resources primitive (spec revisions 2025-06-18 and 2025-11-25) is mature on plumbing but thin on payload semantics. Resources are application-controlled, URI-identified, support text and base64 blob content, cursor-paginated listing, optional per-server subscribe and listChanged capabilities, RFC 6570 URI templates for dynamic resources, and three first-class annotation fields: audience, priority (0.0–1.0), and lastModified (ISO 8601). A Resource object exposes uri, name, optional title, description, mimeType, size, and annotations — no token estimate, no hash, no dependency declaration.
  1. The three-primitive split is a control-plane split, not a data-vs-action split. Tools are model-controlled (the LLM decides when to call); Prompts are user-controlled (slash commands); Resources are application-controlled (the host decides what to surface). The reason "read data" tends to be Resources is that it's content the host or user pulls into context, not content the model autonomously fetches. In practice, many MCP clients — including OpenAI Codex's MCP integration — only wire up tools/list and ignore resources/list, which is why Resources is widely considered "the most underused primitive."
  1. Resources is widely under-adopted by clients. OpenAI Codex's MCP integration only issues tools/list and tools/call, leaving advertised resources invisible to the runtime (open issue openai/codex#4956, opened October 2025: "The client layer only issues tools/list and tools/call, so resources are invisible to the runtime and UI. This gap prevents agents from reading MCP-provided configuration or documents automatically"). Apigene's April 2026 review of 78,849 MCP tool descriptions reported that 98% of them "don't tell agents when to use them," and resource-loading is asymmetric across clients — most clients don't expose resource-loading to the model directly, which is the exact frustration driving the Skills Over MCP Working Group.
  1. Production servers exposing document-like content as Resources almost always operate at the file granularity, not sub-document. The reference filesystem server uses file:// URIs; community Notion MCP servers like seonglae/mcp-notion use note:// UUID URIs for whole pages; community Google Drive MCP servers expose files via MCP Resources; the pawcoding/markdown-mcp-resource server exposes whole markdown files at markdown://{hostname}/{filename}. None of these break a single document into addressable sections.
  1. SEP-2640 (Skills Extension) is the most-developed precedent for "a single artifact with a manifest of addressable sub-units." It defines a skill:// URI scheme, a well-known skill://index.json manifest, and addresses each file inside a skill directory as skill://<skill-path>/<file-path>. It is the closest design analog to your HTML-manifest idea — and it pointedly leaves out three of the four affordances you want.
  1. Token cost is a known, painful, unsolved problem in MCP. MindStudio's measurement piece on Claude Code MCP overhead reports "A single well-documented tool might consume 200–500 tokens… The 18,000-token figure you'll see cited for MCP overhead isn't a worst-case scenario — it's a realistic mid-range estimate for a developer who has assembled a practical working setup." Maxim AI's Bifrost Code Mode benchmark documents the upper end: "Round 3, 508 tools across 16 servers: input tokens fell from 75.1M to 5.4M (−92.8%), estimated cost from $377.00 to $29.00 (−92.2%), pass rate 100% in both configurations." But none of this token bookkeeping is exposed by the MCP protocol itself as a per-resource estimate; everything is computed client-side from schema serialization.
  1. Progressive disclosure is a recognized pattern, but it lives above MCP, not inside it. Anthropic's Agent Skills system implements three-level progressive disclosure (frontmatter → SKILL.md body → linked files), and the Claude-Mem and "scout pattern" implementations describe the same shape, but none of them are protocol features of MCP. The MCP-side equivalent is what the Skills Over MCP WG is now trying to standardize — and they are choosing to do so via Resources, not a new primitive.
  1. The protocol is silent on URI fragment identifiers. The spec inherits RFC 3986 generic syntax (fragment semantics are media-type-dependent and client-side only). RFC 5147 line-range fragments for text/plain exist as prior art, but no MCP SEP references them.

Details

1. The MCP Resources spec, as of late 2025/early 2026

The current stable spec revision is 2025-11-25, released November 25, 2025 on the one-year anniversary of MCP. The Resources surface is essentially unchanged from 2025-06-18 in shape; the 2025-11-25 release added icons metadata for tools, resources, resource templates, and prompts (SEP-973), and OAuth/auth improvements, but did not restructure Resources.

Listing. Clients send resources/list with an optional opaque cursor parameter; the response returns resources: Resource[] and an optional nextCursor. The server emits notifications/resources/list_changed when the list changes, provided it declared capabilities.resources.listChanged: true.

Reading. resources/read with a uri parameter returns contents: ResourceContents[], where each element is either text ({ uri, mimeType, text }) or blob ({ uri, mimeType, blob: <base64> }). Multiple contents in the array are intended to represent the same logical resource in multiple formats (e.g., .txt and .pdf), though in practice an analysis of real servers (cited in SEP-2093's commit history as the justification for an isCollection flag) found that 65% of multi-content usage is actually "collection/list" semantics — returning all items in a directory — which is why SEP-2093 is now in review to clean this up.

Templates. resources/templates/list returns ResourceTemplate[] with uriTemplate (RFC 6570), name, optional title, description, mimeType. Arguments can be auto-completed through the completion API. This is the protocol-level mechanism for dynamic addressing: a template like skill://{skillName}/{+path} lets a client construct any number of valid resource URIs at runtime.

Subscriptions. A client sends resources/subscribe with a URI; the server sends notifications/resources/updated when that resource changes. Subscribe is currently a server-level capability — SEP-2093 proposes moving it to per-resource granularity.

URI schemes. The spec calls out https://, file://, git:// as standard, and explicitly permits custom schemes per RFC 3986. Servers in the wild use note://, config://, stock://, db://, screen://, ui://, skill://, weather://city/country/current, and more.

Annotations. Resources, resource templates, and content blocks share three annotation fields: audience (["user" | "assistant"]), priority (0.0–1.0, where 1.0 is effectively required), and lastModified (ISO 8601 timestamp). There is no built-in token-cost field, no content-hash field, and no dependency field.

Resource object shape (full). uri (required), name (required), title (optional human-readable display name), description (optional), mimeType (optional), size (optional, in bytes), annotations (optional). SEP-973 adds icons.

2. Architectural rationale: Resources vs Tools vs Prompts

The community-converged framing (Anthropic engineering, Speakeasy, Exo Technologies, AWS Heroes write-ups) is three control planes:

  • Tools — model-controlled. The LLM decides when to call. Used for actions with side effects (writing, sending, deploying) and for dynamic data retrieval where the query depends on conversation context (database queries, search). Tools are most adopted because most clients implement tools/list and tools/call first.
  • Resources — application-controlled. The host decides when to surface. Used for read-only, cacheable, static-or-semi-static context (schemas, configuration, documentation, current files, user-selected attachments). Resources are designed to be loaded into context passively, not fetched through tool calls. They support change notifications and subscriptions.
  • Prompts — user-controlled. The user explicitly triggers via slash commands or menus. Used for reusable workflow templates (e.g., "code review template," "weekly sales report") that compose multiple tools and resources.

The boundary cases are genuinely confusing. A tool that "returns something" can do exactly what a resource does; the practical disambiguation is who controls invocation and whether the operation has side effects or token-cost variability. The Apigene/MindStudio framing is: "If any tool call returns static data that doesn't change between turns, refactor it into a resource."

Does Resources have a metadata-before-fetch affordance? Today, in the stable spec: partially. resources/list returns description, size, and annotations (audience, priority, lastModified) per resource, which is a real "summary before full fetch" surface. But there is no separate resources/metadata method that returns metadata for a single specific URI without listing or reading. SEP-2093 (in review as of February 2026, sponsored as draft) proposes exactly this: a new resources/metadata endpoint to fetch a resource's metadata without retrieving content, motivated by "Estimating context window usage via size before loading" as one of the named use cases. If accepted, this is the protocol-level affordance closest to your manifest design.

3. Production examples — file granularity dominates

  • Filesystem (@modelcontextprotocol/server-filesystem). Uses file:// URIs. Large files are handled by either chunking via the tool API (community servers like fast-filesystem-mcp, large-file-mcp, mcp-documentation-server) or by relying on the client's context window. No section addressing.
  • Notion. The official remote MCP server (https://mcp.notion.com/mcp) is largely tool-shaped rather than resource-shaped — search, fetch, notion-create-pages, notion-update-page. Community servers like seonglae/mcp-notion do use note:// URIs with UUID slugs for pages, but a Notion page is the smallest addressable unit, not a section within it.
  • Google Drive. Google's official remote MCP server (https://drivemcp.googleapis.com/mcp/v1) and community servers like piotr-agier/google-drive-mcp expose files via MCP Resources for reading content, plus tools for create/update/move. File granularity, not section granularity.
  • GitHub MCP server. Tool-centric. File content via get_file_contents (a tool), not a repo:// resource template. Code symbols/ranges are exposed by community tree-sitter servers (wrale/mcp-server-tree-sitter, ctoth/mcp_server_code_extractor, jgravelle/jcodemunch-mcp) — but again, all of those are tools that return line ranges, not Resources with addressable URIs.
  • OpenAI Apps SDK / MCP Apps (SEP-1865). Uses the ui:// URI scheme to expose static HTML (typically a single-page widget) as an MCP Resource, with a tool referencing it via _meta["openai/outputTemplate"]. MIME type is text/html+skybridge. This is the closest production analog to "HTML as an MCP-served artifact" — but the HTML is a whole sandboxed widget bundle, not a progressively-disclosed document.
  • Markdown documentation. pawcoding/markdown-mcp-resource exposes whole markdown files at markdown://{hostname}/{filename} based on a remote index.json listing. This is the simplest existing precedent for a "manifest of addressable text resources," but it operates at the file level, not the section level.
  • Mintlify. The hosted Mintlify MCP server exposes whole skill.md files as MCP Resources and provides a separate "query docs filesystem" tool for section-level reads. The Skills Over MCP WG has flagged this as a natural integration point.

How servers handle large payloads. Three patterns: (a) chunked-read tools with offset/length parameters; (b) URI templates that take a path or range parameter, parsed server-side; (c) returning a resource link from a tool ("the 2-step process: tool fetches an artifact, writes to disk, returns a templated resource URI, so a client can later fetch it on-demand"). None of these is a protocol-level chunked-read for resources.

4. Community criticism and open problems

Where Resources is underused. The most frequently cited gripes:

  • Many clients (Codex, several IDE plugins) implement only the tool side of the protocol, so server authors can't rely on resources being discovered. The pragmatic workaround is wrapping resources as tools — Wix Engineering and the FastMCP community both document this as a default pattern: "Not all AI agents know how to discover and use resources directly. However, all agents understand tools. By exposing resource link as a tool, you make resources discoverable in all AI agent flows through tool listing."
  • The description field on a Resource is widely considered insufficient for agent routing. Without a structured semantic-tags vocabulary, agents either ignore resources or load them all, defeating the point.
  • Resources are pull-only by design: the model can't autonomously fetch a resource, which forces awkward designs in agent-driven flows.

Open issues and SEPs related to Resources, by number and status (modelcontextprotocol/modelcontextprotocol):

  • SEP-973 (accepted, shipped in 2025-11-25). Adds icons and websiteUrl metadata to Implementations, Resources, Tools, Prompts.
  • SEP-2093 (in review, Feb 2026). Extends resources/read responses with full metadata (name, title, description, icons, annotations, size), introduces a resources/metadata endpoint, defines per-resource capabilities (list, subscribe) and resourceType, clarifies multi-content semantics, removes EmbeddedResource.annotations. Olaservo's review comment notes that the Skills WG built a _manifest resource with SHA-256 hashes as a workaround, and asks whether a hash field on Resource would be in scope. The author's response: "I do think this is worth considering but I think it would be good to separate it from this SEP… computing hashes may not be cheap: potentially turns metadata reads into an O(size) operation."
  • SEP-1865 (MCP Apps). Standardizes ui:// HTML resources for interactive widgets, with text/html rendered in sandboxed iframes; jointly authored by MCP-UI, OpenAI, and Anthropic.
  • SEP-2640 (Skills Extension, draft with sponsor). Defines skill:// URI scheme over Resources, well-known skill://index.json manifest, RFC 6570 templates for dynamic skill spaces. Explicitly omits content digests ("integrity is the transport's concern over an authenticated MCP connection"), declares no dependency edges, defines no cost/token annotation.
  • SEP-1649 (MCP Server Cards, draft). Adds .well-known/mcp.json for server-level metadata, complementary to the Skills work.
  • SEP-1960. Proposes .well-known/mcp discovery endpoint; alternative path to 1649.
  • SEP-1686 (Tasks). Async/durable requests with task handles; relevant indirectly because it could let an HTML-section fetch be a long-running task.
  • Issue #1533, PR #1535 (closed in favor of 2093). Earlier attempts at resource metadata in read responses.

Richer-resource-metadata proposals. The pattern in the wild is _meta and tags (FastMCP exposes a tags field on resources for client-side filtering, plus a free-form meta dict). The OpenAI Apps SDK convention uses _meta keys like openai/widgetDescription, openai/widgetCSP, openai/widgetPrefersBorder. There is no convention for semantic tags, token-cost estimation, or dependency graphs at the protocol layer.

5. Sub-document addressing — the actual gap

Targeted research turned up no production MCP server that treats sections of a single document as separately addressable Resources with a dedicated URI scheme.

The relevant data points:

  • Mintlify's MCP server exposes whole skill.md files as MCP Resources and uses a tool — "Query docs filesystem: Reads and navigates your documentation's virtual filesystem using shell-style commands. Use this to retrieve page content, browse the docs structure, or extract specific sections—including batch reads across multiple pages in a single call" — for section-level access. The section affordance is in tools, not resources.
  • GitHub MCP server documents file-level reads via the get_file_contents tool; no repo://path#range resource URI scheme. SEP-2640 references it as a target reference implementation for the skills extension, not for code-range addressing.
  • Tree-sitter MCP servers (wrale/mcp-server-tree-sitter, ctoth/mcp_server_code_extractor) expose symbols via tools that return line ranges; they do not register one Resource per function.
  • SEP-2640 skill:// scheme addresses files within a skill directory: skill://<skill-path>/<file-path>, with the manifest at skill://index.json. This is the closest precedent — a single artifact (a skill bundle) with a manifest of addressable sub-units (files). But:
  • No content hashes: "the digest field is omitted (integrity is the transport's concern over an authenticated MCP connection)."
  • No dependency edges between skills: the SEP explicitly states "This extension has no dependencies beyond the base MCP Resources primitive," and per-skill dependency declaration is an open issue (agentskills/agentskills#110) noted in the WG meeting notes.
  • No per-skill token-cost annotation: relevance is judged from frontmatter (name, description), not cost.
  • MCP spec on URI fragments. The spec inherits RFC 3986: fragment semantics are media-type-dependent and client-side only ("Fragment identifier semantics are independent of the URI scheme and thus cannot be redefined by scheme specifications"). RFC 5147 (text/plain line-range fragments like #line=10,20) exists as prior art but is referenced by no MCP SEP.

Emerging convention? Not yet at the protocol layer. The convention that is emerging is: use RFC 6570 URI templates to expose dynamic addressing parameters ({skill}/{path} or {document}/{section}), and let the server decide how the path resolves. This works fine for any HTML-section addressing scheme you invent — html://artifact/section-id — but the manifest schema with token estimates, hashes, summaries, and dependency edges is not standardized anywhere.

6. Synthesis — does MCP Resources solve "addressable section of one document"?

At the protocol layer, mostly yes — but only the plumbing. MCP Resources provides:

  • URI-based addressing (any custom scheme, including html://artifact#section)
  • A listing endpoint with cursor pagination
  • A read endpoint with text and blob content
  • A change-notification model
  • Optional subscriptions for live updates
  • Annotations for priority, audience, last-modified
  • URI templates for dynamic per-section URIs (e.g., html://manifest/{section_id})
  • An optional size field on Resource (which can carry approximate byte count and, if you want, you can stuff a token estimate in _meta)

At the protocol layer, what's missing relative to your HTML-manifest design:

  • Token cost estimate: no first-class field. SEP-2093 introduces resources/metadata for cheap metadata reads, which is the right shape, but the token-count field is not proposed. You'd carry it in _meta (FastMCP-style) or annotations extensions.
  • Content hash: no first-class field. Olaservo's SEP-2093 review comment explicitly raised this for cache validation; the author punted to a future SEP.
  • Dependency edges: no first-class field. The Skills Over MCP WG has the same problem and treats it as an open issue.
  • Human-authored summary distinct from content: description is the closest field, plus title. There is no convention for a "summary-of-this-section" field separate from description.

At the server-implementation layer, building one HTML-as-MCP-server is straightforward: register one resource template per section URI pattern, parse the manifest at startup, hash sections at parse time, expose a resources/list that walks the manifest, and put per-section token estimates in _meta. This is essentially the SEP-2640 skill pattern applied to a single HTML artifact instead of a directory of markdown files.

At the document-format layer is where the most novel work is. The MCP spec defines neither (a) a manifest schema with token cost, content hash, dependency edges, and human-authored summaries, nor (b) a convention for fragment-based URI addressing of HTML sections. Both are genuinely unsolved in the MCP ecosystem. The closest analog is SEP-2640's skill://index.json schema — and as documented above, it omits exactly the four affordances you care about.

Where the gap is, in one sentence: The protocol gives you the transport and addressing; the existing servers give you patterns for file-granularity but not section-granularity; and the manifest schema (with token cost, hash, dependency edges, summary) is genuinely unbuilt.

Recommendations

Stage 1 — Build the HTML manifest as an MCP server, pattern after SEP-2640. Define one custom URI scheme (e.g. html://<artifact-id>/<section-id> or use fragments per RFC 5147 prior art). Register a resource template (html://{artifact_id}/{+section_id}). Expose one well-known resource at html://<artifact-id>/manifest.json whose schema includes id, summary, token cost, content hash, dependency edges per section. This gives you everything your manifest needs while reusing existing MCP plumbing for listing, reading, subscriptions, and change notifications.

Stage 2 — Put per-section token cost, hash, summary, and dependency edges in two places, redundantly. (a) In the manifest JSON resource (canonical, version-controllable, human-readable). (b) In the _meta field of each Resource entry returned by resources/list (so MCP clients that don't parse the manifest still get the data). Use description for the human-authored summary and size for byte count; reserve _meta["x-tokens"], _meta["x-hash"], _meta["x-deps"] for the rest. Threshold to revisit: if SEP-2093 lands with a resources/metadata endpoint and any of {hash, tokens} become first-class, move those out of _meta and into the standard fields.

Stage 3 — Engage the Skills Over MCP WG before you finalize the manifest schema. Their skill://index.json and your HTML manifest are 80% the same artifact: a structured index of addressable sub-units of a single bundle. The WG explicitly noted that "no client implementer currently treats Markdown as structured data with progressive disclosure—they just dump entire contents into context" — your work is downstream of theirs. Comment on PR #69 in experimental-ext-skills and on SEP-2640 in the main repo to make the case that hashes, token estimates, and dependency edges belong in the canonical manifest schema, not punted. Even if they don't accept it for skills, this is precisely the design forum where the question gets adjudicated.

Stage 4 — Wrap the section-read as both a Resource and a Tool. Until client support for Resources catches up (Codex, several IDE plugins still don't list resources), expose a read_section(id) tool that returns the same content. This is the Wix Engineering pattern. Threshold to drop the tool wrapper: when the majority of your target clients (whichever you care about) advertise full resources capability and actually surface resources to the model.

Stage 5 — Treat fragment identifiers as a maybe, not a default. The MCP spec is silent on #fragment and RFC 3986 says fragments are media-type-dependent and client-side. If your HTML artifact is served as text/html, fragments work in browsers but agents won't necessarily handle them. Safer: encode the section as a path segment (html://artifact/section-id) and treat the HTML's id attributes as authoritative server-side. You get the same effect without depending on undefined behavior. Threshold to use fragments: if and only if MCP standardizes media-type fragment handling (no such SEP exists yet).

Caveats

  • The fastest-moving area is the Skills Over MCP working group, whose SEP-2640 draft is in active review with reference implementations in gemini-cli, fast-agent, goose, codex, Claude Code, and the GitHub MCP Server. Anything you build that overlaps should be coordinated with their direction or risk needing migration.
  • SEP-2093 is in review as of February 2026, not yet accepted. The resources/metadata endpoint and per-resource capabilities are not stable design. Don't depend on them landing as written.
  • Client support for Resources is the binding constraint, not the protocol. Even if you build a perfect HTML-manifest server, hosts that only call tools/list will not surface your manifest — hence the recommendation to wrap section reads as tools too.
  • The community discussion includes "MCP is dead" arguments rooted in token bloat from tool descriptions. Those critiques apply more to tool-heavy servers; a resource-shaped server with a small manifest is exactly the architecture they recommend in place of dumping a thousand tools. Your design is on the right side of that critique.
  • "Token cost estimate" carries an irreducible bias: tokenizer choices differ across model families. Treat any per-section cost as a hint, not a contract, and ideally provide either a tokenizer identifier alongside the estimate or a range. The MCP spec offers no help here; it's your design call.
  • The 92.8% token-savings benchmark (Maxim AI's Bifrost Code Mode, 508 tools across 16 servers) and the 98% claim from Apigene's review of 78,849 tool descriptions are vendor blog numbers, not peer-reviewed measurements. Directionally informative, not authoritative.
  • The Mintlify "query docs filesystem" tool description, the SEP-2640 URI structure, the SEP-2093 review thread, and the GitHub MCP server tool-shape claims are drawn from the working-group documents and product docs current as of early-to-mid 2026; the WG's direction can shift between meetings.

Provenance: ChatGPT research document, 2025 — validation/audit pass over the Gemini and Claude corpus

Research Synthesis on Agent-Addressable Selective Hydration Artifacts

Executive Judgment

The core idea survives scrutiny, but only in a narrower form than the uploaded documents often claim. There is not yet a broadly adopted, standardized artifact primitive that exactly matches the proposed object. However, there is now a real and increasingly coherent design space around: site-level AI discovery files (llms.txt), repository instruction files (AGENTS.md), capability packages (Agent Skills and MCP), browser-oriented semantic representations (SOM), and HTTP/profile-based alternate representations. What does not exist yet is a settled standard for a bounded, human-readable knowledge artifact that exposes a machine-readable semantic map for selective region hydration. That gap is real enough to justify prototyping, but not yet strong enough to justify calling for a standard. citeturn20view0turn27view0turn26view0turn10view0turn8view0turn25view0

The strongest formulation is not “a brand-new web primitive” in the grand sense. It is better described as an authored knowledge artifact plus a compiled semantic access layer. In practice, that means: a human-facing document, preferably HTML or HTML-rendered content, paired with an agent-facing manifest or semantic map that lets a model discover sections, estimate relevance and cost, and hydrate only needed regions. That is meaningfully distinct from MCP, AGENTS.md, and vanilla RAG because it is about knowledge packaging inside a bounded artifact, not tool execution, repo governance, or corpus-wide retrieval. It is also meaningfully distinct from raw HTML or DOM parsing because the model should normally consume the compiled semantic map first, not the DOM. citeturn25view0turn27view0turn20view0turn11view0turn10view0

The idea is already being built in pieces, especially in the SOM / W3C browser-for-agents ecosystem, in llms.txt-style discovery, in Agent-HTML’s artifact-first workflow, and in profile-negotiated alternate representations. But those systems do not yet converge on a single artifact primitive for bounded knowledge objects. Most of the uploaded documents are strongest when they argue for this missing middle layer. They are weakest when they overstate adoption, standardization status, browser implementation status, or benchmark generality. citeturn10view0turn8view0turn28view0turn35view0turn20view0turn13view1

The most credible next move is not a standard or RFC. It is a repo-backed concept memo plus a small prototype CLI and reference corpus. The prototype should compile a manifest from an authored document, list nodes, hydrate selected nodes, validate anchors or selectors, and measure token and latency tradeoffs against baselines such as plain Markdown, llms.txt plus page markdown, and raw DOM extraction. That is the minimum move that would test whether the concept is genuinely useful instead of just rhetorically attractive. citeturn20view0turn25view0turn14academia1turn23academia2

Verified Core Concept

A defensible rewrite of the concept is:

An agent-addressable knowledge artifact is a bounded document or document package that preserves a human-readable presentation while exposing a compact semantic map of its internal regions, so an AI system can discover what exists inside it and selectively retrieve only the regions needed for a specific reasoning step.

That phrasing is stronger than the uploaded branding variants for several reasons. It avoids assuming a specific substrate such as HTML-only, sidecar-only, or network-only transport. It also avoids implying that “hydration” must mimic front-end framework hydration. Here, hydration simply means selective retrieval into active model context. The essential features are boundedness, human readability, machine-addressable internal structure, selective retrieval, and drift-resistant linkage between map and body. citeturn10view0turn20view0turn27view0turn25view0turn8view2turn8view3

The five uploaded documents share a central thesis: current agent infrastructure has good primitives for instructions (AGENTS.md), capabilities (MCP, Skills), and general retrieval, but lacks a good primitive for knowledge packaging inside a bounded artifact. That shared thesis is the strongest common thread across the corpus. Their most valuable contribution is not any specific name; it is the sharper separation between discovery context and hydrated knowledge context inside a document-scale object.

By direct reading of the uploaded files, the cleanest division of labor across them is this. The SHKO Position Paper is the clearest conceptual statement and the best at naming risks and boundary conditions. The HKO Research Report contributes the richest context-economics framing and the most concrete dual-surface proposal. Agent Knowledge Object Design Space contributes the broadest architecture matrix, but sometimes drifts from artifact design into protocol and runtime design. Architecting the SHKO is the most useful on web primitives, metadata placement, CSS, and HTTP. Evolutionary Archetypes is the most ambitious on ecosystem positioning, but it is also the most error-prone and the most likely to conflate draft, proposal, product, and standard status.

The major contradictions across the uploaded corpus are substantive. Some documents insist the object should be static, passive, offline-first, and non-executable; others drift toward a protocol, browser runtime, or hybrid capability package. Some describe a single-file artifact; others assume HTML plus sidecar manifest, HTTP-negotiated alternate views, or even a SOM-like compiled representation as the real substrate. Those tensions matter because they determine whether the proposal is really about authoring, packaging, serving, or runtime interaction. The strongest surviving formulation is the narrowest one: authored knowledge artifact plus compiled semantic access layer.

Source Validation Audit

Document references in the next two subsections are by filename because the file-search citation layer was unavailable in this session. External factual claims are web-cited below.

What the uploaded documents contribute and where they disagree

Uploaded document Most useful contribution Main weakness
SHKO Position Paper.md Best concise statement of the concept, boundaries, and objections Repeats several unsupported ecosystem claims as settled fact
Hyper-Knowledge Object (HKO) Research Report.md Strongest context-economics framing and concrete dual-file proposal Treats many performance numbers and governance claims as more established than they are
Agent Knowledge Object Design Space.md Best candidate-architecture matrix and comparison across substrates Often slips from artifact design into protocol/tooling design
Architecting the Selectively Hydratable Knowledge Object...md Best analysis of web primitives, metadata placement, CSS, and HTTP negotiation Still over-optimistic about web-native standardization readiness
Evolutionary Archetypes of Agentic Web Infrastructure...md Best attempt to map recent browser-agent work Highest concentration of conflation: W3C, WebMCP, browser preview, schema adoption, MARK, and protocol status

Across all five, the duplicated claims are easy to identify: context-bloat is costly; MCP and tool schemas can inflate prompts; AGENTS.md and Skills demonstrate progressive disclosure; HTML is attractive but noisy; a manifest is needed; CSS is useful for addressing but not as a metadata substrate; BAML may help validation; and HTTP/profile negotiation may help delivery. The strongest repeated claim is the need for a knowledge-packaging primitive distinct from capability packaging. The weakest repeated claims are the ecosystem-status claims around WebMCP, AWP, Schema.org adoption, BAML performance, and various named tools or kits.

Existing Work and the Real Design Space

What counts as direct prior art

The closest public prior art is not AGENTS.md, MCP, or general RAG. It is the cluster around compiled semantic representations of human-facing documents or web pages. The clearest example is the W3C Web Content Browser for AI Agents Community Group, launched on March 24, 2026. Its page says the group will develop a JSON format optimized for agent consumption, explicitly to avoid sending raw HTML or DOM snapshots to language models, and it names the SOM spec as a starting point. Separately, the Schema.org issue for WebPageSemanticRepresentation proposes a way for pages to advertise alternate representations for agent consumption. That is very close prior art for the “discover inside first, then fetch only what is needed” instinct. citeturn10view0turn8view0

The other important direct prior art is Agent-HTML. It is a real repository and artifact system for authoring semantic .agent.html documents and compiling them into stable HTML artifacts for dense human-agent work. Its own framing is “stable, shareable HTML artifacts” with a schema acting as the public contract. That makes it highly relevant on the artifact axis, even though it is much less developed on the selective hydration axis. citeturn35view0

A subtler direct precedent comes from established technical-publishing patterns: topic maps, manifests, and alternate representations. Even where those systems were not designed for LLMs, they embody the same structural ideas: a bounded package, explicit internal organization, and machine-readable discovery separate from visual rendering. That does not collapse the SHKO idea into old publishing tech, but it does mean the concept should be framed as agent-era reuse of older packaging ideas, not as something created from nothing.

What is strongly adjacent but not equivalent

llms.txt is real and useful, but it is a site-level discovery index, not a semantic region map for a bounded object. The proposal says it should provide brief background information, guidance, and links to markdown files, with the goal of helping LLMs use a website at inference time. That is close to the discovery layer the uploaded documents want, but it is intentionally flat and coarse. It does not specify internal node boundaries, dependencies, token budgets, or hydration addresses for a single complex artifact. citeturn20view0turn20view1

AGENTS.md is also real and important, but it solves a different problem. Its official site describes it as a simple open format for guiding coding agents, a predictable place for setup commands, build steps, tests, and conventions, with nearest-file precedence for monorepos. That is a control-plane and repo-onboarding pattern, not a knowledge-object pattern. The relationship is motivational, not substitutive. citeturn27view0

MCP and Agent Skills are adjacent for the opposite reason: they are capability-centric rather than knowledge-centric. MCP standardizes tools, resources, prompts, and capability negotiation among hosts, clients, and servers. Agent Skills package domain guidance and references that an agent reads on demand when building or using MCP systems. Both demonstrate progressive disclosure and structured discovery, but neither is a passive, bounded, primarily human-readable knowledge artifact. citeturn25view0turn26view0

What is weakly adjacent or often conflated

WebMCP is real, but it is solving a neighboring problem: exposing browser-side tools via a JavaScript API. Its draft says web applications can register tools through navigator.modelContext, effectively acting like client-side MCP servers. That is about browser-native tool exposure. It does not by itself define a document knowledge-package format. The uploaded documents often treat WebMCP as if it already solved or proved the SHKO concept; that overstates the connection. Also important: WebMCP belongs to the Web Machine Learning Community Group, while the browser-for-AI-agents work on SOM/AWP lives in a different W3C Community Group. Several uploaded claims blur those lines. citeturn28view0turn10view0

BAML belongs in the tooling layer unless and until stronger evidence says otherwise. The uploaded documents repeatedly use it as if it were a core substrate contender. The stronger reading is that it may be useful for schema-constrained extraction, validation, and mutation around the artifact, not as the artifact format itself. In this synthesis, the operational role of BAML remains plausible, but the headline efficiency claims in the uploaded documents remain unsupported.

Set-of-Mark and vision-first browser agent work are also weakly adjacent. They matter when the task is screen navigation or spatial grounding, but they are not the right abstraction for authored, text-first knowledge artifacts.

Technical substrate analysis

The most plausible architecture is a dual-surface design. Human readers get a normal document. Agents get a semantic map first, and only then the selected regions. The two strongest variants are:

HTML plus embedded manifest: a single file that contains the human-readable body plus a non-rendering manifest, likely in a <script type="application/json"> or <script type="application/ld+json"> block. This is the best option for offline portability, simple sharing, and “one artifact” ergonomics.

HTML plus sidecar semantic map: a canonical human document plus a separate machine-readable map, linked by <link> metadata, a predictable filename, or HTTP headers. This is cleaner operationally, easier to rebuild, and better for selective fetch over networks, but it requires stronger drift controls.

A third strong option exists for web deployment:

Canonical URL plus negotiated alternate representation: serve HTML to humans, but let agents request a semantic profile or compiled representation using link relations and profile/content negotiation. W3C’s Content Negotiation by Profile Working Draft and RFC 6906 make this shape technically plausible. The downside is that negotiation is a delivery mechanism, not by itself the authoring or packaging model. citeturn8view2turn8view3turn10view0

The weaker options are easier to dismiss. HTML-only is too noisy unless compiled down before model consumption. RDFa and Microdata are attractive because they are “web-native,” but they force agent parsers back through the full DOM and inflate authoring burden. XML/DITA-style source is powerful as an authoring backend, but too heavy to be the exchange primitive most independent builders will adopt. EPUB-like archives are excellent for bounded packages but too awkward as the first public experiment unless offline distribution is the main goal. SOM-like flat object models are excellent compiled views for interactive web pages, but a poor primary authoring substrate for human-owned knowledge objects.

The best near-term recommendation is therefore: author in semantic HTML or Markdown-to-HTML, compile a semantic map, and expose that map either embedded or as a sidecar.

The YAML-equivalent question

The uploaded documents are right that this is a central design question. In Skills and agent files, YAML usually carries discovery metadata, trigger hints, type declarations, and control-plane information. The equivalent layer here must do at least seven jobs: identify the artifact, enumerate semantic regions, summarize region purpose, express dependencies, estimate hydration cost, provide retrieval addresses, and record provenance/version/hash state.

The best candidates are not symmetrical.

<head> metadata is helpful for artifact-level discovery, but too small and awkward for a full region map. Link headers and rel="profile" are good for discovery and negotiation, but not for the map itself. RDFa and Microdata are seductive because they look web-native, but they are the wrong center of gravity for a selective-hydration system; they push the agent back into inline DOM parsing. The strongest choices are either an embedded JSON manifest or a sidecar JSON manifest, optionally wrapped in JSON-LD when you want Web-facing semantics or discoverability. JSON-LD is useful if the artifact needs to participate in wider linked-data ecosystems; plain JSON is usually better if the goal is precise internal region addressing and validation. citeturn20view0turn8view2turn8view3turn8view0

A good discovery/control layer should therefore contain: artifact ID; title; source and canonical URL; version; status; authorship or authority; digest or section hashes; list of region IDs; region titles/types/summaries; dependency edges; estimated token cost; reformattable retrieval addresses; and cache or freshness hints. If the artifact is networked, profile hints and alternate-representation links should live in HTTP and <head>; if the artifact is local or shared as a file, the embedded map should carry enough data to work offline.

CSS, BAML, and the DOM question

CSS deserves a more serious answer than “it is only for styling.” CSS selectors can be a useful runtime addressing and extraction grammar. They become much safer when anchored to stable id values or data-* attributes, and much weaker when they depend on framework-generated classes. :has() and combinators may be useful in implementations that need expressive region selection. But CSS should be treated as a dereference hint, not as the semantic substrate. The standard should assign stable node IDs first, then optionally include selector or fragment fallbacks. CSS custom properties as carrier metadata would be architectural overreach. The cascade is inspirational for disclosure policy, but not a good normative foundation for artifact semantics. citeturn13view2turn11view0

The DOM should also not be the main model-facing representation unless the document is tiny or the runtime has no compiler. The best current evidence points toward a compiled semantic map: SOM exists precisely because raw DOM is too noisy and expensive for agent consumption, and the W3C browser-for-agents group is explicitly motivated by that same problem. The right split is: humans read the document; the runtime compiles a semantic map; the model consumes the map first and hydrates body regions second. The DOM remains the backing source of truth and the fallback extraction layer, not the default reasoning substrate. citeturn10view0turn11view0turn13view2turn13view1

Uncertainty, Risks, and Recommended Next Step

Known knowns

There is now high-confidence evidence for four things. First, agent ecosystems are converging on explicit discovery/control layers such as AGENTS.md, llms.txt, MCP registries, and Skills. Second, long-context handling and tool selection quality remain real bottlenecks; the evidence around MCP tool descriptions is especially strong. Third, there is real momentum toward agent-facing semantic web representations, especially around SOM and the W3C community-group work. Fourth, governance and ecosystem institutions are beginning to form around these patterns, as shown by AAIF stewardship of AGENTS.md and MCP. citeturn27view0turn20view0turn25view0turn26view0turn14academia1turn10view0

Known unknowns

The biggest unresolved question is not whether the idea is conceptually elegant. It is whether it beats simpler alternatives in practice. The evidence base does not yet show that a manifest-driven knowledge artifact improves task success, latency, and token use enough to justify its complexity over: careful Markdown authoring, llms.txt plus markdown mirrors, or ordinary indexed documentation. The AGENTS.md literature is a warning here: more context structure can help, but it can also hurt. Likewise, better MCP descriptions improve success in one study while increasing execution steps substantially. citeturn23academia0turn22academia4turn23academia1turn14academia1

A second known unknown is drift control. Any system that separates summary/index from body risks semantic mismatch. That is manageable if the artifact is compiled and validated, but fatal if the map is hand-maintained. A third known unknown is addressability granularity: where exactly should semantic node boundaries sit for real reasoning tasks? Paragraphs are too small for many tasks; whole sections may be too large. That needs experiments, not vocabulary debates.

Unknown unknowns

The most important unknown unknown is whether the agent ecosystem will converge on artifact-centric or protocol-centric solutions. The current landscape arguably points both ways. Another is whether model vendors will increasingly solve this at the runtime level, making authored artifacts less important than compiled semantic services. A third is whether prompt caching, streaming retrieval, and agent-native search will erase enough of the context-economics problem that this proposal becomes less valuable than it seems today.

Major risks and objections

The hardest objection is overengineering. A lot of value may already be obtainable through good Markdown, llms.txt, clean headings, hashes, and simple section IDs. If the prototype only marginally beats those baselines, the concept should be abandoned or narrowed.

The next major risk is semantic drift between manifest and body. This is manageable only if the semantic map is machine-generated, section-hashed, and validated on every build. Closely related is misleading summary risk: if the map under-describes an important node, the agent may never hydrate it.

Latency is real. Selective hydration trades prompt mass for more fetches and orchestration. The MCP description paper is a useful analog here: better discovery information can improve outcomes while increasing step count. That means the artifact needs sensible chunk sizing, dependency prefetch, and perhaps coarse-to-fine hydration modes. citeturn14academia1

Security is also first-order. The MCP spec is explicit that tool descriptions and behavior annotations should be treated as untrusted unless obtained from a trusted server. The same mindset applies here: region summaries, dependency metadata, and embedded instructions are potential prompt-injection surfaces. The artifact must separate descriptive summaries from operative instructions, and agents must treat all untrusted maps as evidence to verify, not truth to obey. WebMCP’s permissions and registration rules underscore the same general lesson: browser- or agent-facing interfaces need explicit trust boundaries and origin controls. citeturn25view0turn28view0

Finally, there is a standards risk. The W3C and Schema.org work is promising, but still draft-level and proposal-level. Starting with a “standard” now would likely freeze the idea before the right abstraction is known. citeturn28view0turn10view0turn8view0

Recommended next step

The minimum credible public contribution is a small GitHub repository that combines a concept memo, a draft schema, a CLI, and a tiny artifact corpus. Not a foundation. Not an RFC. Not a big essay alone.

The CLI should do four things:

  1. inspect: print artifact metadata and list semantic nodes.
  2. hydrate <node-id>: retrieve the chosen region and any declared dependencies.
  3. validate: verify node IDs, hashes, selectors or anchors, and manifest-body consistency.
  4. compile: generate the semantic map from authored HTML or Markdown-rendered HTML.

The initial experiment should compare at least four baselines on the same tasks: plain Markdown, llms.txt plus markdown pages, raw DOM or HTML extraction, and your proposed artifact format. Measure answer accuracy, token use, latency, missed-region rate, and robustness under document edits. Include at least three artifact types: a runbook, a product or API spec, and a policy or governance document. If the artifact format does not show a compelling win on at least one class of task, stop. If it does, then publish a broader research memo and only later a draft vocabulary.
