Candidate terminology

SHKO is descriptive but too mechanism-heavy. It locks in “selective hydration” as the defining brand before the implementation is settled.

Context Surface is the best provisional public term. It is short, does not sound like a standard already exists, and correctly suggests an agent first sees a compact surface before entering the body. Its weakness is that it is slightly vague.

Agent-Addressable Knowledge Object is probably the most precise engineering label, but it is clunky and unlikely to stick in ordinary use.

Hyper-Knowledge Object should probably be dropped. It sounds grander than the evidence warrants and risks collapsing into vague “hypermedia for agents” rhetoric.

My recommendation is to defer final naming until after a prototype exists. In public writing today, the safest phrase is either “context surface” or the fully descriptive phrase “agent-addressable knowledge artifact with selective hydration.” That keeps the concept legible while avoiding premature branding.

Provenance: Gemini research document, 2025

The Selectively Hydratable Knowledge Object: A New Agentic Artifact Primitive

Architectural Conception and the Core Problem Space

The integration of artificial intelligence agents into software engineering, knowledge management, and enterprise workflows has exposed a structural bottleneck in web and document architectures. Currently, agents must choose between two suboptimal methods for ingesting documentation: monolithic context injection or lossy vector-based retrieval.

In monolithic injection, the agent loads entire documents or codebases into its active context window. While this preserves logical cohesion and structural relationships, it incurs severe token costs and degrades agent performance due to the cognitive load of processing irrelevant content.

Conversely, vector-based retrieval segments documents into arbitrary text chunks and retrieves them via embedding similarity. While this reduces the active token footprint, it strips away the document’s hierarchy, structural relationships, and logical flow, often causing the agent to miss crucial context.

To resolve this trade-off, this paper proposes a new agent-facing artifact primitive: the Selectively Hydratable Knowledge Object (SHKO). The SHKO is a dual-use, self-contained, offline-first document format. It remains richly readable to humans—ideally rendered as semantic HTML—while exposing a highly optimized, machine-readable manifest or map.

This map allows an agent to understand the document's layout, semantic boundaries, and node dependencies without loading the underlying content into its prompt. Based on its active reasoning path, the agent can then selectively "hydrate" only the precise sections or data nodes required for its immediate execution step.

The mathematical formulation of this selective hydration mechanism balances context-aware information utility against active token overhead. Let the total document corpus be represented as a set of discrete semantic nodes:

The agent initially ingests only the lightweight manifest, denoted as , which contains metadata for each node, including token sizes , dependency vectors, and semantic summaries. For an active reasoning task, the agent identifies a subset of target nodes to hydrate:

The structural hydration ratio, representing the optimization of the active context window, is defined as:

The design objective of the SHKO is to minimize while ensuring that the semantic utility of the hydrated subset remains functionally equivalent to ingesting the complete document set . This balances human readability with machine efficiency.

Comparative Taxonomy of Existing and Adjacent Paradigms

The proposed SHKO operates in a distinct architectural layer compared to emerging agentic, visual, and documentation standards. To demonstrate its unique value, it is compared below with pre-existing, adjacent, and classic systems.

Skills

Skills package reusable procedures, operational capabilities, and tool-execution strategies for agents. They typically utilize YAML metadata for discovery, Markdown for natural-language instructions, and on-demand local or remote execution hooks.

While Skills focus on operational capabilities (actions the agent can perform), the SHKO is strictly a knowledge packaging format. It does not run code or expose active API tool endpoints, thereby avoiding the safety, sandbox execution, and portability challenges inherent to executable skills.

Model Context Protocol (MCP)

The Model Context Protocol (MCP) defines an open client-server architecture that allows agents to access external data sources, developer tools, and API resources. While highly effective at structuring queries, MCP requires an active server connection to negotiate data transfers.

The SHKO, by contrast, is a passive, portable, and static file format. It can be version-controlled via Git, stored locally on a disk, or transferred as a single file, functioning independently of a live client-server transport protocol.

AGENTS.md

The AGENTS.md file format serves as an onboarding and configuration manual for coding agents within a repository. It is discovered automatically by agentic frameworks and provides tech stack overviews, build guidelines, and operational boundaries.

However, empirical research shows that monolithic AGENTS.md files often degrade task success rates when they grow too complex. Auto-generated or overly detailed files increase inference costs by over 20% and trigger reasoning failures as agents get distracted by irrelevant rules.

The SHKO directly addresses this context-bloat vulnerability by replacing static, all-or-nothing file loading with progressive, manifest-driven node hydration.

Set-of-Mark (SoM)

Set-of-Mark (SoM) prompting overlays alphanumeric visual tags and segmentation masks onto graphical user interfaces or images. This visual-spatial grounding technique helps multimodal models associate coordinate spaces with discrete textual tags.

While SoM addresses visual grounding on a screen, the SHKO focuses on textual and structural grounding within a document, enabling non-visual language models to navigate complex information architectures.

Agent-HTML

Agent-HTML structures agent outputs into semantic, portable HTML documents governed by strict schemas (using attributes like style-ref and elements like \<meta-agent\>). It restricts agents from writing raw layout classes or presentation markup, focusing instead on structural metadata.

While Agent-HTML enforces strict constraints on writing structured documents, it does not provide an input-side mechanism for agents to selectively hydrate fragments of large documents on demand.

Specmint

Specmint converts ephemeral plans into persistent, Git-diffable Markdown or HTML specifications within a .specs/ directory. It manages task states using active markers (such as ← current) and region comment sentinels (for example, \<\!-- region: ... \--\>).

Specmint is highly specialized for tracking active development tasks and maintaining state across coding sessions. However, it is not designed to scale as a generalized, high-performance knowledge representation format for multi-document enterprise corpora.

LLM Wiki Systems (Semiont)

Enterprise semantic wikis, such as Semiont, utilize W3C Web Annotations, event-sourced storage, and graph databases to collaboratively organize unstructured documents. Semiont’s GATHER flow programmatically constructs context windows by querying relational neighborhoods and extracting targeted text passages.

However, these systems require a complex, multi-container infrastructure (including PostgreSQL, Neo4j, Qdrant, and background workers) to function. The SHKO packages these semantic compilation benefits into a single, offline, serverless file.

Feature / Metric SHKO Skills MCP AGENTS.md SoM Agent-HTML Specmint Semiont

Substrate Hybrid HTML/JSON-LD YAML & Markdown JSON-RPC Protocol Monolithic Markdown Tagged Images Constrained HTML Markdown/HTML Distributed DBs

Technical Substrates and Architectural Feasibility

Designing a selectively hydratable knowledge object requires selecting a substrate that balances human readability with machine navigability. This section evaluates several potential substrates.

HTML5 and DOM-Derived Semantic Representations

Embedding human-readable text directly in native HTML5 ensures universal compatibility with web browsers and legacy scraping tools. Under this model, an agent uses standard Document Object Model (DOM) selectors (such as querySelector) or XML Path Language (XPath) queries to target specific block-level container tags (like \<section id="sec-01"\>).

This keeps human formatting separate from machine metadata, allowing browsers to render the document while agents parse its structural tree. However, raw HTML can contain verbose style classes, tracking scripts, and layout markup that bloats the context window during hydration.

JSON Sidecars and JSON-LD/RDFa/Microdata

Instead of parsing raw HTML, structural metadata can be serialized using standard web semantics:

  • JSON-LD: Serializes linked data in a dedicated script block (such as \<script type="application/ld+json"\>). This format is highly recommended by search engines, as it isolates structured metadata from rendering markup.
  • Microdata: Inserts structured attributes (such as itemscope, itemtype, and itemprop) directly into visible HTML tags. This keeps metadata close to the rendered content, but it bloats the HTML and complicates template updates.
  • RDFa: Uses inline semantic attributes based on RDF graph principles. While powerful, RDFa is highly verbose and complex to maintain outside academic or specialized environments.

XML and Structured Authoring (DITA Maps)

The technical writing industry has long solved the progressive assembly problem using the Darwin Information Typing Architecture (DITA). DITA decomposes documents into granular, topic-oriented XML files (covering tasks, concepts, and reference items). These modular topics are organized and assembled on demand using a separate, high-level structural document called a DITA map.

DITA maps support conditional processing (using attributes like @deliveryTarget) and dynamic chunking (using the @chunk attribute) to compile tailored outputs. While DITA provides a mature precedent for selective hydration, its dependency on verbose XML schemas, complex processors, and heavy enterprise transformation chains (such as DITA-OT) makes it poorly suited for modern, fast-paced agentic pipelines.

EPUB and Publication Manifest

The W3C Publication Manifest standardizes how a collection of web resources is packaged into a single logical work. Using a JSON-LD manifest context, it lists primary documents in a strict readingOrder and auxiliary assets in a resources block to define clear boundaries.

EPUB extends this by packaging these resources into a zipped archive containing HTML chapters, CSS stylesheets, and metadata files. While highly effective for packaging book-length content, the multi-file, zipped structure of EPUB makes it too heavy for simple, lightweight agent interactions.

CSS as a Selective Rule/Selector Layer

CSS selectors are widely supported by parsing libraries across all major programming languages. This makes CSS highly effective as a selector layer for the SHKO.

An agent can read a CSS-compatible selector map from the manifest and use it to extract targeted DOM nodes (such as .shko-node\[data-id="sec-auth"\]) using standard, low-overhead library calls. This avoids the need to invent a proprietary querying language.

BAML as a Schema-Generation Layer

Boundary AI Markup Language (BAML) provides a domain-specific language for defining structured, type-safe LLM function contracts. It is highly token-efficient, utilizing typescript-like schema definitions that consume up to 4x fewer tokens than verbose JSON schemas.

Furthermore, BAML's Schema-Aligned Parsing (SAP) algorithm is highly resilient, extracting structured data even from malformed, conversational, or truncated text blocks.

Integrating BAML into the SHKO toolchain allows the system to dynamically convert Pydantic models into BAML types, validate hydrated node data at runtime, and generate type-safe clients across multiple languages (including Python, TypeScript, Go, and Rust).

HTTP/Web Protocol Approaches: Profile and Content Negotiation

At the web protocol layer, the SHKO can leverage standard Content Negotiation and Web Linking frameworks to manage remote hydration.

  • Profile Links (RFC 6906\): Defines the profile link relation type (rel="profile"), allowing clients to identify additional structural or semantic constraints associated with a resource representation without altering its base media type. This allows clients to process the same resource differently depending on which profiles they support.
  • Accept-Profile and Content-Profile Headers: Standardizes how clients request, and servers indicate, specific structural schemas or application profiles during HTTP exchanges.
  • Link Headers: Uses Link headers to point clients to alternate representations of a resource (for instance, linking a human-facing HTML page directly to its machine-readable JSON-LD manifest).

Under this protocol-compliant model, an agent can query an SHKO resource over HTTP and negotiate exactly what it receives using standard headers:

The server intercepts this request, processes the document, and returns only the JSON-LD manifest block. Once the agent determines which node it needs, it issues a subsequent targeted request:

The server parses the document and streams back only the requested HTML node, minimizing network overhead and protecting the agent's context window.

Ecosystem Convergence and Empirical Evidence

The demand for a selectively hydratable knowledge primitive is evidenced by several convergent trends across the AI and document-processing ecosystems.

Progressive Disclosure in Multi-Objective Agentic Optimizers

In complex, long-context data processing tasks, forcing an agent to process all instructions, schemas, and directives simultaneously degrades output quality. To mitigate this, state-of-the-art query optimizers (such as the MOAR optimizer used in DocETL) have adopted progressive disclosure techniques from human-computer interaction.

Initially, the agent is exposed only to a high-level index of operator names and brief descriptions. The full specification—including detailed rules, instantiation schemas, and few-shot examples—is loaded on demand only when the agent selects that specific operator. This progressive design directly mirrors the selective hydration pattern of the SHKO.

The Rise of the Agent Web

The internet is splitting into a human web optimized for visual engagement and an agent web built for structured, machine-to-machine logic. To support this transition, infrastructure providers are introducing on-the-fly content transformation.

For example, Cloudflare can intercept HTTP requests from AI user agents and dynamically convert verbose HTML into clean, readable Markdown. Additionally, the widespread adoption of llms.txt files (acting as modern robots.txt files for AI) shows an urgent industry move toward exposing clean, machine-navigable indexes alongside human-facing sites.

Region Sentinel Markers in Coding Specs

Modern coding tools are converging on structured, file-based specifications to maintain context across sessions. Specmint’s HTML plugin demonstrates that HTML specs can remain fully editable by agents without human co-authors needing to touch the markup.

This is achieved by using HTML comment-based region sentinels (such as \<\!-- region: ... \--\> and \<\!-- endregion \--\>). This proven mechanism allows agents to target and edit precise blocks of a document surgically, demonstrating the feasibility of using structured comments as semantic boundaries in human-readable documents.

Technical Challenges and Strategic Objections

While the SHKO offers significant architectural benefits, implementing it across diverse environments introduces several critical technical challenges.

The Semantic Drift Problem

The most severe threat to the SHKO is semantic drift between the machine-readable manifest and the visible human-readable content. Because the JSON-LD metadata block and the HTML layout are decoupled, developers might update the visible text (such as altering a database schema description) without regenerating the manifest's token counts, node summaries, or dependency mappings.

If an agent relies on an outdated manifest, it may skip a critical section because its summary falsely indicates the content is irrelevant. To prevent this, the SHKO must be treated as a compiled asset, where the manifest is automatically generated and validated by CI/CD pipelines whenever the underlying content changes.

The Latency-Token Trade-off and Network Overhead

Selective hydration replaces a single, high-token-cost operation with multiple, lower-token-cost operations. While this successfully protects the agent's context window, it introduces a severe latency penalty.

Every selective hydration request requires a discrete roundtrip between the agent coordinator and the file server or local filesystem. If an agent must crawl a document with numerous small, nested dependencies, the sequential network latency of fetching individual nodes can easily eclipse the cost-savings of loading a slightly larger monolithic context in a single shot. This is represented by the total context cost equation:

where is the base system prompt token count, is the token size of the navigation manifest, is the set of selectively hydrated nodes, is the token size of node , and represents the latency and compute overhead associated with each individual hydration network request. If the overhead is high, or if the cardinal size of the hydrated set is large, selective hydration becomes less efficient than monolithic context loading.

Prompt Cache Invalidation

Modern LLM APIs utilize prompt caching to reduce latency and costs for repetitive queries. Prompt caching relies on strict prefix matching; the cache remains valid only as long as the initial sequence of tokens in the prompt remains identical.

If an agent dynamically hydrates different subsets of nodes () across sequential reasoning steps, the prompt prefix changes constantly, invalidating the prompt cache. This can paradoxically increase overall latency and inference costs compared to loading a single, static document that remains fully cached across multiple turns.

Architectural Boundaries and Do-Not-Overclaim Guidelines

To establish this primitive with scientific rigor, its operational boundaries must be clearly defined.

Not a High-Frequency Database

The SHKO is a passive document format. It is not designed to replace high-frequency, real-time databases or active transactional state stores. It is a static, write-once, read-many format suited for version-controlled knowledge bases, specifications, and reference manuals.

Not an Executable Runtime or Sandbox

Unlike Skills or custom agent scripts, the SHKO contains no executable code or active tool hooks. It is entirely declarative, serving as a structured target for reading and parsing. It does not run commands, spawn processes, or access networks.

Not a Global RAG Solution

The SHKO is designed to manage context within a discrete document or tightly bound document set. It does not replace global enterprise retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipelines, vector databases, or semantic knowledge graphs that scale across millions of disparate corporate files. Instead, it serves as an optimized input format for those systems.

Not a Visual Layout Framework

The SHKO is completely decoupled from visual design systems, CSS component frameworks (such as Tailwind or Shadcn UI), and presentation parameters. It enforces structural and semantic organization, leaving visual styling to the user agent or web browser.

Charter and Implementation Roadmap

Given the rapid development of the agent web, the establishment of a selectively hydratable knowledge primitive is highly valuable. To introduce this concept to the community effectively, a structured, multi-stage implementation roadmap is proposed.

The Initial Release Strategy

Rather than publishing a theoretical essay or a heavy, complex database platform, the most effective first step is to release a public GitHub repository containing a formal specification (RFC Draft) alongside a working Schema and a lightweight CLI parser.

Developers in the AI engineering space prioritize immediate utility and running code. Providing a clear schema backed by a functional, offline-first CLI tool allows developers to experiment with the format instantly in their local workspaces.

Proposed Repository Structure: agent-shko

agent-shko/ ├── RFC-01-SHKO-SPEC.md \# The formal concept specification and HTTP headers ├── schema/ │ ├── shko.jsonld \# The base JSON-LD vocabulary context definition │ └── manifest.schema.json \# JSON Schema for validating SHKO manifests ├── cli/ │ ├── shko-parser/ \# Rust or TypeScript-based parsing engine │ │ ├── src/ │ │ │ ├── parser.rs \# Implements selector-based node extraction │ │ │ └── server.rs \# Implements a local content-negotiating server │ │ └── package.json ├── examples/ │ ├── system-spec.html \# Exemplar specification utilizing HHD pattern │ └── api-docs.html \# Exemplar API documentation with nested nodes └── README.md \# Quick-start guide and tool integrations

Actionable Implementation Steps

Phase 1: Standardize the Vocabulary

Define the base schema context (shko.jsonld) to standardize key vocabulary terms:

  • SelectivelyHydratableObject: The core class identifier.
  • manifest: The container object for structural metadata.
  • nodes: An array of individual hydratable sections.
  • selector: The CSS or JSONPath query used to isolate a node's content.
  • dependsOn: An array of node IDs representing structural or logical dependencies.

Phase 2: Develop the CLI Parser and Local Server

Build a lightweight, high-performance CLI tool (written in Rust or TypeScript) that provides two core utilities:

  1. shko inspect \<file\>: Parses the document's header, extracts the JSON-LD manifest, and prints a compact visual tree of available nodes, their token sizes, and their dependencies.
  2. shko hydrate \<file\> \--node \<id\>: Uses fast, stream-based DOM selection to extract only the text content of the target node, completely stripped of styling tags, tracking scripts, and visual clutter.

Additionally, build a simple local server mode within the CLI that supports profile-based content negotiation. This server should process standard Accept-Profile headers and return either the raw manifest or a specific hydrated node dynamically.

Phase 3: Community Integration and Tooling Alignment

Integrate the SHKO parser with existing developer workflows:

  • Claude Code / Cursor Plugins: Develop local skills or plugins that allow terminal-based coding agents to automatically detect and progressively hydrate local SHKO files.
  • BAML Integrations: Create a BAML-driven extraction helper that automatically parses raw text from hydrated nodes into strongly typed application objects.

By releasing an open, lightweight, and standards-compliant framework, the developer community can transition away from costly, monolithic context-stuffing toward precise, on-demand agentic knowledge hydration.

Provenance: ChatGPT research document, 2025 — primary schema reference

Selectively Hydratable Agent-Readable Documents

Executive Judgment

The strongest surviving thesis is not “put a manifest next to HTML,” “reuse content negotiation,” or “serve everything through MCP.” It is narrower and more defensible: there appears to be a real gap at the document/artifact layer for a human-readable document that also publishes a compact, machine-readable map of its own internal semantic regions, so an agent can inspect what exists, estimate reading cost, and selectively load only the needed sections. The closest prior art I found clusters around hierarchical document retrieval and progressive disclosure—especially Prompt-RAG, RAPTOR, PageIndex, Anthropic Agent Skills, and OpenAI Codex Skills—but none of those, as currently documented, standardizes a portable, public-facing, document-native artifact that combines stable section identity, explicit section summaries, cost hints, and selective hydration mechanics in a way that is independent of a specific runtime. That gap is plausible and worth prototyping, but it is not yet proven to be important enough to standardize. citeturn27view0turn27view1turn27view2turn22view4turn27view3turn20view3

The most defensible architecture is a dual-representation model: one source document model that can emit both an embedded representation for portability/local/offline use and a discoverable sidecar manifest for web deployment and runtime efficiency. That preserves the “single artifact” intuition without forcing the open web to depend on the operational fragility of server-driven content negotiation. On the public web, the sidecar form is the better default. For local files, attachments, and versioned artifacts, embedded metadata remains important. HTTP content negotiation should stay optional, not foundational. MCP Resources should be treated as an adapter and transport substrate, not the primitive itself. citeturn38view3turn38view2turn11search1turn10search9turn38view1turn20view3turn20view5

What should be abandoned is the idea that the concept is best framed as transclusion, “lighter DITA,” Shadow-DOM-based sectioning, or a browser-automation workflow manifest. DITA and EPUB provide genuinely useful lineage—maps separate from content, topic summaries, chunking, reading order, packaging—but their value here is as source of primitives and cautions, not as ready-made models to revive wholesale. Likewise, WebMCP, VOIX, and the current AI Manifest draft are mainly about action affordances and browser/UI execution; they do not solve the reading/navigation artifact problem. citeturn15search3turn15search1turn17search1turn17search2turn17search12turn7search0turn32search1turn27view5turn27view7turn27view6

The strongest version of the concept, after adjudication, is this:

An agent-addressable document is a human-readable document plus a compact section map that exposes stable semantic section identifiers, short section summaries, retrieval pointers, and pre-hydration size hints, enabling an agent to inspect and selectively hydrate the document without first loading the whole artifact into active context. Fields like per-section hashes are promising and probably useful; dependency edges are interesting but currently speculative. citeturn22view4turn27view3turn25view4turn25view0turn15search3turn17search1

Cross-Document Synthesis and Architecture Decision Memo

Cross-document synthesis. The nine uploaded briefs, treated skeptically, largely converge on one shared argument: the missing piece is not merely metadata, transport, or retrieval, but a document-level progressive-disclosure primitive. Their strongest common insight survives validation: established systems already show fragments of the pattern—hierarchical summaries in research retrieval, “load name/description first, full payload later” in Skills ecosystems, maps/manifests in publishing systems, and explicit resource listing in MCP—but these pieces are split across different layers and use cases. What does not survive is any strong claim that one existing carrier or runtime has already solved the whole problem. The most defensible revision is that the unbuilt gap is a portable document artifact that combines stable sub-document identity, author-visible summaries, cost hints before read, and selective hydration mechanics. The case for content hashes is strong enough to keep in the conversation, especially for sidecar deployments and cache/drift validation, but not strong enough to make mandatory in v0. The case for dependency edges does not yet survive as core. citeturn27view1turn27view2turn22view4turn20view3turn15search3turn17search1turn18search0

Candidate A: single self-contained HTML with embedded manifest. This is the best form for local/offline artifacts, attachments, archival snapshots, and “one file that carries everything.” JSON-LD is technically feasible here: JSON-LD 1.1 explicitly defines embedding in HTML using a script element with type="application/ld+json", and the registered media type allows an optional profile parameter under RFC 6906 semantics. But feasibility is not adoption. I found no primary-source evidence that major agent runtimes routinely consume arbitrary custom JSON-LD manifests embedded in HTML the way they consume skills, tools, or schema.org-specific structured data. So JSON-LD is a valid carrier, but not a compelling default. If linked-data interoperability matters, support JSON-LD as an alternate serialization; do not make it normative for v0. Embedded single-file atomicity is a real advantage, but—on the open web—fewer systems will benefit from it than from an addressable sidecar. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn30search0turn20view2turn20view3turn27view5

Candidate B: canonical HTML document plus sidecar manifest resource. This is the best operational default for public deployment. Explicit companion resources are a normal web pattern; the Web Application Manifest standard itself uses a linked manifest resource, and link relations are a tractable discovery mechanism. A sidecar avoids the main weakness of embedding: to inspect the map, a client may otherwise have to fetch the full document body first. It also improves independent caching, invalidation, CDN handling, and structured diffing. The cost is that “single artifact” becomes “single source model, two emitted representations.” That is an acceptable trade. A single authoring source can—and should—emit both forms. citeturn38view1turn38view0turn18search0

Candidate C: HTTP content/profile negotiation as primary deployment. This loses. HTTP absolutely supports server-driven negotiation, and W3C has published Content Negotiation by Profile as a Working Draft, while JSON-LD registers a profile parameter for application/ld+json. But RFC 9110 also spells out strong disadvantages to proactive negotiation, including implementation complexity and reduced cache reusability, and major CDN documentation makes the cache cost concrete: Fastly explicitly warns that Vary: Accept can noticeably reduce cache performance, and Cloudflare generally does not honor arbitrary Vary values in caching decisions outside limited cases. This makes negotiation elegant in theory but fragile in routine deployment. Use it only as an optional server feature, not as the core artifact model. citeturn38view3turn38view2turn10search3turn11search1turn10search9

Candidate D: MCP Resources as carrier/runtime. The distinction the briefs make here is correct: MCP Resources solve protocol exposure, not the artifact problem. MCP Resources already provide resources/list, resources/read, resource templates, optional subscriptions, and hints like descriptions, sizes, audience, priority, and last-modified timestamps. The Skills-over-MCP work is explicit that its current direction is a resources-based extension, and the draft proposal treats MCP as a transport for skills rather than as the definition of skill/document content itself. Current client support is also incomplete: an October 2025 OpenAI Codex issue documented that resources exposed in the MCP handshake were not visible to the CLI agent, underscoring that protocol support does not automatically create usable document semantics. MCP is therefore an excellent adapter target for the proposed primitive, but not the primitive. citeturn20view3turn25view4turn25view0turn20view5turn26view2turn20view6

Candidate E: DITA / EPUB / structured-authoring lineage. This is the richest prior-art cluster. DITA is directly relevant because it already separates maps from topics, supports reusable references (conref), uses topic-oriented chunking strategies, and includes summary-like constructs such as shortdesc. EPUB is relevant because package documents separate metadata, manifest, and reading order, and because CFI demonstrates both the desire for deep fragment addressing and the cost of overly path-dependent addressing. The right takeaway is: keep map separate from content when useful, make topic summaries first-class, model stable topic identity, and let packaging/chunking be controlled rather than emergent. The wrong takeaway is to recreate a heavy XML authoring regime or a “lighter DITA” fork. Lightweight DITA’s status never progressed to an OASIS Standard; the public materials I found still point to a Committee Note lineage and an active but niche subcommittee/repository, so “stagnation” is a fair cautionary inference, not a proven dead end. EPUB CFI remains alive as a spec, but support for hyperlinking via CFI was removed as a reading-system requirement; that makes it more a cautionary tale than a core model to copy. citeturn15search3turn15search0turn15search1turn15search7turn17search1turn17search17turn17search2turn17search12turn17search0

Candidate F: hypermedia document framing. This framing is stronger than it first appears, but only if it is stated carefully. Fielding’s HATEOAS account is about action/state transitions, and later hypermedia work in multi-agent systems focuses on affordances and signifiers for acting. The missing argument in today’s agent tooling is about reading affordances: a document exposing enough machine-readable structure to let a generic reading client decide what to inspect next without prior schema-specific hardcoding. That is not identical to HATEOAS, but it is a plausible extension of hypermedia reasoning and probably the best essay-level frame available. The safe public claim is not “nobody has ever said this,” but rather “today’s discourse has focused much more on acting agents than on reading agents.” citeturn8search0turn8search16turn8search7turn8search11turn27view5turn27view7turn27view6

Recommendation. Choose Option 3: a source model that can emit both a single-file embedded artifact and a discoverable sidecar manifest. Make the document primitive the center, not the carrier or runtime. Treat HTML as the default human-readable body format, but keep the manifest schema media-type-agnostic enough that other source formats could emit the same map. Support MCP, local files, and browser clients as adapters. Reject content negotiation as the primary model; reject MCP-as-primitive; reject Shadow DOM, CFI-style addressing, and transclusion-first framing. citeturn29view0turn38view1turn38view3turn20view3turn7search0turn17search2

Claim Validation Table
