Prompt-RAG and PageIndex are among the nearest precedents for document navigation Transclusion; PageIndex brief Supported but incomplete Prompt-RAG paper; PageIndex repo/docs. citeturn27view0turn27view1 They are good precedents in spirit because they navigate structure instead of flat chunks, but neither is a portable, public document-manifest standard. RAPTOR belongs in the same family as a hierarchical retrieval precedent. citeturn27view2
Anthropic Agent Skills use progressive disclosure MCP brief; transclusion brief Verified by primary source Anthropic docs. citeturn22view4turn22view1 Anthropic documents that skill descriptions are loaded first and full content loads when invoked; supporting files load only when needed. That is a strong runtime precedent for progressive disclosure, but it is a skills/workflow format, not a general document primitive. citeturn22view1turn22view4turn23search0
No major system ships per-section token estimates + hashes + dependency edges in a portable artifact Multiple briefs Unverified Surveyed systems: MCP Resources, Agent Skills, PageIndex, llms.txt. citeturn25view4turn22view1turn27view1turn37view0 I found no documented mainstream system that combines all of these in a portable document artifact, but this is a universal negative and should not be stated as proven. At best: “not found in the systems surveyed.”
JSON-LD 1.1 defines embedding in HTML; application/ld+json has a profile parameter JSON-LD brief Verified by primary source W3C JSON-LD 1.1 and IANA registration. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn30search0 This is technically sound. If JSON-LD is used, application/ld+json;profile=... is preferable to inventing a new media type for a prototype.
No agent runtime routinely consumes arbitrary custom JSON-LD manifests in HTML JSON-LD brief Supported but incomplete Vendor docs surveyed for Skills/MCP/WebMCP/structured data. citeturn20view2turn20view3turn27view5turn19search11 I found no primary-source documentation showing routine consumption of arbitrary custom JSON-LD manifests by major agent runtimes. That is absence of evidence, not proof of non-use.
Full HTTP content negotiation and Vary: Accept have practical deployment problems HTTP brief Verified / strongly supported RFC 9110, Fastly docs, Cloudflare docs. citeturn38view3turn38view2turn11search1turn10search9 The abstract complaint is correct. The exact severity varies by stack, but the disadvantages are not speculative.
Parallel URL + Link header discovery is stronger in practice than negotiation HTTP brief Plausible inference RFC 9110, Web App Manifest, JSON-LD alternate-link behavior. citeturn38view3turn38view1turn29view0 This is not a formal theorem; it is the most operationally credible reading of how the web actually deploys companion metadata.
MCP Resources are real, but client support and semantics are incomplete MCP brief Verified by primary source MCP spec, Skills-over-MCP charter, Codex issue, rejected SEP-2093 proposal. citeturn20view3turn20view5turn20view6turn24search0turn24search3 Correct. MCP Resources are useful, but they do not yet imply portable section-manifest semantics or ubiquitous host support.
DITA shortdesc, conref, ditamaps, and chunking are relevant prior art DITA brief Verified by primary source OASIS DITA 1.3 materials. citeturn15search3turn15search0 Correct. DITA is among the strongest ancestors here.
Lightweight DITA’s stagnation warns against formal-authoring reinvention DITA brief Plausible inference LwDITA Committee Note status and subcommittee materials. citeturn15search1turn15search7 The caution is fair, but “stagnation” should be stated gently: LwDITA exists, but I did not find evidence of broad standards uptake comparable to classic DITA.
EPUB manifest/nav/spine are useful analogies; CFI is mostly cautionary EPUB brief Verified by primary source EPUB 3.3/3.4, EPUB CFI, EPUB changes/issues. citeturn17search1turn17search6turn17search2turn17search12turn17search0 Correct. Manifest and reading-order separation are useful lessons; CFI-style pathing is likely too brittle for a new document primitive.
llms.txt has a narrow useful niche but is oversold as a general-web standard llms.txt brief Supported but incomplete llmstxt.org proposal; third-party adoption/crawl studies; docs sites that explicitly use it. citeturn37view0turn37view1turn37view2turn20view3turn23search0 Fair. It is a proposal, not a standard. There is evidence of niche utility in docs discovery, but little evidence of broad platform reliance. Google non-support is reported by reputable trade press, not by a canonical public Google spec. citeturn37view3
Web Components / Shadow DOM are a poor fit for static-document agent manifests Web Components brief Verified / plausible MDN Shadow DOM docs and declarative-shadow-DOM docs. citeturn7search0turn32search1turn7search12 The core purpose of Shadow DOM is encapsulation and hidden internals. That conflicts with a public, stable, addressable section contract.
“Reading affordances” are a useful hypermedia extension, but not an established doctrine HTMX/hypermedia brief Plausible inference Fielding, htmx HATEOAS essay, signifiers paper. citeturn8search0turn8search16turn8search7turn8search11 Strong essay framing, but should not be marketed as a settled web-architecture category.
VOIX and the AI Manifest draft are adjacent action-affordance work, not section-manifest work HTMX brief; Web Components and HTTP briefs Verified by primary source VOIX paper; IETF AI Manifest draft; WebMCP spec. citeturn27view7turn27view6turn28view0turn27view5 Correct. These disclose actions and UI workflows, not reading/navigation structure for long-form documents.

Core Manifest Schema and Section Addressing

Core artifact design. The primitive should be defined as a document plus section map, not as a transport, crawler hint, or protocol extension. The minimum v0 needs only a small set of fields, but those fields should be chosen because they directly support the selective-hydration workflow: “inspect → choose → hydrate → validate.” A good v0 section entry needs a stable semantic identifier, a human legible title, a short summary, a retrieval pointer, and a pre-hydration size hint. Most of the surveyed systems that get closest to this pattern surface names, descriptions, resource sizes, or hierarchical trees; none requires the full bundle proposed in the briefs, which is why a tighter v0 is preferable. citeturn22view4turn27view3turn25view4turn27view1

A defensible v0 core is:

{
  "$schema": "…schema URI…",
  "manifest_version": "0.1",
  "document_id": "urn:uuid:… or canonical URL",
  "title": "Document title",
  "source": "canonical source URL or file URI",
  "sections": [
    {
      "id": "intro.problem",
      "title": "Problem statement",
      "summary": "What this section covers in 1–3 sentences.",
      "href": "#intro-problem",
      "char_count": 1842,
      "kind": "section",
      "children": [ ... ]
    }
  ]
}

That is enough to test the core behavior. summary should be human-authored where possible; “generated and reviewed” is acceptable; “generated and unchecked” should be treated as lower-trust metadata. char_count should be the required portable cost hint, with tokenizer-specific estimates optional. The schema should allow an optional token_estimates object keyed by tokenizer name, but it should not make token counts normative because tokenization varies across models and providers. citeturn20view2turn22view3turn22view1

The right status for other fields is more selective. content_hash is the strongest candidate for promotion after v0: it is genuinely useful for drift detection, cache validation, and reconciling sidecar metadata with changing body content. But section-level hashing also requires a stable extraction/canonicalization rule, so it is best marked recommended in v0.1, mandatory only after implementation experience. last_modified and source are also good optional fields. kind is useful if it stays lightweight—e.g. section, appendix, example, table, warning, api-reference—but do not over-ontology it. Existing systems like MCP already show the utility of lightweight metadata, size, and last-modified hints without requiring dense semantics. citeturn25view4turn25view0

depends_on does not belong in minimal v0. It is plausible that dependency edges could help an agent avoid misreading a dependent section in isolation, but I did not find strong prior-art evidence that they are a proven win for document hydration. The same is true of explicit preload policies beyond a simple boolean such as standalone: true|false. Keep this empirical: if the prototype later shows that “load prerequisites first” materially beats “follow parent/child structure plus summaries,” then add edges in a later revision. Likewise, avoid duplicating tree structure by storing both parent_id and children; pick one representation. A recursive children tree is simplest for authoring and inspection. citeturn27view1turn27view2turn22view4

Section addressing and hydration model. The canonical identity of a section should be a stable semantic section ID, authored or generated once and intended to survive presentation changes. In HTML deployments, the best physical retrieval address is still the boring one: URL plus fragment. HTML fragment identifiers are simple, inspectable, and already part of the web’s basic model. The manifest should therefore point first to id and href, where href usually resolves to #fragment or document.html#fragment. The runtime may use selectors internally to extract the node contents, but CSS selectors should be an implementation detail or optional hint, not the public contract. That is especially important because selector-based contracts drift with layout and styling decisions. EPUB CFI and XPath/XPointer-like schemes are too brittle and too editorially complex for a first public proposal. Text fragments and HTTP range requests are useful conveniences, but they should remain non-core. citeturn17search2turn17search12turn29view0turn7search0turn32search1

A good hydration rule is therefore:

  1. Resolve the section’s semantic id.
  2. Map it to the body node via fragment/DOM lookup.
  3. Extract the node’s canonical text or HTML excerpt.
  4. If a content_hash exists, verify the extracted section against it.
  5. If verification fails, warn and either re-index or fall back to a more conservative extraction mode.

That separation—semantic ID public, extraction mechanics private—is the lowest-risk model I found. citeturn29view0turn25view4

Relationship to Adjacent Systems and Design Principles

Comparison matrix.

System Classification Why it matters here
Agent Skills Adjacent prior art Strong precedent for progressive disclosure of instructions and supporting files; not a general document primitive. citeturn23search0turn22view1turn27view4
MCP Resources Deployment adapter Provides list/read/templates/subscriptions and metadata hints, but not document-internal section semantics. citeturn20view3turn25view4
SEP-2640 / Skills Over MCP Deployment adapter Shows one path for serving progressively disclosed skill content over MCP; draft extension, not final standard. citeturn20view5turn20view4
llms.txt Weak analogy Sidecar discovery file with human-written summaries and links, but no stable sub-document addressing or hydration contract. citeturn37view0turn20view3
Prompt-RAG Direct ancestor in spirit Hierarchical, non-vector retrieval over document structure; runtime method, not portable artifact. citeturn27view0
PageIndex Direct ancestor in spirit Table-of-contents tree plus reasoning-based retrieval over document sections. citeturn27view1
RAPTOR Adjacent prior art Hierarchical summarization/tree retrieval, but generated retrieval structure rather than authored document contract. citeturn27view2
JSON-LD / RO-Crate Adjacent prior art / carrier option Useful for graph metadata and packaged objects; not inherently a section hydration model. citeturn29view0turn33search12turn33search9
DITA Direct ancestor Topic maps, shortdesc, conref, chunking, map/content separation. citeturn15search3turn15search0
EPUB / Publication Manifest Adjacent prior art Manifest/nav/spine and packaged publication semantics; CFI is a caution. citeturn17search1turn18search0turn17search2
WebMCP Adjacent runtime Great for tool/action exposure in browsers, not reading/navigation for long-form documents. citeturn27view5turn9search18
VOIX Adjacent action-affordance work Declares actions and relevant state in HTML; focused on invokable capabilities. citeturn27view7
IETF AI Manifest Adjacent action-affordance work Embeds step-by-step UI workflows keyed to CSS selectors; different problem. citeturn27view6turn28view0
Agent-HTML / similar experiments Weak analogy Interesting artifact experiments, but not credible prior art for section-addressable selective reading. citeturn34search4

Design principles. Any prototype or public proposal should follow these principles.

  • Keep the primitive at the artifact layer. The document map should exist independently of MCP, browser APIs, or RAG plumbing. citeturn20view3turn20view5
  • Make semantic IDs public and stable. Retrieval mechanics can change; public section identity should not. citeturn17search2turn29view0
  • Prefer summaries written for routing, not marketing. The summary is navigation metadata, not SEO copy. Anthropic and OpenAI both make description quality central to skill selection. citeturn22view4turn27view3
  • Use portable cost hints first. Require char_count; allow tokenizer-specific estimates as optional overlays. citeturn20view2
  • Separate source model from deployment form. One source should emit both embedded and sidecar variants. citeturn38view1turn29view0
  • Treat selectors as adapters, not public API. Selector contracts drift; semantic IDs age better. citeturn7search0turn32search1
  • Prefer explicit discovery over clever negotiation. Linkable companion resources are more web-native operationally than mandatory server-driven negotiation. citeturn38view3turn38view1
  • Design for partial trust. Manifest poisoning and stale metadata are real risks; hashes and validation hooks matter. The AI Manifest draft is a good reminder that agent-facing metadata can become a security surface. citeturn28view1turn28view4
  • Do not mix reading affordances with action affordances. Reading maps and executable workflows are complementary but distinct layers. citeturn27view5turn27view7turn27view6
  • Do not over-formalize before utility is demonstrated. DITA’s power came with heavy complexity; LwDITA’s softer path never clearly became mainstream. citeturn15search3turn15search1

What not to build. Do not use Shadow DOM or Web Components as the primary section primitive; their core value is encapsulation, and declarative shadow DOM even rewrites the parse tree during HTML parsing. Do not make full HTTP content negotiation the normative deployment model; the cache and operational costs are real. Do not market this as “lighter DITA,” because the opportunity is narrower and more web-native than a new authoring framework. Do not frame the concept primarily as transclusion; transclusion is one compositional mechanism, not the central user value. Do not make MCP the primitive if the real novelty is document structure. Do not invent a new MIME type yet; use plain JSON plus schema URI, or JSON-LD plus profile, until there is evidence the concept deserves media-type standardization. And do not overclaim llms.txt as either universal success or universal failure; it has a narrow docs-index niche, but not broad validation as a general AI-web standard. citeturn7search0turn32search1turn38view3turn11search1turn15search1turn20view3turn29view1turn37view0turn37view1turn20view3turn23search0

Known Knowns, Known Unknowns, Risks, and Falsifiers

Known knowns. Progressive disclosure clearly works as a runtime pattern for agent workflows and knowledge packaging. Anthropic Agent Skills and OpenAI Codex Skills both document “description now, full file later” behavior. MCP Resources clearly provide a transport substrate for list/read/template/subscription patterns. DITA and EPUB clearly demonstrate that map/content separation and independent structural metadata are longstanding, real needs. HTTP proactive negotiation plainly has disadvantages for cacheability and deployment. Those pieces are established. citeturn22view4turn27view3turn20view3turn15search3turn17search1turn38view3turn11search1

Known unknowns. The proposal still depends on several empirical questions. First, does manifest-guided selective hydration actually beat simpler baselines like “just read the heading tree,” “use llms.txt-style summaries,” or “run a good long-context model over the whole page”? Second, does summary quality dominate everything else, making hashes and cost hints secondary? Third, are section-level hashes worth their complexity in ordinary use, or only in sidecar/cached deployments? Fourth, do dependency edges help materially, or does parent/child structure plus summaries already capture most of the benefit? Fifth, which open-web deployment is more usable in practice: embedded manifest, sidecar manifest, or both? Sixth, can generic models navigate these manifests reliably without heavy prompt scaffolding? These are the experiments that must decide whether the concept graduates from interesting architecture to necessary primitive. citeturn27view1turn22view1turn22view4turn25view4

Unknown unknowns and open questions. Hidden prior art may exist in annotation systems, digital publishing tools, IDE symbol indices, browser-reader modes, enterprise documentation platforms, or legal/policy content-management systems. Hidden failure modes may also emerge around prompt injection, manifest poisoning, ownership of summaries, governance of edits, multilingual section identity, cross-format canonicalization for hashes, and incentives for publishers to maintain the map. The current evidence base is also incomplete on two important negatives: whether some major agents already consume custom embedded metadata in undocumented ways, and whether section dependency graphs might matter more than current literature suggests. Those remain open. citeturn28view4turn20view6

Risks, objections, and what would falsify the idea. The strongest objection is that the idea may be overfitting a transient weakness in current agents. If long-context models and compression-aware browsing stacks make whole-document reading cheap and reliable, the extra authoring burden will not pay for itself. A second objection is that the problem may already be solved “well enough” by ordinary headings, summaries, and index pages. A third is adoption economics: unless agents demonstrably use the map, publishers will not maintain it. The concept should be treated as falsified if a benchmark shows little or no gain over strong baselines on token budget, answer quality, or latency; if authors cannot maintain high-quality section summaries at acceptable cost; or if the only workable implementations require product-specific runtime adapters so heavy that the “portable primitive” dissolves. citeturn38view3turn37view1turn27view1

Recommended Public Framing and Next Artifact

Public terminology. The strongest technical term is agent-addressable document. It is clearer than “hyper-knowledge object,” less magical than “self-hydrating document,” and less implementation-specific than “manifest-addressed document.” The strongest plain-English explanation is: “A document that tells an AI what sections it contains before the AI reads them, so the AI can load only the parts it needs.” The strongest essay framing is “hypermedia for reading agents.” That framing connects the idea to web architecture without pretending it is already a recognized web standard category. citeturn8search0turn8search16turn8search7

Recommended next artifact. The minimum credible next move is a staged sequence, not an RFC-style standardization push.

First, write a short position paper of roughly 5–8 pages. Its job is to separate the four layers cleanly:

  • Core artifact design: an agent-addressable document with selective hydration.
  • Transport/discovery layer: explicit sidecar discovery, optional embedded form, optional negotiation.
  • Runtime/adapters: MCP Resources adapter, browser helper, local CLI.
  • Public framing: “hypermedia for reading agents,” not “new markup language,” not “AI sitemap.”

Second, publish a small demo repo plus CLI. That prototype should take Markdown or semantic HTML as input and emit both forms from one source. Minimal commands:

  • build — generate semantic HTML plus manifest, with --embed and --sidecar outputs.
  • inspect — print the section tree with ids, summaries, counts, and optional hashes.
  • hydrate — fetch a section by semantic id or href.
  • validate — verify schema, section-extraction integrity, and optional hash drift.

The repo should include two example documents: one long narrative/research document and one highly structured reference document. It should also ship one MCP Resources adapter that exposes the same manifest and hydrated sections as MCP resources, proving that MCP is an adapter target rather than the source definition. citeturn20view3turn20view5turn26view2

Third, include one benchmark harness. Keep it humble. Compare four baselines on 10–20 tasks over the example docs:

  • Full-document load.
  • Heading-tree only.
  • Sidecar map with summaries and char_count.
  • Sidecar map plus optional hashes.

Score token/character budget, latency, answer accuracy, and citation/trace quality. If the map does not clearly beat the simple baselines, do not escalate the concept. If it does, then publish a small schema draft and a “why this is not llms.txt / not MCP / not DITA” explainer for developers. That is the realistic independent-builder path. citeturn37view0turn20view3turn22view4turn27view1

Annotated bibliography.

Standards and specifications. RFC 9110: HTTP Semantics. The most authoritative source for why proactive content negotiation is real but costly, and why Vary expands cache keys and can reduce reuse. Essential for rejecting negotiation as the primary deployment model. citeturn38view2turn38view3

JSON-LD 1.1 and the IANA application/ld+json registration. These establish that embedded JSON-LD in HTML is standards-compliant and that profile is an allowed media-type parameter for JSON-LD. Crucial for evaluating JSON-LD as a carrier without overclaiming adoption. citeturn29view0turn29view1turn30search0

Content Negotiation by Profile. Useful because it shows what “profile-aware negotiation” looks like in standards work, but note its exact status: W3C Working Draft, not Recommendation. citeturn10search3turn30search11

DITA 1.3. The strongest standards-grade structured-authoring ancestor for topic maps, summaries, reuse, and chunk control. citeturn15search3turn15search0

EPUB 3.3 / 3.4 and EPUB CFI. Important for package/manifest/spine lessons, and for showing both the appeal and drawbacks of deep fragment addressing. citeturn17search1turn17search6turn17search2turn17search12

Web Application Manifest and Publication Manifest. Useful examples of explicit companion-manifest discovery on the web. The former is battle-tested; the latter is narrower and less central to ordinary web deployment. citeturn38view1turn18search0

Academic research. Prompt-RAG. A useful structural-retrieval precedent, especially for the argument that not all retrieval needs embeddings and chunk stores. citeturn27view0

RAPTOR. Important hierarchical summarization/tree retrieval prior art. It strengthens the case that document trees are useful for agent reasoning, but it does not define a public artifact primitive. citeturn27view2

Signifiers as a First-class Abstraction in Hypermedia Multi-Agent Systems. Valuable for the affordance/signifier frame and for grounding the essay argument about machine-perceivable cues, although its focus is action, not reading. citeturn8search7turn8search11

Repositories, working-group materials, and community specs. Model Context Protocol Resources spec. The key source for MCP’s current resource model, templates, metadata, and subscriptions. citeturn20view3turn25view4

Skills Over MCP charter and SEP-2640 draft. Important because they show the ecosystem explicitly treating skills as content transported over MCP Resources rather than as a new primitive inside MCP itself. citeturn20view5turn20view4

Agent Skills specification and docs. The clearest current open documentation of progressive disclosure in agent-facing content packaging. citeturn23search0turn23search1turn22view1

llms.txt proposal. Useful as a nearby but narrower design: human-readable summaries plus links in a predictable location. It is best treated as adjacent, not as the answer. citeturn37view0turn6search1

Vendor and product docs. Anthropic Agent Skills docs. Primary evidence for on-demand skill loading and supporting-file hydration. citeturn22view1turn27view4

OpenAI Codex Skills docs. Primary evidence that progressive disclosure is now documented outside Anthropic as well. citeturn27view3

Chrome/WebMCP docs and spec. Important to separate browser-exposed action affordances from reading/navigation affordances. citeturn27view5turn9search8

Commentary and technical analysis. Fastly and Cloudflare documentation on Accept/Vary. Not standards, but highly relevant operational evidence that negotiation-heavy designs impose real caching costs. citeturn11search1turn10search9

OtterlyAI and SE Ranking llms.txt studies. Not primary standards work, but currently some of the only concrete adoption and log-level evidence available. Useful if handled carefully and narrowly. citeturn37view1turn37view2

Search Engine Land reporting on Google and llms.txt. Reputable technical reporting, but still secondary. Use with attribution rather than as if it were a normative Google spec. citeturn37view3

Provenance: Claude (Sonnet) research document, 2025

Transclusion, Manifests, and the Selective-Hydration Document: A Research Brief

TL;DR

  • Ted Nelson's transclusion is the right conceptual ancestor for what you are building, but it is the wrong word to use with an LLM-engineering audience. The artifact you are designing — a single HTML file carrying a machine-readable manifest of addressable, summarized, hash-stamped sections that an agent can selectively hydrate — is operationally closer to Anthropic's "progressive disclosure" for Agent Skills, Vectify AI's "PageIndex" tree-search retrieval, and the broader "context as virtual memory" pattern (MemGPT/Letta) than it is to Xanadu's link-and-quote literary system.
  • The fine-grained addressing piece is essentially solved at the syntactic level (block UIDs in Roam/Logseq/Notion/Athens, ^block-id in Obsidian, #+INCLUDE in Org-mode, $transclude in TiddlyWiki, #:~:text= Text Fragments in browsers); the manifest-with-cost-and-dependency-edges piece is not. Existing systems either ship address schemes without summaries, token costs, or dependency edges, or ship summaries/skills metadata without stable per-section content hashes and explicit DAG edges between sections.
  • The genuinely unbuilt piece is the combined artifact: a self-describing, single-file HTML document whose manifest gives an agent enough information (id, summary, token cost, content hash, dependency edges) to plan its hydration before paying to read any section. Prompt-RAG (Kang, Kim, Yun, Kim, arXiv:2401.11246) and PageIndex (Vectify AI, 2025) come closest, but neither ships content hashes or dependency edges, and both are server-mediated rather than artifact-mediated.

Key Findings

  1. Nelson's transclusion specifies live-by-reference inclusion with bidirectional traceability, addressed by "tumblers" — transfinite-number addresses of the form 0.zzz.yyy.xxx worked out by Mark S. Miller and Roger Gregory in Swarthmore in summer 1979. The defining property, per Nelson, is that "a link is a connection between things which are different, and a transclusion is a connection between things which are the same" (xanadu.com.au).
  2. HTML never adopted true transclusion. <object> and <iframe> give whole-document embedding only; #anchor addresses only what an author already declared with an id= attribute; the W3C XPointer Framework (Recommendation, 25 March 2003; editors Paul Grosso, Eve Maler, Jonathan Marsh, Norman Walsh) was blocked by a Sun Microsystems patent (held until 2016) and an XML-only design that browsers wouldn't ship. The closest modern fragment-level standard, Text Fragments (#:~:text=, WICG, shipped in Chrome M80 in February 2020; Safari in 2022), is read-only and lossy.
  3. Block-reference systems converged on a consistent pattern: every block gets a stable UID; a reference embeds the UID; rendering hydrates the referenced block live (Roam, Logseq, Athens) or copies at render time (Obsidian core, TiddlyWiki, Org-mode #+INCLUDE). Notion uses UUID v4 per block at the API level. None ship per-block token-cost estimates, content hashes, or explicit dependency edges.
  4. **The LLM-agent ecosystem has independently re-invented progressive disclosure but at the skill layer, not the document layer.** Anthropic's Agent Skills (launched 16 October 2025) implement a three-level disclosure model — frontmatter → SKILL.md body → bundled files — that maps almost exactly onto your manifest-then-section design, but for behavioral instructions, not document content.
  5. Document-level "navigate a TOC before fetching" exists in two places worth knowing about: Prompt-RAG (Kang et al., arXiv:2401.11246) and Vectify AI's PageIndex (open-source, ~29k GitHub stars as of late March 2026 per AlphaSignal). Both have the LLM read a table-of-contents tree and then fetch sections. Neither ships content hashes or dependency edges.
  6. MemGPT/Letta gives you the virtual-memory framing but operates on opaque chat history and a vector-backed archive, not on a manifest of named sections with summaries and dependency edges.

Details

1. Project Xanadu, transclusion, and tumblers

Nelson coined "transclusion" in Literary Machines (1981); he claims the idea is present in his 1965 ACM paper on "zippered lists." His own canonical definition, quoted by Wikipedia, is "the same content knowably in more than one place," explicitly distinguishing transclusion from "inclusion" (which loses identity), "transdelivery" (delivery from another location), and "transquotation" (an explicit quotation that remains linked to its origin).

Addressing scheme — tumblers. In summer 1979 in Swarthmore, PA, Mark S. Miller and Roger Gregory worked out an address system for arbitrary substrings of arbitrary documents in arbitrary servers, based on transfinite numbers they called tumblers. Per the xanadu.com tech document: "This address space is measured in tumblers, which are multipart numbers of the form 0.zzz.yyy.xxx..., where the fields zzz, etc., are any integer." The first field encodes a node, the second a document, deeper fields encode position and span. Critically, tumblers permit span arithmetic — addition and subtraction of address ranges — which is what lets Xanadu compute, for example, "which existing transclusions overlap this region I'm about to edit." This is structurally similar to the "enfilade" data structures (Granfilade, Poomfilade, Spanfilade) that Nelson and Jonathan V. E. Ridgway developed beginning in 1971–72.

Transclusion vs. inclusion vs. embedding. Nelson is emphatic: embedding (HTML's <img>, <iframe>, <object>) breaks identity — the included content has no canonical relationship back to the original after the embed completes. Transclusion preserves a bidirectional, content-addressable link such that you can ask, of any byte in any rendered document, "where else does this appear?" This is the property HTML never delivered.

Original vision for fine-grained addressing. Every byte of every document is independently addressable. Quotation is structural, not textual — when you "quote" something in Xanadu, you transclude its tumbler range; the renderer fetches the bytes from the source. This solves Nelson's "frictionless non-negotiated quotation" copyright problem (the reader pays the source author when reading), the "transcription error" problem (you cannot mis-copy because you are not copying), and the "broken link" problem (links are by stable address, not by URL).

2. Why HTML never got transclusion

<iframe> and <object>. Both embed whole documents. <iframe> introduces a nested browsing context with its own DOM, history, and security boundary; the seamless attribute that briefly promised inline integration was made obsolete in HTML5 (W3C HTML 5.2 working draft, 2016). <object> similarly expects "a whole file." There is no standard HTML element that says "fetch this URL, parse the response, extract this fragment, and splice it into my DOM as if I had written it locally." A 2011 W3C IRC log (Richard Ishida, public-xml-processing-model-wg) captures the state of the art: an editor asks why HTML still cannot do this, and the responses converge on "you want transclusion! Ted Nelson would be proud!" followed by twenty years of "the use case for client-side transclusion isn't clear" and "server-side includes handle it."

Fragment identifiers. HTML's #anchor system only addresses what an author already declared with an id= attribute. There is no way to address "the third paragraph after the heading 'Methods'" or "characters 4096–8192 of this document." This is exactly the deficiency Doug Engelbart's purple numbers and Eugene Eric Kim's Purple tool (~2001) attempted to retrofit: every paragraph gets a visible, persistent NID anchor that you can link to. Matthew Schneider's PurpleSlurple did it as a transcoding proxy. None of these caught on at web scale; IDs were not stable across edits, and authors did not want to author them by hand. Tim Bray (ongoing, 2004) experimented with them on his blog and turned them off because they shifted on every paragraph insertion.

XPointer and the patent that killed it. The W3C XPointer Framework reached Recommendation on 25 March 2003 (editors Paul Grosso, Eve Maler, Jonathan Marsh, Norman Walsh). It was designed precisely to give the web Nelson-grade fragment addressing — point to elements, attributes, character ranges, even XPath-style structural expressions. Browsers never implemented it. Per XML.com (Edd Dumbill, January 2001): "XPointer is affected by a technology patent held by Sun Microsystems. The legal terms and conditions offered by Sun to XPointer implementors can be found in the archives of the public comments list." The underlying Sun/Jakob Nielsen patent on "hypertext scroll attributes" (1997) was not released royalty-free until 2016 — too late. XPointer was also XML-only, while the web converged on HTML5; the full xpointer() scheme remained a Working Draft and never reached Recommendation.

Text Fragments (#:~:text=). The most recent and most successful fragment-addressing scheme: WICG's Scroll-To-Text-Fragment, shipped in Chrome M80 (February 2020), Safari (2022), and supported by Mozilla in a "positive" standards position. Syntax: https://example.com/page#:~:text=prefix-,start,end,-suffix. The browser scrolls to and highlights the first text match. The mechanism is a "fragment directive" using :~: as a delimiter so legacy fragment-based routing doesn't break (editors Nick Burris and David Bokan, Google). How far does it go? Read-only navigation and highlight. No DOM-level addressability for scripts beyond document.fragmentDirective. No transclusion — you cannot use it to embed someone else's paragraph in your page. It is, effectively, "Ctrl+F as a URL." The W3C Media Fragments URI 1.0 Recommendation (25 September 2012) added a similar capability for time-ranges in audio/video, but the spatial and track dimensions saw very limited adoption.

3. Block-reference systems in the wild

System Address scheme Scope Live or copy-at-render
Roam Research 9-character block UID, e.g. ((GGv3cyL6Y)) Single block (one bullet/paragraph) Live — edits to source propagate; {{embed: ((uid))}} makes the embed itself editable
Obsidian ^block-id (author-defined or auto-generated); also #heading for section transclusion via ![[note#^block-id]] Block (single paragraph, list item, callout); also heading sections Copy-at-render in core; the Mirror plugin (jdsimcoe/obsidian-mirror) makes the embed editable in place
Notion UUID v4 per block via API Any block type (paragraph, heading, child page, callout, synced block, etc.) Synced Blocks are live; ordinary blocks rendered via link_to_page are reference-only
Logseq ((uuid)) for block-ref; {{embed ((uuid))}} for block-embed; {{embed [[Page]]}} for page-embed Block including its children for embed; single block for ref Live — both refs and embeds update with source
Org-mode #+INCLUDE: "file.org::*Headline", ::#custom-id, ::ID-uuid, or :lines "5-10" Headline/subtree, named block, custom-ID, ID-property, or line range Resolved at export/render time (one-shot copy)
TiddlyWiki <$transclude $tiddler="Name" $field="text"/> (modern) or {{Title!!field}} (legacy); slots/fills for parameterized regions Whole tiddler, a single field, or named slot regions Live — re-evaluated every render
Athens Research ((block-uid)) (Roam-compatible) Block Live, with bidirectional Linked References
MediaWiki {{PageName}} with noinclude/onlyinclude/includeonly markup Whole page or marked sections Live — propagates on edit

Pattern. The convergent design across all these systems is: (a) every block gets a stable identifier at authoring time, often a UUID or short opaque UID; (b) references embed the identifier; (c) the renderer resolves at display time. What none of them ship: per-block summaries, per-block token-cost estimates, content hashes, or explicit dependency edges. Block-reference systems treat blocks as flat addressable atoms; they do not treat them as nodes in a DAG with semantic relationships beyond parent/child and "is referenced by." This is the gap your design targets.

4. LLM-agent systems and addressable fragments

OpenAI File Search / Assistants / Responses API. Vector stores; OpenAI auto-chunks, auto-embeds, runs hybrid keyword+vector retrieval, returns annotations. Critically, in the migration from Assistants to Responses API, chunk-level traceability regressed: a specific OpenAI Developer Community thread, "How to handle file citations with the new Responses API?" (thread #1146454), documents that after migration, file citations return only a file ID and filename rather than chunk-level text spans, and an OpenAI engineer (Steve Coffey) confirmed that as the intended design. This is the opposite direction from what your architecture wants: it is "stuff relevant text into context," not "give the agent an addressable map."

Gemini File Search Tool supports media_id annotations that persist across calls and let you download the exact chunk referenced — better than OpenAI on this axis, but still chunk-level, not author-defined-section-level.

LlamaIndex. Has HierarchicalNodeParser, IndexNode, AutoMergingRetriever. The pattern: parse documents into a node tree (root nodes large, leaf nodes small); embed leaves; on retrieval, "walk" the hierarchy and merge leaves up to parents when many leaves under one parent score highly. This is adjacent to your design — it has hierarchical structure and addressable nodes — but the navigation is driven by embedding similarity, not by an agent reading a manifest of summaries.

LangChain. Document loaders chunk and embed. No first-class concept of "give the agent a TOC and let it choose."

Anthropic Agent Skills (16 October 2025). This is the closest production analog to your design. The launch post — "Equipping agents for the real world with Agent Skills," by Barry Zhang, Keith Lazuka, and Mahesh Murag on the Anthropic Engineering blog — describes a three-level model: "Progressive disclosure is the core design principle that makes Agent Skills flexible and scalable. Like a well-organized manual that starts with a table of contents, then specific chapters, and finally a detailed appendix, skills let Claude load information only as needed." The levels: (1) YAML frontmatter (name + description) always in the system prompt — "metadata is the first level of progressive disclosure: it provides just enough information for Claude to know when each skill should be used without loading all of it into context"; (2) SKILL.md body, loaded when Claude decides the skill is relevant; (3) bundled files referenced by name from SKILL.md, loaded only when the running task references them. Anthropic published the spec as an open standard on 18 December 2025. The gap relative to your design: Skills are oriented at behavioral capability (how to fill a PDF form, how to render a slide deck), not at document content (what does this paper say in section 3.2). And they have no notion of dependency edges between sub-files — they're a tree, not a DAG.

Claude Code's MCP fetch server and Zed's configurable fetch tool support max_length, start_index, and "raw vs. summarized" parameters for agent-controlled retrieval — but this is byte-range pagination, not section-aware addressing.

MemGPT / Letta. From "MemGPT: Towards LLMs as Operating Systems" (Charles Packer, Sarah Wooders, Kevin Lin, Vivian Fang, Shishir G. Patil, Ion Stoica, Joseph E. Gonzalez; arXiv:2310.08560, October 2023, revised February 2024): virtual context management with main context (RAM), recall storage (warm), archival storage (cold), and function-call-driven paging between tiers. This is the right framing for your problem — virtual memory for context windows — but MemGPT operates on opaque chat history and a vector-search-backed archive, not on a manifest of named sections with summaries and dependency edges.

Prompt-RAG (Bongsu Kang, Jundong Kim, Tae-Rim Yun, Chang-Eop Kim; arXiv:2401.11246, 20 January 2024). This is the canonical academic precedent for your design. Three stages: (1) extract or author a Table of Contents from the source; (2) prompt the LLM with query + ToC, ask it to return the headings most relevant to the query; (3) concatenate the text under those headings as the "reference text" for a second generation prompt. From the abstract: "We propose a natural language prompt-based retrieval augmented generation (Prompt-RAG), a novel approach to enhance the performance of generative large language models (LLMs) in niche domains. Conventional RAG methods mostly require vector embeddings, yet the suitability of generic LLM-based embedding representations for specialized domains remains uncertain." Figure 1 caption: "the LLM directly accesses the table of contents for creating a contextual reference, followed by generating a response with it." The paper applies this to Korean medicine (where domain-specific embeddings are scarce) and reports it outperforming vector-RAG baselines. What it lacks for your purposes: no token-cost annotations per section, no content hashes, no dependency edges, no single-file artifact form factor. The ToC is server-side; the agent's call is to a server.

PageIndex (Vectify AI; Mingtian Zhang, Yu Tang and the PageIndex Team, 2025). Open-source "vectorless, reasoning-based RAG" — AlphaSignal reported approximately 29k GitHub stars in late March 2026. From the project README: "Inspired by AlphaGo, we propose PageIndex — a vectorless, reasoning-based RAG system that builds a hierarchical tree index from long documents and uses LLMs to reason over that index for agentic, context-aware retrieval. … PageIndex performs retrieval in two steps: Generate a 'Table-of-Contents' tree structure index of documents [and] Perform reasoning-based retrieval through tree search." Reports 98.7% accuracy on FinanceBench (self-reported, not peer-reviewed). Same gap as Prompt-RAG: no per-section content hashes or explicit dependency DAG, server-mediated.

RAPTOR (Parth Sarthi, Salman Abdullah, Aditi Tuli, Shubh Khanna, Anna Goldie, Christopher D. Manning; ICLR 2024, arXiv:2401.18059, 31 January 2024). Recursive Abstractive Processing for Tree-Organized Retrieval. From the abstract: "We introduce the novel approach of recursively embedding, clustering, and summarizing chunks of text, constructing a tree with differing levels of summarization from the bottom up. At inference time, our RAPTOR model retrieves from this tree, integrating information across lengthy documents at different levels of abstraction. … by coupling RAPTOR retrieval with the use of GPT-4, we can improve the best performance on the QuALITY benchmark by 20% in absolute accuracy." Important nuance: retrieval is still embedding-driven, not LLM-reasoning-driven over a manifest. RAPTOR is the canonical academic precedent for hierarchical-summary-first retrieval, but not for manifest-driven agentic selection.

llms.txt (industry, not academic, 2024). A robots.txt-style file at the root of a website giving LLM agents a structured table of contents with links to Markdown versions of pages. From llmtext.com: "llms.txt is a standardized file format (similar to robots.txt) that helps AI agents and LLMs efficiently navigate and understand your website's content." This is the site-level analog of what you are building at the document level. No per-section token cost or content hash and one level coarser than your target.

The thing nobody seems to have published in peer review: an artifact-format that combines (a) addressable sections, (b) per-section author-written summaries, (c) per-section token-cost estimates, (d) per-section content hashes for cache validity, and (e) explicit dependency edges between sections. The pieces all exist; the integration does not.

Synthesis: transclusion as primitive, selective hydration as practice

Nelson's contribution is not a file format but a conceptual primitive: the move from "content is copied where it is used" to "content is referenced where it is used, and the reference is resolvable to identity." That primitive is what makes your manifest design sensible at all. Without it, the natural shape of LLM context-loading is what we have today: chunk, embed, retrieve top-k, paste into prompt. That is inclusion by similarity, and it has well-documented failure modes (semantic drift, context poisoning, citation-provenance loss — see Elastic, Weaviate, and the OpenAI Developer Community thread #1146454 on Responses API citation regressions).

Your design — single HTML artifact + manifest + addressable sections + per-section summary/cost/hash/dependencies — is best understood as applying Nelson's primitive at the granularity of LLM context. The artifact is a self-contained docuverse; the manifest is the address table; the agent is the user who reads the address table first and pulls in only the spans it needs. Where Nelson assumed a global server-mediated address space (xu88/xu92), you are proposing a local, single-file address space — which is actually a much simpler engineering problem, and one that solves real problems (offline operation, deterministic replay, cache validity via content hashes) that the global Xanadu approach never figured out.

The closest production analog is Anthropic's Agent Skills: the three-level progressive disclosure model (frontmatter → SKILL.md → bundled files) is structurally isomorphic to your (manifest → section summary → section body). Where your design extends Skills:

  1. Skills is a tree; you want a DAG. Sections in a real document have dependency relationships that aren't strictly hierarchical — "section 4 builds on the result from section 2.3" is a cross-edge, not a parent-child edge. Your dependency-edges field captures this; Skills can't.
  2. Skills has no token cost. Claude has to guess whether reading a file is worth it. Your manifest gives the agent the actual cost in tokens before it pays, which lets the agent plan a budget.
  3. Skills has no content hash. Cache invalidation is implicit. Your hashes give the agent a way to verify that a previously read section hasn't changed and a way to detect divergent versions of the same artifact.
  4. Skills is filesystem-backed. Your design is single-file HTML — portable, archivable, signable, embeddable in an email or a static-site CDN. This is a real difference: a single self-describing artifact has fundamentally different operational properties than a directory tree on a VM.

The closest academic analog is Prompt-RAG (Kang, Kim, Yun, Kim, 2024), which validates the core hypothesis that LLMs are competent at reading a TOC and picking sections — but Prompt-RAG is a server-side retrieval pipeline, not a portable artifact, and it lacks cost/hash/dependency metadata.

The unbuilt piece, then, is precisely the artifact: a single HTML file that an agent can fetch(), parse for its manifest, plan against (using token costs and dependency edges), and then fetch_section(id) against — possibly via Text Fragments URLs, possibly via inline script that exposes a JS API, possibly via HTTP range requests if served. The conceptual ancestor is Xanadu; the operational ancestor is Agent Skills; the academic ancestor is Prompt-RAG / PageIndex / RAPTOR. None of them is the same thing, and the gap is large enough to be worth building.

A useful framing for the design discussion: you are building a document that knows how to be partially read. That is the one-sentence pitch, and it is more accurate than "a transclusion-enabled document" because nobody is transcluding into it — the agent is reading out of it.

Frank vocabulary assessment

Should you call it "transclusion"? No — at least not in your primary framing.

Why "transclusion" will hurt you with an LLM-engineering audience:

  1. Recognition asymmetry. AI engineers who know the term know it from Roam/Obsidian/Notion, where it means "live-embed this block into my note." Your system does the inverse: it's a source of selectively-read sections, not a destination of embedded content. Using the same word invites the wrong mental model.
  2. It's a tribal-knowledge term. Outside knowledge-management circles and the Xanadu diaspora, "transclusion" is unfamiliar. In a pitch to an AI infra team, it functions as a shibboleth, not a clarifier. Andy Matuschak's notes on networked note-writing software and the long Obsidian-forum threads on whether "transclusion" even exists in Obsidian (forum.obsidian.md/t/transclusion-in-obsidian/69551) are evidence of how contested the term is even among practitioners.
  3. Nelson's own usage is contested. He distinguishes transclusion from transdelivery and transquotation; popular usage conflates all three; Mark Baker (everypageispageone.com) argues that the term has become essentially undefinable in practice. Importing this baggage into an AI-engineering doc is a tax.

What the audience will recognize:

  • "Progressive disclosure" — Anthropic's term, well-known after October 2025, maps directly onto your three-level (manifest → summary → content) flow.
  • "Selective context hydration" or "selective context loading" — internally consistent with the "context engineering" vocabulary that Anthropic, Weaviate, and Elastic have standardized on.
  • "Demand-paged context" / "virtual context" — MemGPT/Letta vocabulary; resonates with anyone who knows OS-level virtual memory.
  • "Fragment-addressed retrieval" or "section-addressed retrieval" — descriptive, neutral, web-native.
  • "Lazy loading" — universally understood, but understates the design (you have richer metadata than just "fetch when needed").

Recommendation, in order of preference for your audience:

  1. Primary framing: "a self-hydrating document with a section manifest" or "a manifest-addressed document for agentic reading." This is descriptive, gives the right intuition, and doesn't require any prior vocabulary.
  2. Secondary framing (for the technical mechanism): "progressive disclosure at the document level" — explicit nod to Anthropic Agent Skills, which is what your audience will pattern-match to. This is your best one-line elevator pitch in an AI-engineering setting.
  3. Tertiary framing (for the systems analogy): "demand-paged context" — for anyone who has touched MemGPT/Letta or who appreciates the virtual-memory metaphor.
  4. Use "transclusion" once, in the intellectual-history section — to credit Nelson, to distinguish your design from block-embedding in Roam/Obsidian, and to make clear you are aware of the lineage. Then drop it.

Use "transclusion" the way a 2020 paper would use "von Neumann architecture" — as background, not as branding. The word will signal historical literacy if used sparingly and will signal vagueness if used as the headline.

Recommendations

Stage 1 — Within the next two weeks: nail the manifest schema. This is your competitive surface. Specifically:

  • id: opaque string, stable across edits (UUID v4 or content-hash-prefixed). Do not use heading text — Roam's lesson from 9-character opaque UIDs is that human-readable IDs break.
  • summary: human-authored, ≤ 1 sentence, ≤ 40 tokens. Author-written summaries are what Anthropic's Agent Skills got right; LLM-generated summaries (Notion-style) drift.
  • tokens: integer estimate using tiktoken or an equivalent tokenizer for a target model family. Document which tokenizer.
  • hash: SHA-256 of canonicalized section content (strip whitespace, normalize Unicode). This is the piece nobody else ships.
  • deps: array of section IDs this section semantically depends on. Be strict about what "depends on" means — recommend "the meaning of this section is incomplete without that one."

Stage 2 — Once schema is stable: ship a reference reader. A 200-line JavaScript client that, given the artifact URL, parses the manifest, exposes fetch_section(id), and emits a Claude/OpenAI-compatible tool. This is what turns the artifact from a spec into a usable agent capability. Anthropic Skills shipped with bundled scripts for exactly this reason.

Stage 3 — Validate against Prompt-RAG and PageIndex. Run the same query against (a) your manifest-driven agent reading your artifact, (b) Prompt-RAG over the same source content, (c) vector-RAG with hierarchical chunking via LlamaIndex AutoMergingRetriever. Report token cost, latency, and answer quality. Benchmark target: match Prompt-RAG quality at lower token cost, or exceed it on dependency-aware queries (questions whose answer requires sections that don't lexically overlap with the question).

Benchmarks that should change the strategy:

  • If an agent given your manifest performs worse than vector-RAG on simple lookup queries → your summaries are bad; rewrite them or generate them with an LLM and audit.
  • If an agent never uses the dependency edges → they aren't earning their schema weight; consider dropping them and shipping a simpler manifest.
  • If content-hash cache hits are rare in your target workflow → you don't need hashes; drop them and ship a smaller manifest.
  • If Anthropic ships document-level (not skill-level) progressive disclosure as a first-class API before you ship → reframe immediately as "Skills for documents, with hashes and DAG edges" and concede the broader pattern.

Vocabulary discipline:

  • Headline term: "self-hydrating document" or "manifest-addressed document."
  • Secondary: "progressive disclosure" (credit Anthropic), "demand-paged context" (credit MemGPT).
  • Background only: "transclusion" (credit Nelson, distinguish from Roam/Obsidian usage).
  • Avoid: "block reference" (means the wrong thing to KM users), "lazy loading" (understates), "RAG" (means the wrong architecture).

Caveats

  1. "PageIndex" claims are self-reported. Vectify AI's 98.7% FinanceBench accuracy has not been peer-reviewed. The star count (~29k as of late March 2026, per AlphaSignal; Towards AI independently reported ~23k around the same time) is real, but treat the accuracy figure as marketing until reproduced.
  2. Prompt-RAG is an arXiv preprint. Kang et al. (2401.11246) has not, as far as I could confirm, been published in a peer-reviewed journal. The technique is sound; the evidence base is one preprint.
  3. "Transclusion" usage varies even among practitioners. The Obsidian community, the Roam community, and the Xanadu community use the word with different intensities. My recommendation to demote the term in your framing is partly a calculation about your AI-engineering audience; if you are writing for a knowledge-management audience, the calculus reverses.
  4. The Text Fragments spec is a Draft Community Group Report, not a W3C Recommendation. It is shipped in Chrome and Safari; Firefox's standards position is "positive" but implementation has lagged. Don't depend on #:~:text= as a section-addressing mechanism for your artifact — it's a hint, not a load-bearing protocol.
  5. Section dependency edges are the least-validated piece of your design. No major system ships them. That could mean "untapped greenfield" or "everyone tried and dropped it." Plan to A/B test whether the edges actually change agent behavior; if they don't, cut them.
  6. One arXiv source flagged during research (a "ContextWeaver" paper at arXiv:2604.23069) has a future-dated identifier and is excluded from the report pending verification.
  7. Notion API and OpenAI Responses API are moving targets. As of May 2026, the Responses API returns file-level citations only; this regressed from the Assistants API (OpenAI Developer Community thread #1146454, confirmed by Steve Coffey). Anthropic's Agent Skills became an open standard on 18 December 2025. Re-check both before publishing the artifact spec.
